#2951. Sam Darnold’s cratering play is a big picture problem for the Seahawks

Seattle Seahawks quarterback Sam Darnold is a Super Bowl champion who played an important part in bringing another Lombardi Trophy to Seattle, and that can never be taken away.

Sam Darnold is also playing bad football.

You can blame the offensive coordinator, you can blame the offensive line, you can blame the lack of a running game, but Sam Darnold is playing bottom-tier football. His worst throw from Monday night's blowout loss against the Chicago Bears didn't even count, but it's emblematic of his incomprehensible decision-making.

Incidentally, the play design is pretty much DOA but there is no need to turn 2nd and 1 into a double coverage gamble.

Darnold's touchdown-to-interception ratio is barely 1:1 over his last 16 regular season starts. His mostly great playoff run is the break-up in what is otherwise a prolonged stretch of play that is far removed from much of his 2024 Minnesota Vikings season and the first 10-ish games of his Seahawks career.

(Editor's note: It's 344/551 and not 334)

There are no advanced stats that can positively spin Darnold as an above-average starter at the moment. He really wasn't playing particularly well for most of the back-end of 2025, but the running game finally sprung to life and Seattle's defense and special teams repeatedly either scored or gave the Seahawks extremely short fields.

The 2026 Seahawks are 4-4, which is not the end of the world, but how they've gotten to 4-4 is pretty much the worst case scenario in the short- and long-term. If Mike Macdonald's defense hypothetically had curiously dropped off to be a middling group and the Seahawks were losing shootouts while Darnold was putting up impressive numbers, I think we could live with that and acknowledge that defenses can be more volatile year-to-year than offenses. Darnold is heavily culpable for at least three of the four defeats, and even his best game against the Washington Commanders is significantly impacted by what was effectively a game-losing pick-six.

Darnold's contract is a great mid-level contract for someone with his track record and roughly two seasons worth of good productivity. It's also a contract that expires in 2027, and the current version of Darnold does not look like someone who should be Seattle's Week 1 starter, much less the starter in 2028.

Barring substantial improvement over the second half of the season, John Schneider has a major decision to make on Darnold, regardless of whether or not Brian Fleury is still the offensive coordinator. We've seen Darnold be very good when paired with the right play-caller, and I am not going to put the failures of this offense exclusively at his feet. But Darnold is in that tier of quarterback who needs way more help to be functional than the likes of Josh Allen or Joe Burrow or Patrick Mahomes. Every quarterback needs at least some semblance of foundational support to succeed, but Sam needs way more stuff than, say, prime Russell Wilson. That includes, seemingly above all else, having an experienced and effective play-caller who can play to his strengths and minimize his worst habits. Brian Fleury does not seem to fit the bill.

Darnold also needs play-action to work like we need air to breathe. When it's not working, Darnold has consistently been anywhere from average to ruinous in pure dropback situations. He needs an offensive line to protect him so well that his pressure rate is low, otherwise this is what you get.

Darnold with a clean pocket and/or the ability to get the ball out of his hands quickly can sling it with the best of them. When he is dealing with pressure in his face, he is barely a starting caliber quarterback. We have two years of evidence in a Seahawks uniform.

Play-action to be so insanely efficient that it overrides the lack of non-PA success.

Favorable game scripts such that his team is almost never trailing.

Related: A top-5 caliber defense such that his team is rarely trailing.

An offensive coordinator/play-caller who can scheme up easy-button throws for fun.

An offensive line that can pass protect competently.

An explosive running game in case none of the above works out or is figured out by NFL defenses.

There's nothing wrong with building a great environment around a competent but limited quarterback, but that also means a crevice turns into the Grand Canyon when said environment is even marginally worse. You can live with Darnold's turnovers if he's as efficient as he was in 2024 and 2025. That is not even close to what we're seeing in 2026.

The possibility also cannot be discounted that two years of film effectively exhausted all of the routes that the likes of Kevin O'Connell and Klint Kubiak could make Darnold thrive. As I wrote not too long ago, "With non-elite quarterbacks there are only so many levers you can pull before competent defenses respond." If Seattle sticks with Sam then it will likely need an(other) offensive coordinator to get him back on track, all while not being an immediate head coach candidate. This is the downside of having a defensive minded coach and your OC hires are either bad (see: Grubb, Ryan) or are so good that NFL teams take notice (see: Kubiak, Klint). The churn truly never stops unless you find that unicorn who can be a stable part of your organization while being no threat to hold a head coaching job.

Seattle's roster as a whole is a very good one; most of the players from last year's Super Bowl squad are still here. Any premise of Seattle having a sustained window of playoff and Super Bowl contention was contingent upon Darnold sustaining his success and mercifully giving us all a break from debating Seattle's next franchise quarterback. If Darnold cannot recapture even half of what made him go from draft bust to unlikely Super Bowl champion—this includes the rest of this year and (if applicable) his contract year in 2027—the Seahawks' long-term vision is suddenly a lot more complicated. Drew Lock is a free agent and frankly I don't want to bank on the QB revival thing yet again for a career backup entering Year 9. It's not like Drew himself hasn't had a history of being turnover prone with volatile week-to-week performances when he was a starter elsewhere. Jalen Milroe is a great unknown outside of preseason, and 2027 is a pivotal year for him to see if he's even ready to be an NFL starter. Other than that? Seattle might find itself once again dealing with major uncertainty at the most important position in the sport.

That's what stings the most, even coming off an unforgettable Super Bowl season. The Seahawks have their head coach, have a lot of hyper-talented, young core pieces under contract either on rookie deals or massive extensions, but quarterback has become a giant question mark now and therefore makes for a much murkier future than hoped.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2950. Hawks edge narrow victory against six-man Pacers

The Atlanta Hawks continued their mini road-trip on Monday night, picking up a 108-104 victory against the Indiana Pacers. Nickeil Alexander-Walker scored 34 points, tying a career-best nine three-pointers in the process. Lu Dort added 14 points for Atlanta. For the Pacers, Jay Huff led with 22 points with Quenton Jackson adding 20 points.

The big story of this game could only be the injuries/absences. The following players were either injured or rested last night for the Pacers (who were on the second night of a back-to-back): Tyrese Haliburton, Paskal Siakam, Ivica Zubac, Aaron Nesmith, Andrew Nembhard, Obi Toppin, Kelly Oubre, and TJ McConnell, to name but a few, leaving the Pacers with just six available bodies.

NBA dot com's summary of this game paints quite a picture when it comes to the Pacers absences and who was available:

"The players who sat out have a combined salary of about $193 million, while those on the court make about $15 million."

For the Hawks, Jalen Johnson was sidelined in this game after the ankle tweak in New Orleans, while Jock Landale also missed out. In Johnson's place, Lu Dort would start. Kingston Flemings was also absent due to a one game league suspension following an elbow in New Orleans.

If one is to be realistic about the situation that presented itself for Atlanta last night, it's hard to conceive any situation where the Hawks should have work excessively hard or be in any position where this game is realistic doubt. But that's exactly where the Hawks found themselves when it came to clutch time.

At several points in this game the Hawks established secure double-digit leads — 14 points, 15 points — in the second and third quarters. And in both instances, the Pacers pegged them back. A lack of size inside meant that when the Pacers found Huff, they also found a recipe that worked inside and led to consecutive baskets which wiped the lead in the second quarter for a spell.

In the third quarter, the Hawks shot 28% from the field, and then went on a nearly four minute field goal drought in the fourth quarter down the stretch, with Onyeka Okongwu's hook fortunate to drop to give the Hawks a secure four-point lead with 10 seconds remaining.

With a second left and a good contest, it was pretty desperate stuff for the Hawks but they finally got their much needed field goal, and the Pacers' juice finally ran out.

This Okongwu basket was also a rare example of the Hawks getting a basket in the paint, scoring just 26 points in the paint, only 10 of which came in the second half.

The Hawks found success early shooting the three, and fell flat shooting inside the arc, and decided the strategy was to just hoist threes where possible as players such as Alexander-Walker (nine threes), Corey Kispert (four threes in the first half) and Lu Dort (4-of-6 from three) found fortune outside. In the end, the Hawks shot 23-of-51 from three, and it's the only reason they won this game. Atlanta shot 45% from three and 38% from the field.

Hawks head coach Quin Snyder praised the quality of the win despite acknowledging the Hawks were not at their best at the rim while missing players themselves

"It's a really good win," said Snyder postgame. "We knew we were going to be in a dogfight kind of game. Huff gives him size and rim protection. We weren't very good at the rim, and they made plays. We got a 10/12-point lead at one point, that's when you hopefully have a chance to break the game open and we were never able to do that. At this point in the year, the way every team is kind of growing, hopefully, the Pacers can get healthy. That's really hard. We had a little bit of that tonight with not having Jalen, Jock, and Kingston. We missed a lot of layups, and some of that was because we were playing against a team who had size. Good to get out of here with a win, all wins are good. These are the type of games, close games, that help you get better."

I couldn't disagree more with the general sentiment here that this was a 'good win'. While all wins ultimately add to the Hawks' record, so from that point of view they're 'equal' in that they all add a '+1,' not all victories are equal in how they're made, and not all victories are 'good.'

For example, how would you compare the recent victory against the Cleveland Cavaliers with this victory? Or the absolute drumming of Charlotte earlier in the season? Yes, they all added one win to the Hawks' record, but the perception around them and how the Hawks performed are completely different.

Adding to that, while the loss of Johnson, Flemings, and Landale in this game are note worthy, Snyder has no leg to stand on by mentioning that the Hawks missed these players while Indiana was missing all who they were missing.

With respect to the 'Super 6' as they were dubbed by the Indiana broadcast (and there's lots of good things to say about those six players), the Hawks — even without those three players — have so much more quality on paper of those who were available that this should never have been a close game. Not to mention depth; the Hawks ran out — almost on purpose, it felt — a deeper rotation than normal. Some of that accounts for injuries/absences, so there were minutes for Asa Newell and Zuby Ejiofor in the front-court, but the likes of Kispert and RayJ Dennis also got real rotation minutes.

Just to add further insult to the embarrassment of riches that were in Atlanta's favor, five of those six Pacers players played real rotation minutes against Brooklyn on Sunday, and were tasked with playing — in most cases — 40-plus minutes with little time to rest.

The Pacers deserve a lot of credit for how close they brought this game, because they had absolutely no right to be anywhere close to winning this game. Everything that was stacked against them should have resulted in a double-digit loss at the bare minimum.

But plays from Huff on both ends of the floor, energy from Kobe Brown coming off the bench, the tenacity of Braden Smith (who I thought was excellent) kept the Pacers close enough at times to make a run and keep the Hawks within striking distance of a run, which they made multiple runs to get back into the game.

From the Hawks' perspective, it was clearly not working at the rim so they dialled in from the perimeter. Against New Orleans, the three-point shooting was deplorable, but was excellent last night and was Atlanta's saving grace. So, in that regard, that's a positive the Hawks can build on that they can bounce back from an extremely cold performance to an extremely high volume performance, led by Alexander-Walker's nine threes leading to 34 points on 12-of-25 shooting and 9-of-15 from three:

Postgame, Snyder praised Alexander-Walker's ability to defend while also continuing to hit shots, also crediting the role of CJ McCollum in Alexander-Walker's performance.

"The thing about Nickeil is he's still playing on the defensive end too," said Snyder postgame. "He seems to make plays on both ends of the floor. He's playing with so much confidence. I thought CJ did a really good job of finding him late. I liked the shots that CJ was getting too, you just have certain nights that the ball won't cooperate. Nickeil hit big shots for us, it seemed like, the whole game."

With Johnson and Flemings out — in addition to Alexander-Walker's hot hand — McCollum took more of a playmaking role in this game, dishing out 12 assists in total and the first time McCollum has registered 10 or more assists in a game in over three years.

While McCollum finished 4-of-17 from the field, his contributions in the playmaking department do help make up for the volume of misses.

Elsewhere across the roster, Lu Dort put in a fine performance in the starting lineup, shooting 5-of-10 from the field and 4-of-6 from three. Dort hit what I thought was a huge three in the third quarter to stop the bleeding and prevent Indiana from getting too close:

This was, arguably, one of the most important swings in the game. Had Huff hit that three, the Pacers would have cut the lead down to two points. Dort's three really punished that miss, taking the lead up to eight points and buying the Hawks some immediate relief. If there was a spot when the Hawks sorely needed a good night shooting the three from Dort, this was the one and he delivered.

While everyone will forget the nature of this victory soon enough, the overarching takeaway, again, is that the Hawks were easily beaten inside the paint (conceding 54 points). This is Snyder's biggest concern from this game, spotting the trend that has occurred in several games now (but not before doubling down on his belief that this was a good win).

"Every win is a good one, and people say that because it's true," said Snyder. "Tonight was one of those nights. I thought when they did make the late run, we didn't get rattled. We didn't do everything right. The Pacers made some plays. We had a stretch where we put them on the foul line and that makes it hard because we can't get any force offensively, or thrust. didn't get any offensive thrust. The paint is something that we've got to shore up one way or the other. That's probably the biggest thing for us right now defensively is just not doing a better job protecting the paint because it's going to be real difficult to win when teams score on the paint like these guys did, and some other teams have too."

The Hawks (4-4) are back in action on Wednesday for the first of two meetings against the Washington Wizards (4-3), the first of which wraps up their three game road-trip.

#2942. Sione Vaki emerges as Lions' change-of-pace back to pair with Jahmyr Gibbs

Even though the Lions have only played seven games and most teams have played eight, Lions running back Jahmyr Gibbs is leading the NFL with 191 touches — a huge workload that raises questions about whether Detroit needs a player to share carries with Gibbs, the way David Montgomery had done before he was traded to Houston.

Vaki had a career-high 11 carries and a career-high 65 yards in Sunday's win over the Vikings, and his direct running style was a valuable change of pace for the Lions to have while Gibbs got some breathers between his own 23 carries for 153 yards.

Lions coach Dan Campbell emphasized after the game that the Lions told Vaki they needed him to step up against the Vikings, and Vaki answered the bell.

"Vaki was one of the guys we challenged this week going into this game," Campbell said. "We were going to need him. He needed to show up. He was going to need to get some carries for us, some significant carries. They needed to be real yards, and he was going to need to be able to pass protect for us. And I thought he took a big step today. That's a huge step. He gained a lot of trust today, a ton."

Lions quarterback Jared Goff said the entire offense is excited about what Vaki can bring.

"Vaki ran his tail off," Goff said. "And when he's able to do that, it just gives Gibbs that much more of a breath on the sideline to come back and do his thing at his full capacity. And the O-line loved it. They loved the challenge of, like, we're going to keep leaning on you guys. And I thought they played great."

The Lions think they have the best running back in the league in Gibbs. If Vaki can prove to be one of the better backup running backs in the league, the Lions' running game will be in very good shape.

#2940. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#2936. The Best Team in the NFL is the Chicago Bears

The Chicago Bears are dominating the NFL, and the rest of the league is finally starting to notice.

Maybe it's because so many people hate Caleb Williams and they won't believe it. Maybe it's because the Bears have been so bad for so long, it's hard to believe this is actually them. Or maybe it's that Caleb Williams was hurt and the Bears went 2-2 in the 4 games he missed all or part of the game.

Whatever the reason, the way the Bears have played the last three games leaves you with only one logical conclusion: The Chicago Bears are the best team in the NFL.

The Bears are dominating right now. And they aren't dominating the Tennessee Titans and the Arizona Cardinals. In the last two weeks, the Bears did this against the two teams that met in the Super Bowl just nine months ago.

The way this team is playing, and with the addition of Jeffery Simmons, something has happened with my expectations that I haven't had since I was a child. Never in my adult life have I truly believed the Bears can win the Super Bowl. Maybe that makes me overly critical. Maybe that just makes me a realist, but let me try to explain.

To me, there are two types of good teams: playoff contenders and Super Bowl contenders. The Bears have been playoff contenders on more than one occasion. In 2018, you knew they were headed for the playoffs. During the Lovie years, you knew the Bears were good enough to play additional football in January. But did you ever truly believe they were going to win a Super Bowl? Did you truly believe it? Or did you just hope they could?

In 2006, I never truly believed they could beat the Colts. How was Rex Grossman going to beat Peyton Manning? In 2018, if the Bears did, in fact, take down Sean McVay again and reach the Super Bowl? Did we really think Matt Nagy and Mitch Trubisky could outduel Bill Belichick and Tom Brady?

You could hope that things would align just perfectly and somehow, someway, the Bears could pull out a miracle win and bring home a title, but did you truly believe they were a Super Bowl contender?

The last time I felt this way was in 1987. The Bears still had Jim McMahon. Walter Payton was still on the roster. The '85 defense was still largely intact. This was the last time I felt this way. I was 10. Did I actually know what I was watching? No, of course not. I was a hopeful child who believed my team could win. Why not? They won just two years before; why couldn't they do it again? I didn't understand that Payton was on his last legs. I didn't understand that McMahon had no chance of staying healthy. I didn't understand how much Wilber Marshall would be missed and that other key pieces on the defense were aging. I didn't understand the context. Now I do.

I've never had this belief and this level of confidence in this team in my adult life. The Bears have the coach. They have the quarterback. They have the offensive line. They have the weapons. They have the defense. The Bears have everything they need to win it all.

The team isn't perfect. If they can land Khalil Mack and make this defensive line even stronger, they should. If they can land a CB and beef up their secondary, it's a must. Spend what you have to do to make this happen. Draft picks are for teams that aren't drafting with the last spot in each round, and that's where the Bears are headed if they keep this up.

This Chicago Bears team can win the Super Bowl. That should be the expectation. If the Bears aren't playing a game in Los Angeles on February 14th, I'm going to be disappointed.

That's an awesome feeling to have on November 3rd.

Is there a team you are afraid of in the NFL? The only team that should give you any concern in the NFC is the SF 49ers. In the AFC, it's the Buffalo Bills. Are you afraid of the Kansas City Chiefs? I'm not. Right now, the Bears have a more talented roster. They have the better coach. They have the better quarterback (Yes, I know what I said).

This offense is the best in the NFL. When it's rolling, no defense in the league can stop it. Ask the Seahawks; they stop everyone and still gave up 34 points. This defense, with Jeffery Simmons, is a whole new ballgame. As we talked about last week, Simmons is so good, he makes everyone around him better. We saw that already in his first game. Montez Sweat, Austin Booker, Devin Bush- they all looked fantastic.

The Chicago Bears have arrived. There's no team in the league better than this one. You can have concerns about the secondary; you can have concerns about their depth; you can have concerns. You should have concerns. No team in the league is perfect. But despite any of those concerns, one thing is clear:

The best team in the NFL resides in Chicago.

#2930. Investors are doing just as well this year by shunning AI. How to invest if the tech trade keeps fizzling

A version of the S&P 500 without AI briefly pulled ahead of the traditional index this year and is now neck-and-neck as investors rotate into other areas of the market.

A proprietary Goldman Sachs index tracking the S&P 500 without stocks classified as "AI enablers" was up 12.4% through Oct. 22, edging ahead of the standard S&P 500's 12% gain. The Goldman index is now neck-and-neck with the S&P 500 as tech rallied in Monday trading.

Tech and AI-related companies make up about 45% of the S&P 500. That heavy weighting has helped power the index for years, but it's turned into a liability lately; the S&P 500 tech sector is down about 7% this month while other sectors have held up.

Investors have soured on debt and equity issued by companies like CoreWeave and Oracle. Shares of Meta Platforms sank almost 7% Thursday after it outlined more plans to spend heavily on capex and issue additional shares.

Companies supplying power and equipment for the buildout have been hit hard — names like GE Vernova, Bloom Energy, Vistra and Quanta Services all suffered steep declines during the recent selloff.

Goldman, which launched the measure earlier this year with S&P, uses the S&P 500 as its starting universe and excludes companies perceived to be enablers of AI before applying S&P 500 Index weighting methodology to the remaining universe of companies.

The ex-AI index lagged the S&P 500 for most of the year as investors continued to favor chipmakers, cloud-computing companies and other firms benefiting from heavy AI spending. However, that advantage has been narrowing and it disappeared last week.

Meanwhile, investors are rotating into defensive areas. ETFs like State Street Consumer Staples Select Sector SPDR ETF, State Street Health Select SPDR ETF and State Street Utilities Select Sector SPDR ETF are up 7% to 10% this month. Energy is ahead 10% too, though that's more related to the war in Iran.

Some analysts recommend sticking with non-AI companies for diversification and protection against more pain in tech. The "AI pendulum has swung back in favor of non-AI exposure over the last two weeks," Oppenheimer technical analyst Ari Wald said in a note on Oct. 24.

Two industries that Oppenheimer favors now are commercial services and insurance. The firm upgraded those industries to overweight last week, recommending Republic Services, Cintas, Verisk Analytics, Broadridge Financial Solutions, MetLife and Arthur J. Gallagher.

Berkshire Hathaway is also capitalizing on the rotation out of tech. Its Class B shares are up 8% this month, though it's still lagging behind the S&P 500 this year.

Berkshire owns a wide swath of operating companies, including insurance giant Geico and Burlington Northern Santa Fe railroad. It also has a $364 billion stock portfolio including companies like Apple, Coca-Cola, Alphabet and Bank of America. That diversification may help Berkshire continue to outperform if the tech sector drags down the S&P 500.

Trivector Research sees potential in "non-tech compounders." Healthcare giant Eli Lilly is one idea. The stock is up 22% on the strength of Lilly's weight-loss drugs. The company has more in the pipeline, including a next-generation obesity drug that Lilly plans to file for FDA approval early in 2027.

Viking Holdings, a cruise operator, is also proving durable. The company said on its August earnings call that 40% of its river capacity and 60% of its ocean capacity was already booked for 2027. "Based on recent events, we've seen our guests have proved to be resilient and are continuing to book 2027 and future seasons," the company told investors.

Viking stock is up 15% this year but it may still have upside. The average Wall Street target is $109, above recent prices around $82. Barclays recently upgraded the cruise operator to overweight with a $102 target, citing the company's "compelling risk/reward."

#2928. Steelers Week 8 grades in win over Browns

The Pittsburgh Steelers are coming off their most dominant performance of the season in a 27-6 clobbering of the Cleveland Browns. The win puts Pittsburgh at 6-2 going into the bye and just a half game back of the top seed in the AFC.

As we do every week, Joey Bray and I are here to give you our grades from the Steelers' biggest win of the year.

Jarrett Bailey: Rodgers didn't light up the stat sheet, but he didn't have to when the Steelers ran for 200 yards. He did, however, lead a 44 second touchdown drive at the end of the first half that served as the dagger into the Browns' chances of winning, and he hit Darnell Washington on multiple chunk passes downfield. If DK Metcalf holds onto a ball that hits him in the chest, Rodgers' stat line looks better and doesn't have an interception. Grade: B-

Joey Bray: Aaron Rodgers was just along for the ride in this one, and that's not a bad thing at all. His 13-25, 172 yards, and an interception stat line is easily one of the worst we will see this season from Rodgers. However, you never watched this game and thought Rodgers was playing poorly. With the Steelers up multiple possessions for most of the game, and running the ball as well as they were, it was a low stress game for the veteran quarterback. He stayed clean and moved well, taking only one sack, which matters as the Steelers will undoubtedly have to rely on Rodgers more and more down the stretch. Grade: C

Jarrett Bailey: The Steelers' running back duo had their best game as a tandem, racking up a combined 199 yards and two touchdowns. Jaylen Warren tied his career high of 143 rushing yards, while Rico Dowdle had his best game as a Steeler with 56 yards and the game's first score. This is the Steelers' offensive identity – pound the rock with these two backs and dare you to stop them. Grade: A+

Joey Bray: The Steelers have an identity offensively, and it's running the damn football. Jaylen Warren has the second most scrimmage yards in the NFL through eight weeks, and is the most efficient running back in the NFL on a per-touch basis. Warren's breakout season coupled with Rico Dowdle's emergence over the last few weeks has given the Steelers a true two-headed monster in their backfield. Both backs got in the end zone and controlled the pace of the game as the Steelers cruised to an easy victory. Grade: A+

Jarrett Bailey: This is just unfortunately who Metcalf is. He isn't a No. 1 receiver. One catch for four yards is beyond unacceptable for the money he is making. Grade: F

Joey Bray: It's simply impossible for anyone to defend Metcalf anymore. It's not just that the receiver isn't making an impact, but he's actively costing the team. Rodgers hit Metcalf right in the chest as the receiver bobbled the ball, which eventually led to an interception. With the Steelers up multiple possessions, it didn't end up mattering much in terms of the outcome of the game. However, drops and frustrating plays have been the theme of the Steelers' most expensive offensive player. The team almost certainly needs to add a weapon at the deadline so they don't have to rely on Metcalf down the stretch. Grade: F

Jarrett Bailey: The Steelers led the NFL in EPA per rush in Week 8. They've led the NFL in that stat since Week 3. Aaron Rodgers also faced the third-lowest pressure percentage in Week 8. The offensive line has become one of the game's best. Can't say enough about how tremendous this group is. Grade: A+

Joey Bray: Tell a friend to tell a friend the Steelers have an elite offensive line. It is something to behold seeing just how far this offensive line has come along since they slid rookie Max Iheanachor and veteran Brock Hoffman into starting spots. Pittsburgh ran for 200 yards and only allowed one sack. The offensive line has suddenly become one of the strongest units on the Steelers roster. Grade: A

Jarrett Bailey: If I see another fade to DK Metcalf after Aaron Rodgers clearly checked out of a called run, I'm going to launch a brick through my TV. Run. The. Ball. Pittsburgh still scored three red zone touchdowns, but this should have been a 38-6 win. Grade: C-

Joey Bray: Stop throwing the goal line fades to Metcalf. It was especially curious to me that for as well as the Steelers were running the ball, they refused to run in goal to go situations. Whether it's McCarthys playcalling, or Rodgers choice to throw the fades, they need to have a better collective plan moving forward. It's hard to complain about much considering they won by multiple scores. But they will have to turn those field goals into touchdowns if they want to establish themselves as a serious contender in the AFC. Grade: D

Jarrett Bailey: Watt has officially returned to elite form. He had two sacks against the Browns, giving him 7.5 on the season – he had seven all of last season. He leads the NFL in sacks, and his pressure to sack percentage is fourth in the league (min. 100 pass rush snaps). Grade: A+

Joey Bray: At the midway point of the Steelers season, TJ Watt is on pace for 15 sacks. Watt added two to his total on Sunday as the Pittsburgh pass rush feasted on the Browns' offensive line. The veteran edge rusher is defying all odds by having a career rejuvenation in 2026. Watt has once again become a player who can change the game in an instant. Grade: A+

Jarrett Bailey: Eight sacks is an absurd number. It was great to see Alex Highsmith register five pressures. Highsmith, T.J. Watt, Cam Heyward, Patrick Queen, Kevin Jobity, and Keeanu Benton all registered at least half a sack, and the Steelers had a combined 31 pressures. Just tremendous work. Grade: A+

Joey Bray: Whenever you can sack the opposing quarterback eight times, you have a good chance of winning the game. The Browns were not able to handle the Steelers' pass rush whatsoever on Sunday. The blitzing even got creative, as linebacker Patrick Queen recorded a sack and a half in the contest. This was a display of just how lethal the Steelers' pass rush can be down the stretch for Pittsburgh. Grade: A+

Jarrett Bailey: The Steelers were fifth in the NFL in EPA per play in Week 8, and they didn't allow the Browns to reach the end zone. Overall, they've allowed just one second half touchdown this season, which is an insane stat. This unit has turned into one of the NFL's best. Grade: A+

Joey Bray: You cannot ask for much more than holding your opponent to season lows in points and rushing yards, and that is exactly what the Steelers did on Sunday to Cleveland. The pass rush and linebacker play were immaculate and a large part of why the Steelers dominated this game from start to finish. Patrick Graham deserves all of the credit for the way he has this defensive playing with a secondary that still has some question marks. Grade: A+

Let us know what you think in the comments. Be sure to bookmark Behind the Steel Curtain for all the latest news, breakdowns, and more!

#2926. Luka Dončić's offense is carrying Lakers. Is LA too dependent on its 'showman'?

LOS ANGELES — Luka Dončić earned a standing ovation from the Los Angeles Lakers fans before going on to beat the Miami Heat 138-117 at Crypto.com Arena on Monday, Nov. 2.

Dončić has become the early favorite for regular season MVP as the NBA's leader in scoring average (38.3) and 3-pointers made per game (5.9).

The Slovenian point guard is known for his offensive prowess and his ability to score. It's also what makes the Lakers a threat in the Western Conference.

He finished with 42 points, 13 assists and seven rebounds against the Heat on Nov. 2.

More: Heat defense under scrutiny after Lakers rout

Lakers coach JJ Redick praised Dončić's offensive dominance on several occasions in recent weeks, referring to him as a "showman" and a "supernova" while also comparing his rhythm on the court to a theatrical stage.

"He's just making tough shots; averaging 38 points is not easy in this league," Miami Heat center Bam Adebayo said. "He's making tough shots and you just tip your hat to him."

The two-time scoring champion is averaging 29.3 points per game for his career, which places him third all-time behind Michael Jordan and Wilt Chamberlain. Both players averaged 30.1 ppg for their respective careers.

Dončić has propelled the Lakers to a fast start this season, and his play on Monday was no different. He scored 16 points as Los Angeles led Miami 37-30 in the first quarter.

Dončić is averaging 15 points in the opening quarter and has scored at least 13 in all but one game this season.

When the six-time All-NBA player is hot, so are the Lakers.

The Lakers have outscored opponents by 113 points when Dončić is on the court during the seven games he's played this season, according to StatMuse.

When Dončić isn't on the floor, things can get tricky for Redick.

Los Angeles' bench nearly blew a 16-point lead with 1:43 left in the fourth quarter on Sunday against the Toronto Raptors.

The Raptors cut the Lakers' lead to make it a five-point game. Toronto scored 11 points in 40 seconds and forced Redick to put Dončić and Austin Reaves back into the game and help secure the victory.

On Monday, Dončić and the Lakers produced 111 points, the team's highest-scoring point total through three quarters this season. This time, the Lakers left very little room for mistakes and refused to give up the lead in the final frame.

Dončić's ability to score isn't the only benefit of having him on the court.

Even if Dončić had a low-scoring night, he will always remain a threat that will keep defenses focused on him. It does free up his teammates for opportunities, who can also benefit from his playmaking and his ability to draw a double team.

That was the case Monday, as the Heat applied double-team looks on Dončić.

"I think at the beginning of the season we didn't execute well with the double team, but today I think we improved a lot," Dončić said. "Everybody was in the right spots, so it's not just me.

"It's everybody, just to be in the right spot and being aggressive and shooting with confidence."

Lakers forward Ziaire Williams has been able to benefit from some of the open looks that come with sharing the floor with Dončić.

"Very easy looks," Williams said. "Way easier than they've been in the past. They're sending two (players) to Luka and two at (Reaves) every time."

Reaves has proven he can be a high-efficiency volume scorer, but he is less likely to thrive without Dončić on the court.

If Dončić were unavailable, opposing game plans would focus on Reaves and he would be challenged by teams' toughest defenders.

Beyond Reaves (23.1 ppg), the Lakers would have to rely on center Walker Kessler (14.3) as a third-scoring option.

Williams (11.3) and Collin Sexton (10.3) could also be options, but neither would be able to guarantee similar production to Dončić on a nightly basis.

This article originally appeared on USA TODAY: Luka Dončić's offense is carrying Lakers. Is LA too dependent on its 'showman'?

#2922. Brian Kelly expresses interest in another open coaching position. What coach said:

There's a real chance Florida State football's top two candidates to replace Mike Norvell are currently without coaching jobs.

Former LSU coach Brian Kelly expressed his interest in the Florida State opening on Tuesday, Nov. 3, two days after the school fired Norvell. Kelly joins former Florida State coach Jimbo Fisher, who led the Seminoles to a national championship in 2013, as former college head coaches as potential candidates.

REQUIRED READING: Florida State coaching candidates after Mike Norvell fired: Who replaces him?

"When you're talking to an unemployed football coach, every job's a great job, you know? Kelly said on Sirius XM's "College Sports Radio. "But Florida State, look, I've got a great history with Florida State, great games. I know what that's like, the history and tradition of Florida State, playing in that state, and the fan base.

"So anytime you have an incredibly brand driven, great tradition and history, there's a want and desire to be part of somebody that has had that success and, certainly, leading that program, from anyone's perspective, would be an honor and privilege."

Kelly told USA TODAY Sports in April he wants to be back in coaching. He has also been a rumored candidate for the Maryland opening after it fired Mick Locksley earlier this season.

Kelly has won everywhere in his career, even leading LSU to an SEC championship game berth in his first season with the Tigers before stalling out last season. Kelly, the winningest coach in Notre Dame history, has a 297-109-2 all-time record with multiple coach of the year awards and two Division II titles dating back to his stint at Grand Valley State.

REQUIRED READING: Brian Kelly wants back in coaching. Did he just hint at his preferred job?

He reflected on what went wrong at LSU in his interview with USA TODAY Sports before the season.

"This past transition for me gave me some time to think about the things I would have done differently or better," he said. "We were in a real new change in college football, where building a front office was crucial. Making sure that your hires were the right hires.

"I think most of the hindsight for me, would be based upon not the culture or process of building a championship program but probably the hiring process. And, making sure that you have the pieces in place to handle where college football is today."

It's clear Kelly wants back in the game. Where, and which team might want to hire him, are the only questions.

#2916. Prediction market traders say an Anthropic IPO is near-certain this year after company filed with SEC

Anthropic on Monday unveiled its Form S-1 filing, taking the company one step closer to completing its long-awaited move to the public markets, which is now seen as almost guaranteed to come this year.

Speculators on prediction market platform Kalshi place 95% odds that the artificial intelligence giant officially announces an initial public offering by the end of 2026.

Kalshi defines officially announcing an IPO as either the Securities and Exchange Commission declaring an S-1 effective, a priced IPO or an exchange assigning a ticker to a stock.

The Anthropic S-1 envisions a Nasdaq Stock Market ticker symbol of "ANT."

Anthropic is aiming to start trading the week of Nov. 16, according to a report in the Wall Street Journal. Traders on Kalshi place just a 14% chance that the company begins trading before then.

On the Polymarket platform, traders give a 69% chance that Anthropic will list on a public exchange and open for trading by Nov. 20.

#2914. Fantasy Football Film Room: Buy low on the forgotten Bear; Justin Jefferson is now a boom-or-bust WR2

Fantasy football is inundated with stats and charts (guilty!), but the more context the film provides, the more reliable the data gets. That's why we have a 2.5-hour show called Stats vs. Film every Tuesday at 6 p.m. ET. That show is live — join us in the chat!

This weekly column will look at some of the biggest storylines I picked up by grinding the film.

Previous versions of this column have broken down Colston Loveland's first-read targets and Luther Burden III's exact role in the Bears offense, but Odunze is the forgotten man right now and he feels on the verge of a big game. On Monday Night Football, Odunze had a 4-87-0 line on an 18% target share. That is in line with bye-week WR3/4 value as a baseline, but can it be more?

Odunze is WR3 in 15+ yard receptions (19), only behind Jaxon Smith-Njigba and Tee Higgins. Of the 19 WRs to have at least 11 explosive receptions, Odunze is the lone player to not have a TD on them. What he's done is impossible to do and a very obvious positive regression sign. In this last game, Caleb Williams happened to miss him on a double move go ball heading into halftime that would've been a 50+ yard TD. Odunze has had his fair share of inexcusable drops that should've been scores, but these are the things that should even out moving forward.

The positive plays Monday are what they typically are. He had a 12-personnel dig route on play action where he added a little fake to the outside at the top of his stem for extra separation. These looks are very valuable because there are often 2.5 players in the route, and these throws are often farther down the field. He also had a scramble drill explosive, something that's baked in with Williams being among the best at creating out of structure.

Ben Johnson found a "trick play" that is really easy to execute, but it's enough eye candy for the defense to get lost in the sauce. When D'Andre Swift flips the ball to Caleb, the entire defensive line stops rushing the passer as usual, which gives him extra time and extra space to throw it. The second level zone defenders are also biting and eyeing Swift in the flats more than usual, allowing more space for Odunze to execute his dig route. This is the perfect trick play to me. The risk of ruin is minimized, while still giving the defense something they rarely see.

Overall, Odunze is the WR51 on WR17 usage in his games with Caleb this year, and he should be bought low as an upside fantasy WR3. The signs are there for a big end of the season.

Quickly on Burden, he played 3 of 21 snaps in heavy personnel. When the Bears established a lead, they went away from 3-WR sets, which is partially explaining the massive splits between halves for Burden this season. He's the WR3 in yards per route in the first half and the WR61 in the second half. When Johnson hasn't isolated him on a first read, the results are worse. That's the reality of a physically dominant yet in-development player. Burden is the WR24 on WR7 usage in games with Caleb this year, so it's no reason to really panic. He's an upside WR2/3 despite the obvious inconsistency.

The backfield is set. Emanuel Wilson was active but didn't play and is on track to be a healthy scratch moving forward, leaving Zach Charbonnet, Jadarian Price and George Holani as the active players. Charbonnet started and played 37% of snaps, leading to 11.6 expected half PPR points (RB22 usage). Price played 48% of snaps, leading to 9.9 expected half PPR points (RB30). Holani played 17%. The trio played on both early and late downs, pass protected, ran routes and carried the ball. It's officially a "let-the-best-man-win" committee, for an organization that held back Kenneth Walker III for years.

So who looked the best? Take a look at this:

Athletically, it's a no contest. Price is so much faster in a straight line and laterally agile. The speed difference is evident on the outside runs, while the lateral ability is easier to see on the cutback runs. That's not the end all, be all, but it absolutely does matter and it's unlikely to change with Charbonnet coming off a February ACL surgery. Price was the one to receive the pitch on a sweep, so the coaching staff obviously agrees.

That said, Price did have two reps where he probably could've done a lot more with the look. One of them was by not giving his center the time to climb to the next level. He instead jammed into a crowded lane, where a more patient and crafty runner would've cut back for a nice gain. The other rep was attempting to bounce it outside of nickel CB Kyler Gordon, when he could've hit his inside shoulder hard for a potential nice gain. Neither were egregious, but the top-end veterans treat them differently.

Charbonnet's runs were more textbook, but there was space to carve out real explosives if he was simply faster. Both of the runs in the video above showcase that glaring flaw. I'd understand it if they'll always value Charbonnet's size and quality decision making in short yardage, but the Seahawks would do well if they continued giving Price a lot of the early down and between the 20s runs to increase their odds of an explosive and to give Price more time on task to develop.

Beyond that, the Seahawks have a very obvious WR depth problem. Jaxon Smith-Njigba is so good that he'll get home regardless, but teams are either straight-up bracketing him or shading a safety over the top of him on most reps. Sam Darnold isn't seeing the field well after the first read, and the depth pass-catchers aren't talented enough to make the reads clearer. It's part of the reason why JSN's role is slightly more underneath this year and very much why Darnold isn't playing nearly as well in 2026. I don't think this is OC Brian Fleury's issue per se, but they need to find another WR at the deadline or find a breakout at RB to counteract this glaring pass-game issue.

The Rams offense has looked a lot different this past month. A lot of that is having zero depth at WR to overcome the Davante Adams injury, but I'm not convinced that's the entire story here.

From Weeks 5-8, Stafford is 1 of 9 on throws with 20+ air yards. The 11% completion percentage and raw completions are obviously alarming but so is the attempt total! He is QB27 in deep attempts on QB6 total attempts during this four-game span.

On tape, Stafford doesn't look fully healthy right now. Some of his sideline and downfield attempts lack his usual zip, and his throwing motion is feeling a little more Philip Rivers-like. He had a sideline completion to TuTu Atwell, but even that one really could've been a score had he led him more. That was more of a line drive that went to his inside shoulder that caused him to hit the brakes a bit. Some of his throws on the move are drifting low and away. And then other throws that are to the intermediate aren't coming with his normal spot-on accuracy.

I wasn't surprised to see Monday's report that his elbow is sore. That's what his throwing motion looks like right now, and the offense being extremely quick-hitting right now is further evidence of just that.

He's fantasy QB16 right now, and I'm not sure if the Rams 3-TE set offense with Puka Nacua working on a 3.4 average depth of target sans Adams is one that's going to lead to massive fantasy days, even if the offense is still moving the ball well. The margins are just a lot tighter in this current offense, and the defense is good enough to keep games on the lower end.

On the Nacua front, he hasn't had a reception of 20+ air yards since Week 4 and has had one target of 15+ air yards in the two most recent games. He's probably not comfortable completely opening up his stride on downfield routes right now, paired with whatever is up with Stafford's elbow. That's OK. He's still the WR3 overall on WR1 usage since returning in Week 4 and is up to 41% targets in the two games without Adams. That'll do.

I still can't believe Lamar Jackson is headed to IR because of a damn thumb injury on the first snap of a showdown with Josh Allen. For Tyler Huntley (turf toe) to also go down is all the more sickening. The Ravens play on Thursday night, so it's too quick to make a move for a Jacoby Brissett or other veteran QB. They'll see what they have with Fagnano again, and it'll be an uphill climb with the Jaguars defense on deck.

Fagnano is a big (6-foot-3, 230 pounds) statue quarterback from UConn, where he finished last year with a 28:1 TD:INT ratio. He went undrafted and has been the QB3 despite not matching up with the Ravens' athletic QB room. He is definitely more of a pocket passer who will struggle to throw on the move or scramble around. That is doubly true now that he's practicing with an ankle brace on. The Declan Doyle offense doesn't need a Lamar Jackson-level athlete to execute, but the margins are tighter, especially behind an offensive line that is in pretty rough shape.

Quickly on that, new center Cam Jurgens has to do a better job of picking up stunts and games. There were too many wasted reps on that alone, and the rest of the right side of the line also had their own issues on one-on-one opportunities. The Ravens really need first-round left guard Vega Ioane back, and he has a chance to return this week.

Fagnano did have a couple of really nice completions to the sideline. They came with anticipation and quality arm talent. That's encouraging in what should be a heavy dropback environment in Week 9 with Jacksonville's defense facing the highest neutral pass rate allowed. Fagnano does look like he can stretch the field horizontally at the very least.

Zay Flowers does a lot of his damage over the middle. It was not Flowers' best work in Week 8. He had a drop on a shallow with room to run. He also fell down on a 3rd-and-8 first-read look before a target was thrown to him. The Bills also bracketed him on another key third down, leading to an incompletion. Fagnano did attempt a big sideline throw to him, but an edge rusher knocked the ball upon release. His lone interception was to Flowers, where he simply stared him down, allowing a zone linebacker to slide his way. My takeaway coming from this game is there is more of a chance for fantasy points for Flowers than what his goose egg would suggest. Fagnano appears to understand the assignment of throwing it to Flowers when he can, but the Jaguars defense will have Travis Hunter and a well-coached defense to squat on these middle-of-the-field throws.

I'd view Flowers as a fantasy starter but more of the WR3 variety than a must start. Here are his splits without Jackson since last year. Please note that last week did fall into the "with" Jackson game. The true split is closer to 13.5 versus 10.5.

Derrick Henry's splits are also problematic. He's the RB28 on RB17 usage this month without Jackson, and his efficiency is night and day depending on if the star QB is out there to threaten defenses himself. I don't trust the OL talent when they simply line up under center, and Henry is far more likely to be phased out due to game script now. He takes the bigger positional tumble in rankings without Jackson and with the Jaguars' dominant run defense. The Ravens have a pathetic team total:

Everyone. The scale I'm using is: Is Justin Jefferson happy? There's no reason he shouldn't be, as very clearly one of the best players in the NFL. Well, right now he's mad and not speaking to the media. Meanwhile, Kevin O'Connell is talking and making pretty direct comments about Kyler Murray: "I thought he made some big-time throws. There were some positives, but there are still some plays we're leaving out there that will help with the overall rhythm of the game. We've gotta continue to find the best way to get him comfortable."

Agree!!! There are some turned down throws beyond his first read, like this one to Jefferson:

Then there are inaccuracies. Murray had a touchdown opportunity up the seam on a correct backside read, but he sailed it. The play below was out of structure, but it's the same result. Sailed. In previous weeks, his intermediate timing-paced routes to Jefferson were missed. Murray has never been considered perfect on these intermediate throws, but this is a step down from his usual for whatever reason.

O'Connell is also on my radar. Even during the Sam Darnold high-flying era, O'Connell left his QB out to dry by asking him to hold onto the ball for downfield concepts without a checkdown available. This is a very difficult offense to run, and it needs an aggressive thrower with stars at receiver to execute. This OL is solid, but it's not elite, making the holding onto the ball part harder.

KOC also doesn't do enough to protect Jefferson when teams are doubling him. The Lions did a great job with Jefferson in Week 8 by playing outside leverage with a CB at the line and sinking a safety to the inside. Jefferson isn't in stacks, in motion, or on the backside of these concepts enough to overcome what is a pretty obvious way to handle this Vikings offense. Murray can't simply throw it up to him in these looks without risk of an interception. The Jordan Addison routes are too often derailed, too. If he played like a first-round WR, then Jefferson wouldn't see these looks nearly as often. This is how Tee Higgins is producing like a top-five receiver in a similar situation.

Jefferson has, by far, the least blame, but he did drop a pass and could've made a highlight touchdown grab up the seam in this past game. He has a 78-855-5 receiving line over his last 17 games for a 50 YPG average. He was at 96 YPG before that. His effort dipped at times throughout this span, but it hasn't been an issue this year. I totally understand if his frustrations start to spill over if this doesn't get corrected. In the meantime, Jefferson has lost his fantasy WR1 label. He's a boom-bust WR2 while the Vikings dip to 31st in passing yards per game on the No. 25 neutral pass rate.

#2913. Ghosted after a job interview? There's a website for that—and thousands of frustrated job seekers have weighed in

Gary Meyer didn't intend to become a ghostbuster. But his website, Did They Ghost You, has become an outlet for thousands of frustrated job seekers who have been ghosted by employers.

Based on anonymous reports — 2,888 at last count — the site tracks and ranks the companies that are most often accused of abruptly ceasing contact with candidates, including some of the biggest names in the S&P 500.

Ghosting is a widespread issue. A recent report from hiring platform Greenhouse found that 45% of job seekers who made it to the interview stage of a hiring process said they were ghosted by an employer afterward, based on a survey of 1,200 U.S. candidates conducted in September 2026.

Meyer, 42, an artificial intelligence-focused marketing consultant who lives in Toronto, created Did They Ghost You in June after listening to his friends vent about interviewing with companies and then never hearing back.

He never intended for the website to be more than a "fun little side project," but when he shared a link to his site on LinkedIn, the reports started pouring in. He says that within the first few days, the site received more than two million visitors: "Clearly, it struck a bit of a nerve."

Meyer, who reads and approves each report himself, says he fielded five or six new submissions every minute at the height of the site's popularity over the summer. To protect people's identities, Meyer typically doesn't publish the contents of the report, and his site doesn't track personal information. Each report contributes to an overall tally of ghosting incidents.

"Was told that I was the preferred candidate by the Director of HR in a debrief immediately following the final interview and then just didn't hear back," one report featured on the site said.

"Ghosted, then reposted their ad a week later," said another.

Meyer's definition of being ghosted by an employer doesn't include applying for a job and never hearing back — unfortunately, "that's part and parcel" of the job market today, he says.

His website is for people who make it through several steps of the hiring process and then never hear from the company again.

"These are people who have gone through multiple rounds of interviews, met with people, flown to various states or cities for the interview and then right at the end, just nothing, no response, nothing," he says.

Part of the reason he created the site is to let those job seekers know they're not alone, he says.

"I think a huge part of what makes it hurt so much when you get ghosted is the assumption is that you've screwed up so badly that you're not worth responding to, that you're not even worth an email," he says.

It's easy for job seekers to blame themselves when they get ghosted, according to career coach Phoebe Gavin, but it's really a failure on the company's part to practice "basic etiquette" in the hiring process.

"The hiring process requires a great deal of unpaid labor from candidates, and it is a reasonable expectation that you will receive some sort of communication, that you will be treated with some sort of dignity," she says.

Although it doesn't excuse their actions, there are a few common reasons that a company might ghost a candidate, Gavin says.

Especially at small companies, managers are often tasked with supervising the hiring process on top of their regular duties. While juggling all those responsibilities, it's easy for a candidate to get "lost in the shuffle," she says.

Another possibility is that there could be some behind-the-scenes factors that the hiring manager can't share with an external candidate, like a budget cut, hiring freeze or leadership turnover, Gavin says.

Still, "candidates deserve to hear from hiring managers regardless," she says.

Meyer says he doesn't intend to turn Did They Ghost You into the next Glassdoor. The site's popularity "got a little bit overwhelming," he says, and he currently has a backlog of reports to read and approve.

He plans to keep running the website, but "the best case scenario is that this problem gets fixed, and there's no point in having the site anymore."

#2912. 2026 NFL Week 9 Power Rankings: Cowboys proving no bottom exists

Don't you dare think that we have reached the bottom. The Dallas Cowboys are proving over and over again that such a place does not exist. There is always lower to go.

Just a few weeks ago we thought that losing to Jalon Daniels and the Tampa Bay Buccaneers was the end of the black hole. Maybe you feel like that was more embarrassing than what happened last week, but the point is we at least have to have the conversation which proves that things are not fun.

It is just barely November and the season feels over for the Cowboys. This isn't something that only fans of the team who have been around for forever are saying because we are tired… this is universal opinion and belief.

Kings stay kings. It was a bummer that last week's game against Baltimore lost juice when Lamar Jackson was hurt.

How many points will they beat the Cowboys by next week?

I've said before… I really doubted them this year. They have proven me very wrong.

Ben Johnson sure can coach. And yes… we all know.

They just do the simple things right. Over and over and over again. That is so much harder than it seems.

I was also very wrong about the Broncos. The offense I think is still a little to be determined, but the defense is incredible.

We really need them to start losing for draft pick purposes.

The undefeateds have all fallen. Cheers to the '72 Dolphins.

Look at Mike McCarthy. 6-2 and in first place with a bye to rest. Must be nice.

Perhaps they are turning into the top team everybody envisioned them to be. We need to see more.

It really seemed like they were turning into a team that could be feisty. Last week was dreadful, though.

On some level it was strange to see them in a pickle and not find the only way to pull it off. They are going to have to work harder than normal this season.

Back from the bye, but they will likely crush the Eagles and fully announce themselves as the soon-to-be NFC East winners.

All the best to Lamar Jackson. This franchise feels a bit at a crossroads at quarterback.

A win is a win and they are stacking them.

They gave Green Bay all they could handle.

Maybe they will jump Dallas in the standings, too.

Did you know Boise State unveiled a Kellen Moore statue? We are all getting old.

How many yards for Jonathan Taylor this week?

They might also give the Cowboys a tough time.

Best in-season trade acquisition ever: Package in 1989 RB Herschel Walker deal

Regardless of whether the credit for the trade goes to Jerry Jones or Jimmy Johnson, it altered the Cowboys' trajectory into becoming a three-time Super Bowl champion. They were 0-5 when they traded Walker, ending the 1989 season at 1-15. It wasn't the five players the Cowboys received from Minnesota that were important, but rather the conditional draft choices that would come if players Dallas acquired were cut the next offseason. Ultimately, the trade landed them Emmitt Smith, Kevin Smith and Darren Woodson, and gave them flexibility to make other moves. Without the trade of Walker, the Cowboys of the 1990s probably never would have existed. — Todd Archer

Remember when that guy in the AT&T Stadium bowels described a Cowboys loss as "embarrassing" a few weeks ago? Well, that individual would have been spot on again on Sunday. Dallas disintegrated against the Cardinals, blowing a 17-3 lead and surrendering 467 yards to Arizona. It's a loss that feels too devastating to have occurred in Week 8. It's where the Cowboys are right now, though: They pin their hopes on their passing game, haven't improved enough defensively and can only hope the running game produces. That's simply not a winning formula.

They had been 69-0 at home when nursing a lead of at least 14 points in the fourth quarter … but that was before Sunday.

Biggest need: Adding to the defense, maybe

At some point the Cowboys need to have an honest conversation about themselves, which seems impossible after team owner Jerry Jones proclaimed everyone is letting head coach Brian Schottenheimer down. Jones has talked about making big trades, and already made one in-season for cornerback Joey Porter Jr. A putrid defense that let Gardner Minshew tear it apart in the fourth quarter and overtime on Sunday needs a lot of help. But, is a team that has losses at home to a Buccaneers team led by an undrafted rookie quarterback and to a Cardinals team that came back from a 14-point deficit in the fourth quarter really a defensive piece away from contending for a Super Bowl? Probably not.

It puts the Cowboys in a weird place. They're not out of the NFC East race because the division isn't good. They're also not very good themselves. It's not even outlandish for the Cowboys to look in the mirror after that hideous loss to the Cardinals and admit they should be sellers. That would require self-awareness, which isn't abundant in Dallas.

What in the hell was that against the Cardinals? That's the kind of loss that gets a coach fired. Jerry Jones said it's not happening now, but Brian Schottenheimer better get it going.

The Cowboys owner said after the game, one in which Dallas blew a 24-10 lead in the final 11 minutes, that he doesn't expect to make a head coaching change during the season, but he didn't rule out doing something. "This is something we will take a good hard look at," Jones said. "That was a total team effort out there for us. We have to be, for our fans, better than that." Dallas has lost three of its last four.

#2911. 32 NFL QBs (including Texans' C.J. Stroud) ranked by QBR in Week 9

Is C.J. Stroud still among the top passers in the NFL entering Week 9?

Stroud, perhaps the one constant for a struggling Houston Texans team, had arguably his worst game of the season, yet still wasn't the biggest problem in a 27-21 overtime loss to the New York Giants at Reliant Stadium on Sunday afternoon.

Against the Giants' secondary, Stroud went 17-of-28 passing (60.7%) for 153 yards and two touchdowns with no turnovers. He was only sacked once, but only converted 25% of his third-down plays.

For the season, Stroud has been averaging over 300 yards per game. He has thrown only one interception and been credited with three turnovers, two of which came back in Week 1 against the Buffalo Bills. On Sunday, it was plenty of short passes for quick gains in hopes of avoiding the pressure, though he did connect three times on 20-plus-yard throws for Tank Dell and Kayshon Boutte.

Stroud also found newcomer Marvin Harrison Jr. for a 6-yard touchdown reception in the second quarter, marking the first scoring drive between the two since their time at Ohio State. Stroud also found Xaiver Hutchinson late in the fourth quarter for a 3-yard touchdown grab to tie the game at 21 and send it into overtime.

After betting on himself and waiting for a larger deal, Stroud continues to emerge as one of the best quarterbacks in the NFL during the 2026 season. Here's a look at where Stroud lands among the rest of the NFL quarterbacks in Total QBR after Week 7, per ESPN.

Patrick Mahomes, Kansas City Chiefs: 67.4

Trevor Lawrence, Jacksonville Jaguars: 64.4

Jayden Daniels, Washington Commanders: 63.8

Baker Mayfield, Tampa Bay Buccaneers: 41.0

Justin Herbert, Los Angeles Chargers: 29.3

Stroud will have a two-week break to build on his successful season before taking on the Los Angeles Chargers on Nov. 8 at SoFi Stadium. Houston will be looking for its third win of the year and enough mojo to turn the season back around before it's too late.

#2910. New national home listing database is taking on Compass in the battle for control of real estate information

A version of this article first appeared in the CNBC Property Play newsletter with Diana Olick. Property Play covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, from individuals to venture capitalists, private equity funds, family offices, institutional investors and large public companies. Sign up to receive future editions, straight to your inbox.

Some heavy hitters in residential real estate just joined the fight over who can see residential property listings. Their goal, they say, is to counter private listings, which are becoming increasingly popular in certain markets.

AGNT (which owns eXp Realty and NextHome), HomeServices of America, Keller Williams and Real REMAX Group, four of the nation's largest brokerages, announced that they are creating a largely open and shared database (API) of listings for brokers — and inviting all other brokerages to join it. Those who do must submit a new listing within one business day. There is, however, an option within the system for certain private listings.

Open Real Estate is designed, according to its founders, to counter the increasingly fragmented residential listing market, and specifically private, off-market listings, which are favored by Compass, a brokerage behemoth. Compass is embroiled in more than half a dozen lawsuits with state and local Multiple Listing Services (MLS) involving listing rules and antitrust claims.

"We want to ensure that for the American consumer, there's a complete data set that is capital T truth, that's reliable as to what is happening in the marketplace," said Leo Pareja, CEO of AGNT. "So whether it's MLSs fragmenting, pocket listings, walled gardens, whatever, the goal is to ensure that there's a complete data set for the American consumer, so buyers and sellers have the most amount of accurate information."

Those who want to join the platform can do so with no initial charge or fees. Pareja said the four founders are going to make the investment into the infrastructure and the technology. Brown Harris Stevens, Lamacchia Realty, Samson Properties and Windermere are listed as initial participants.

"It's clearly a clap back to Compass, firstly," said Jonathan Miller, a longtime housing market analyst and director of markets at StreetMatrix, a housing data and analytics company. "And it's absolutely better because Compass is literally hiding listings from competitors by design."

Just after the announcement, Compass CEO Robert Reffkin posted a congratulatory message on X, saying that Compass is "excited that there will be a national real estate listing service that will return the power back to the creator of the listing — the homeowner and their trusted agent. This is good news for all agents and their clients, and we look forward to helping build the next chapter of listing services."

When asked, Reffkin declined to comment further.

Pareja's response to that: "We saw that, and we're grateful for his appreciation, and he's very much invited. We'd love to have him participate."

While Reffkin's response may seem odd, given that Compass supports private listings, there is one part of the Open Real Estate platform that does allow for exclusives. It's called "participant view." A property that goes into that view is put into a part of the database, "where only agents that are associated with participating brokerages can see it, and it cannot be publicly marketed by anyone, including the listing agent and the listing brokerage," according to Open Real Estate's website.

Reffkin's response came at the same time another MLS, New York's OneKey MLS, joined the California Regional Multiple Listing Service's federal lawsuit against Compass. The organizations are asking the court to firmly establish that rules protecting the shared, public marketing of listings are lawful and pro-competitive, according to a release. It followed OneKey's rejection of Compass's demands to dismantle local cooperation policies.

"This case raises questions that extend far beyond any one company or market," said Richard Haggerty, CEO of OneKey MLS in the release. "It goes to the core of how homes for sale are shared, how everyday buyers access that information, and how we protect a fair, equal housing market for everyone. We believe these issues demand clarity from the court, and joining CRMLS gives OneKey a direct, active voice in securing it."

Separately, last month six regional MLSs representing more than 120,000 real estate professionals nationwide filed a joint federal lawsuit against Compass seeking to uphold rules that promote access to listings through publicly shared databases.

Pareja said the new database is not meant to replace MLSs and that it should not be seen as a national MLS.

"We are going to work with MLSs. We're going to work alongside them. We're not usurping. We're not replacing," he said.
