#2909. 'It was all right': LeBron James takes a sip of fan's wine during Sixers' win over Bulls

Like many very rich people, 40-somethings and very rich 40-somethings, LeBron James loves himself some wine.

The all-time leading scorer in NBA history has long been nose-deep in the wine game, raising glasses with longtime pals-turned-oenophiles like Carmelo Anthony and Chris Paul for more than a decade. His Instagram posts about which wines he and his teammates were enjoying on any given night could drive consumption trends. He's appeared on the cover of Wine Enthusiast magazine; he once pulled up to a Los Angeles Lakers game with a glass of wine. When he and eventual Lakers head coach JJ Redick launched their basketball strategy podcast, "Mind the Game," every discussion of coverages and tactics was punctuated by no small number of swirls and sips; no less a source than r/wine has proclaimed the King "no wine slouch." (And Reddit would never steer you wrong.)

"I've heard it's good for the heart," James told ESPN's Baxter Holmes for a story on the NBA's "secret wine society" published in 2020. "Listen, I'm playing the best basketball of my life, and I'm drinking some wine pretty much every day. Whatever it is, I'll take it."

James isn't quite playing the best basketball of his life anymore, near the start of his 24th professional season and his first as a member of the Philadelphia 76ers. He's still pretty damn good, though, and he's still taking it, whatever it is … even if "it" is in a fan's cup during a game.

With the Sixers holding an 11-point lead on the visiting Chicago Bulls in Philadelphia on Monday night, James leaked out in transition after a missed 3-pointer by Bulls swingman Isaac Okoro. After corralling the long rebound, Philly point guard Tyrese Maxey looked up and tossed a hit-ahead pass for the streaking James … that was just a bit too ambitious for the about-to-be-42-year-old to haul in.

As the ball went out of bounds, so too did James. As he did, he bumped into a fan seated several rows back behind the Sixers' basket. Rather than sprinting back onto the floor to get back on defense, though, LeBron lingered for a moment — long enough, in fact, for the referees to prevent the Bulls from inbounding the ball without him, and for Maxey to run after his new teammate to get him back into the game.

Soon afterward, the replays showed us why. LeBron didn't just take a moment to say hi to a fan; he also took a little swig of what the fan was sipping on, too.

"LeBron crashed into me," the fan, Troy Clemons, told ESPN's Kris Rhim. "He grabbed me, he said, 'My bad.' My drink spilled everywhere. He took my drink from me, and he basically just took a sip of it, gave it back and ran back on the field."

"I wanted to see if it was worth it on my arm, how good it was," a smiling James said in the locker room after the game, according to Rhim. "It was all right."

This isn't the first time that James has considered engaging in a bit of mid-game imbibing with fans. Back in 2017, with his Cleveland Cavaliers holding a lead over the Toronto Raptors in the first game of the second round of the playoffs, James found himself frustrated after smoking a layup that would've given him a chance for a three-point play … and expressed his emotions by skipping over to a courtside vendor and grabbing a bottle of beer out of her hand:

That time, though, James didn't actually slug it down.

"Not much of a beer guy," he said at the time. "If she had had some red wine, I probably would have definitely took a sip."

LeBron James (right) is pretty clearly having a lot of fun in the early stages of his first season in Philadelphia. (AP/Chris Szagola)

Even with how expensive drinks are at arenas these days, Clemons didn't much mind the spill — or sharing a little bit of his spirit with Philly's newest superstar.

"I'm just glad for him to be here," Clemons said. "I'm not worried about the wine getting on me."

Nor, for that matter, were the 76ers too worried about the Bulls. Philly controlled the action for the bulk of the contest, leading by as many as 22 points in what ended as a 126-107 win. Maxey led all scorers with 26 points and 11 rebounds in the win, which improved the Sixers to 7-1, good for first place in the Eastern Conference — a strong, and very enjoyable, start to the voyage for this new-look superteam.

"Listen, it's a long marathon," James said after the game. "If you don't find ways to have fun throughout it — even though you're working, even though you're locked in — then it could wear on you. So for me, especially at this point in my career too, being able to just appreciate the moments, the small moments."

Clemons enjoyed the moment, too — although, somewhat shockingly, he didn't decide to commemorate it with what would've been a 1-of-1 collectible.

"At the end of the day he's another man like me," Clemons said, according to NBC Sports Philadelphia. "I mean, he's LeBron, but at the end of the day, I threw that cup in the trash. And it's nothing up with him — I just got a fresh drink, though."

#2908. Adam Schefter shuts down Garrett Wilson to Steelers trade rumor

The Pittsburgh Steelers have been connected to several wide receiver possibilities as the NFL trade deadline approaches, but one prominent name appears to be off the table.

ESPN NFL insider Adam Schefter poured cold water on speculation surrounding a potential trade for New York Jets wide receiver Garrett Wilson, making it clear that he does not expect the former first-round pick to land in Pittsburgh.

The speculation gained traction after NFL insider Jason La Canfora suggested Pittsburgh could be ambitious ahead of the Nov. 10 trade deadline and potentially pursue another playmaker. Wilson was among the names mentioned as a possible fit alongside DK Metcalf, giving the Steelers another weapon in their passing game.

However, Schefter made it clear that the connection between Wilson and Steelers quarterback Aaron Rodgers should not be viewed as a reason to expect a reunion.

"I was told last week; no way they trade him." Schefter said on his podcast Tuesday.

Wilson and Rodgers spent two seasons together with the Jets in 2023 and 2024, but reports have indicated that their relationship was not particularly strong toward the end of their time together. Schefter suggested that people speculating about the reunion might not understand the full dynamic between the two.

"I don't think people know that full relationship there. I don't know that that would work. That's not happening. Garrett Wilson's not being traded to Pittsburgh."

There are also significant reasons New York would be reluctant to move on from Wilson. The 26-year-old signed a four-year, $130 million contract extension in 2025 and remains a central piece of the Jets' long-term plans. He also has not requested a trade, despite the team's struggles this season.

Pittsburgh, meanwhile, enters its bye week with a 6-2 record and sits in a strong position in the AFC North. With the trade deadline approaching, the Steelers could still explore ways to strengthen their receiving corps as they look to make a legitimate postseason run. For now, though, Steelers fans may need to look elsewhere for a potential trade target.

#2907. Russia confirms new Ukraine peace talks as Melania Trump prepares to host ceasefire meeting

Russia on Tuesday confirmed that peace talks with Ukraine and the U.S. will go ahead next week in Abu Dhabi, United Arab Emirates, while also signaling that a meeting between Presidents Vladimir Putin, Donald Trump and Xi Jinping to end the conflict was on the table.

Putin aide Vladimir ​Medinsky will serve ⁠as Moscow's chief negotiator ​at the talks in Abu Dhabi on Nov. 10-11, Kremlin spokesman ​Dmitry Peskov told reporters on a conference call, according to Reuters.

Separately, first lady Melania Trump will bring Russian and Ukrainian representatives to the White House to discuss the local ceasefire in Kherson, the White House said Tuesday.

Monday saw the end of a six-day ceasefire in the Russian-occupied city of Oleshky and surrounding areas in the Kherson region to allow for evacuation of residents, in a pause of hostilities that the White House said was brokered by the first lady.

Ukraine on Monday accused Russia of using the ceasefire to reinforce its military positions in the city.

The first lady's summit on Thursday will be "an extraordinary moment in American history," the White House said, but did not name the representatives who will attend. Melania Trump will deliver remarks on the Oleshky ceasefire at 11 a.m. ET on Thursday, it added.

Peskov also said Tuesday the Kremlin hopes to finalize plans for a trilateral meeting between the leaders of Russia, the U.S. and China in the coming days. The comments follow earlier reports that these countries were planning to negotiate an end to the war in Ukraine, without Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy at the table.

Zelenskyy has said that any negotiations over Ukraine's future must include Kyiv.

"It can be acceptable only when we are at the table of negotiations," he said on Oct. 20, adding that he had discussed the proposal of a three-way meeting between the U.S., China and Russia to negotiate an end to the nearly five-year full-scale war. "Nothing about Ukraine without Ukraine," he said.

Tensions between the U.S. and Ukraine have mounted over recent weeks amid continued strikes between Moscow and Kyiv, including on civilian and energy targets.

A series of moves by the Trump administration in October raised questions about Washington's reliability as Kyiv's most important military partner.

Tuesday's announcement follows U.S. special envoy Steve Witkoff saying late Monday that Washington will mediate Ukraine-Russia peace talks in Abu Dhabi on Nov. 10-11, focusing on security arrangements, de-escalation and humanitarian issues.

Witkoff also said that U.S. negotiators will meet European and NATO officials in Brussels this week to discuss postwar security guarantees and Ukraine's reconstruction.

#2906. Yankees Need To Try Trading For This Brewers All-Star

The New York Yankees extended a qualifying offer to David Bednar, but it's extremely unlikely that he will accept it.

While that does not mean that the Yankees won't find a way to bring him back, it does mean the Yankees need to be ready to replace him if he does leave. Bednar is one of the best relievers in the game and has been great ever since the Yankees acquired him.

Last season, he posted a 2.47 ERA and recorded 35 saves. If he leaves, the Yankees will need to find a new closer, and while they could try to do so in free agency, trading for a closer may be the right move. The Milwaukee Brewers are reportedly open to trading All-Star closer Trevor Megill, and the Yankees should try to get him.

Megill has one more season before free agency, just like Devin Williams did when the Yankees acquired him. Williams did not work out in the Bronx, but Megill seems more built for the pressure that comes with playing in New York. Unlike Williams, he shined in the postseason, showing pressure does not bother him.

Not only would Megill replace Bednar, but there's an argument to be made that he's better. He is a National League Reliever of the Year finalist this season and has been lights-out in the biggest moments.

#2905. Knicks Bulletin: ‘It’s a matter of time in my opinion’

As everyone already knew before the game, the Knicks beat the Nets.

Here's the latest from the only people that play meaningful basketball in New York's five boroughs.

On the Knicks' bench performance against Brooklyn:

"Our bench was fantastic. I thought Landry came off and gave us a big lift on both ends of the floor. Jordan as well. And then Tyler — I thought Tyler's minutes were tremendous. He did a fantastic job running the team, getting us some easy looks, and then he was solid defensively."

On searching for a lineup combination to stop Brooklyn's momentum:

"When we went into a little funk was at the beginning of the second. Us not scoring gave [Brooklyn] a little hope because they were able to shrink that lead and tie it up, even take the lead. When you give a young team confidence, now it's hard to turn the water off … So I just started searching at that point and went to Tyler, went to Landry, tried JC, and then we found a group that caught a little groove and helped us take the lead back and create separation."

On Jordan Clarkson accepting a reduced role and being an exceptional teammate:

"You have a veteran that's been there, done that. and he's willing to accept the role that most guys wouldn't accept – even guys younger that haven't proved as much as he's proven in this league. And he's willing to accept it. So, in my opinion, if he's willing to accept it, which is what he did last year, and still try to uplift everybody, connect everybody while he's going through what he's going through, then everybody else should. That's that's the ultimate team player, I will say this about JC. I've been around some good teammates. He's got to be the best. He is the definition of ultimate pro."

"A lot of (his minutes) are based on the feel."

On Mikal Bridges' early-season struggles and what he needs to do to regain his form:

"Yeah, he's just got to keep playing. Everybody goes through those ups and downs throughout the course of the season, and obviously, it's the beginning of the season right now, so he's got to find his way. Not just with him but with everybody, when you're in a funk you gotta find one or two things to hold your hat on. Mikal is one of the best runners in the NBA.

"In both directions – offensively and defensively. So he's just got to try to hang his hat on that as well as his cutting and trying to do the other little things – rebounding and driving and touching the paint and spraying it. Then this shot will come eventually."

On his confidence that Bridges will eventually rediscover his shooting touch:

"It's a matter of time in my opinion, before Mikal gets going."

On not penalizing players, including Alavarado, for missing games because of injuries:

"I try not to take a guy out [of his rotation spot] due to injury and the reality is Jose got hurt."

On his conversation with Jose Alvarado and Tyler Kolek about the backup point guard rotation:

"Jose got hurt, and Tyler gave us some good minutes. But at the end of the day, I sat them both down and I told them, 'Hey, Jose, you're our backup because you ended the season as our backup.'

"Both you guys helped us win last year. Jose in the finals, and Tyler in the Cup final. Both you guys got to be ready because a change can happen anytime."

On Karl-Anthony Towns' increased playmaking responsibilities and turnovers:

"KAT's touches are going up, but his turnovers have gone up. KAT is a load making plays with the basketball. He's just gotta finish the play. And not get it deflected or turn it over."

On the Knicks attempting too many risky passes and Towns' offensive fouls:

"Obviously, they have to do better [with the turnovers] and we talked about it. We will continue to talk about it. We've tried to make the home run pass. That's happened a few times. And obviously some turnovers from KAT are offensive fouls. He's got to be better in that area. But for the most part, we have to keep trying to hit singles as opposed to triples and home runs."

On preferring dead-ball turnovers to mistakes that lead to fast breaks:

"Obviously, you don't want your opponent to have the opportunity to get out on the break, so if it's a situation where we have a chance to get back or the ball is dead, I'd prefer that. We've had some turnovers, like [with] Philly in the first half, it felt like we paid the price for it. So to try to just control that a little better at times will help us. But we'd rather have our defense set up than not."

On making better use of his increased touches and playmaking responsibilities:

"I just care about wins. So I gotta do better, making sure with that time [holding onto the ball] and translating it to more wins."

On the need for keeping the Knicks' offense moving:

"I think we just all gotta be together, figure it out. Things just can't get stagnant, and just gotta keep moving."

On Jordan Clarkson accepting a smaller role to help the Knicks win:

"I think it shows his character more than anything else. He's someone that if he was on a different team, he could play 20, 25 minutes every night. He could score double digits. He could be that spark, that microwave off the bench. And he's here. He was willing to sacrifice and take a smaller role, be a part of something bigger than himself. So it shows its character.

"It shows his professionalism. To be able to go out and do those things. And when he's not doing it, he's out there coaching, not on the court, he's coaching guys up, talking to them, letting them know what he sees. So he's the ultimate professional. He's someone that you want on your team. And whenever he's given an opportunity, he'll go out and play well."

On the Knicks finally increasing their pace in the fourth quarter against Brooklyn:

"We finally played fast, got stops. We've been playing very slow, very stagnant. We finally got out, we ran and we got good looks."

On why the Knicks' strong fourth quarter against Brooklyn did not erase their earlier problems:

"No. No, the issues are still there. That [fourth quarter] doesn't erase the problem or erase the issue. So you gotta figure that out and get better."

On the Knicks' offensive struggles persisting despite their victory over the Nets:

"The issues are still there. I think defensively, we were solid. I think this year, for the most part, our defense has been pretty good. Obviously there have been some lapses. … But our offense has to get in a better flow, and that's kind of what's holding us back right now. So a win doesn't erase the problem or erase the issues. We got to figure those out and get better."

On the contributions of the Knicks' reserves against Brooklyn:

"We talked about [Clarkson] earlier, Landry (Shamet), I mean, he had big threes. That third quarter kept us in it. Jose (Alvarado) played well. Ty (Kolek) played well. [Andre Drummond], I think he had to win like nine rebounds, ten rebounds in, like, 11 or 12 minutes. It shows you the depth that we have, that it could be anybody's night on any given night."

On Mike Brown's confidence in him and his willingness to use different players:

"I think it shows his (Brown's) confidence in me. I think he has confidence in every game on the roster. As he's shown, he'll throw any guy in at any time, to do the spark."

On Mike Brown's willingness to confront the Knicks' problems directly:

"We have to be real with ourselves, and Mike's great with that, assessing things in film, calls it as it is. Our group's great with that. We all understand that by no means — we're not taking this as any potential fool's gold. Like I said, the whole goal right now is to continue to get better, and we want to do that. So that's the thought process."

On his early experiences playing against Jordan Clarkson:

"When I was younger, my only thought of him was that he was really hard to guard and it felt like he always went at me. I used to hate it. He'd score and talk a little s—."

On Clarkson's team-first attitude and willingness to accept substitutions:

"Knowing him now, knowing who he is, it's not crazy. The guy just wants to win. He wants to do whatever he can for this group, his teammates. He'll play a stint out there for two minutes and get subbed out. A lot of guys in the NBA, the reality is, if they get subbed, they're pissed. The whole thing. He never has any of those moments."

On the physical toll of the Knicks' championship run:

"We definitely played until June last season and I think everyone still feels it."

"When I got to Utah, I was trying to think of a number. The only player I remembered as a kid who played for the Jazz (whose number wasn't retired) was Greg Ostertag. So, when I got to New York, I kept it."

#2904. As AI becomes more fractured, one name stands out: AMD

As Anthropic rushes to takes its shares public, the AI trade is showing cracks, and they are not confined to fringe players.

We recently wrote about CoreWeave, which fell 15.5% on Oct. 14 as yields on its 2032 notes touched 15%, and its default-swap spreads nearly doubled in October. IREN is down more than 20% this year. Last Thursday, the contagion reached chipmakers, which makes sense since much of CRWV and IREN's spending is going there. AMD dropped 8.1% as Meta's financing plans and a supply warning from Lam Research exacerbated data-center fears about the pace of AI spending.

Because when you screen for factors that demonstrably generate outperformance over time, the list narrows quickly. My screen: price above the 200-day moving average; revenue growing at least as fast as the S&P 500 (5%+ per annum); EPS growing at least as fast (7%+); rating upgrades; a PEG below 3; and ROE and ROIC of at least 12%. Of the names reporting in the next two weeks, AMD is effectively the only one that makes the list. Yes, you can (and arguably should, as our staples article described) position your portfolio with slightly less tech exposure than the broad indices provide, but we can't ignore the market's biggest and most transformative sector entirely.

Especially because the growth in this case doesn't just meet my criteria; it blows past them. Consensus for Q3 calls for EPS of $1.94 on revenue of $13.06 billion, versus $1.20 and $9.25 billion a year ago. That's YoY growth of nearly 62% and 41%, respectively. The recent drawdown came from a sector selloff, not anything AMD reported, but it shows that if some names in the space get hit, even the larger players are likely to feel it. On Oct. 6, Lisa Su said AMD would substantially increase supply in 2027 because AI orders keep outrunning capacity.

So how do you balance factors we know work for a fast-growing company against a backdrop of cracks in the AI trade? Options.

Owning the stock outright means absorbing a roughly 9% to 11% implied earnings-related move (day of). Earnings are about a week out, and over the past four quarters the smallest move over a similar window was more than 9%, and the largest move, after the company reported its Q1 26 results, was 38%! Of course, that kind of volatility means options aren't cheap, and that rich premium is also a reason not to simply buy calls.

A call spread risk reversal does both jobs: it reduces risk if the cracks widen, and the short options offset the elevated volatility we would otherwise pay for. The bottom chart shows that, by sacrificing about $1,700 of upside for $5,000 in outperformance during a severe downside move, and given how jittery this trade has been lately, this is a way to stay in it with considerably less risk. $5,000 less, specifically. Of course, selling that out will tie up some cash, but less so than simply buying the stock. And selling both the out and the call reduces long call purchase by 66%

#2903. Rams best trade option at WR is to promote from within

Rams head coach Sean McVay had heavy praise for receiver CJ Daniels this week, noting that the rookie sixth-round pick has done "a hell of a job" and deserves "more opportunities". Instead of a big trade, should L.A. just promote from within and give Daniels a starting role alongside Puka Nacua?

McVay has been asked as the trade deadline approaches if the Rams are looking to add to their wide receiver room, and so far, the head coach publicly has leaned more towards a "No" than a "Yes". Instead, verbally opting to see other players return from injury, and step up on the depth chart.

Enter Daniels, a Day Three pick in the 2026 NFL Draft who the Rams traded up to acquire. Daniels hasn't accumulated any stats at the NFL level, yet, but McVay hints that Daniels, rather than a trade, could be an answer for the Rams at their receiver position as they move forward.

The Rams have Puka Nacua, Davante Adams is on IR, and after them there is second-year veteran Konta Mumpfield and veteran Ram Tutu Atwell, but no one has been able to fill the void left behind by Adams, and if Nacua goes down, who can Matthew Stafford reliably throw to?

Said McVay on how the Rams can bolster their wide receiver room for the last half of the season,

"I do think there's some guys on the roster that are capable of being able to step-up if their number's called. Whether you're talking about Tutu, I think Mumpfield's done a nice job with his opportunities. There's a guy in CJ Daniels that's really done a hell of a job that you're gonna see get more opportunities."

What does more playing time for Daniels look like? Will he be featured in the offense in a meaningful way? McVay keeps his answer vague, which is fair as he doesn't want to tell other teams what his plans are, but one telling aspect is that McVay does say that he's not going to hand out chances that could hurt the team. So, it begs the question, is Daniels ready to help the Rams now, or does he need more time to develop?

"I would never rule out opportunities to get better, but at what opportunity cost? And you gotta be able to figure out for what does that look like for the long and the short."

If Daniels is ready, and if he performs well-enough to prove McVay right, then the Rams were correct in standing pat at the trade deadline. If Daniels isn't ready, and if Nacua misses time, then McVay may regret the decision not to trade for a wide receiver before the deadline, and or not adding more help through the draft.

The Rams are taking a gamble if they don't add more depth, and according to McVay, not adding more depth could mean they are betting on Daniels.

#2901. Job openings posted big September gain in positive sign for labor market ahead of payrolls report

Job openings surged in September, as did the number of workers who quit their positions, the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported Tuesday, while hiring also rose and layoffs dropped.

In its monthly look at labor market slack and worker mobility, the BLS reported job openings at 7.52 million, up 333,000 from August and well above the Dow Jones consensus outlook for 7.15 million.

As a share of the total workforce, the rate of job openings increased to 4.5%, up 0.2 percentage point from the prior month, according to the Job Openings and Labor Turnover Survey. There are now some 414,000 more job openings than workers classified as unemployed, or about 1.05 openings to every available worker.

Openings rose in manufacturing (54,000) as well as state and local government (108,000) and professional and business services (106,000).

At the same time, the total of employees leaving their jobs spiked to 3.36 million, an increase of 321,000 from August and the highest level since March 2025. Quits are considered a barometer for worker confidence in finding new jobs after leaving their prior positions.

"There are more job openings than unemployed people in the United States right now. That doesn't solve all problems, but it's a big change from a year ago," said Heather Long, chief economist at Navy Federal Credit Union. "It's an encouraging sign that despite all the headwinds right now with high diesel and gas prices and ongoing inflation problems, companies are slowly increasing their hiring."

Hires rose on the month, up 131,000 to 5.4 million, while layoffs and discharges fell by 127,000 to 1.64 million, the lowest since March 2025.

The report comes ahead of the October nonfarm payrolls report, which the BLS will release Friday. The Wall Street consensus is for an increase of 59,000 after September eked out a gain of just 29,000, while the unemployment rate is figured to hold at 4.2%.

In other economic news Tuesday, September factory orders for manufactured goods jumped 1.8%, well above the estimate for 1.3% after increasing just 0.3% in August.

#2900. Ripple strikes deal with Robinhood to expand its RLUSD digital dollar

Ripple has struck a deal with Robinhood to expand the reach of its digital dollar, RLUSD, strengthening trading and financing links between their institutional businesses and improving Robinhood's internal money management.

The partnership involves using RLUSD to settle institutional trades, automate Robinhood's treasury operations and expand access to digital asset trading and financing through Ripple Prime and Bitstamp, the global crypto exchange acquired by Robinhood last year.

It also helps meet Robinhood's ambition of becoming a global financial superapp, centralizing its customers' financial lives while expanding the underlying infrastructure that can serve both retail and institutional clients.

"Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations and Ripple Prime for financing," Robinhood Crypto General Manager Johann Kerbrat said in a statement shared with CNBC. "That is what a connected, global financial system looks like."

Stablecoins have long been a workhorse of the crypto world, giving traders a way to move money among exchanges, buy and sell crypto assets, and park funds between trades without having to convert back into traditional currency. But as the crypto industry evolves beyond trading and markets move toward 24/7 liquidity, stablecoins are becoming essential financial infrastructure because of their ability to enable near-instant settlement and move dollar-denominated value at any time, regardless of traditional banking hours.

As firms look to do more with stablecoins beyond simply listing them on exchanges, stablecoin issuers are betting they'll favor a one-stop-shop approach that bundles the technology and services needed to move money, settle trades and manage cash.

"Stablecoins have evolved from a crypto trading tool into a critical piece of financial infrastructure," Ripple President Monica Long told CNBC. "The clearest evidence of this is where they're now being embedded — from corporate payments to capital markets. Our partnership with Robinhood reflects that evolution."

"This is about much more than trading," she added. "It's about fundamentally changing how money moves and is managed — giving institutions the ability to operate 24/7/365, unlocking liquidity and putting capital to work more efficiently."

Circle Internet Group, a Ripple competitor, is positioning itself for a similar future, where winning platforms, in addition to issuing stablecoins, also provide the infrastructure to put them to work. Earlier this year, for example, it launched the Circle Payments Network, connecting financial institutions and payment providers using its flagship USDC stablecoin and other digital assets for cross-border payments.

The network is Circle's answer to the 8-year-old Ripple Payments business, a service that helps businesses move money across borders, using any of several assets moving through that system, including USDC, Ripple-created XRP and, as of its debut in December 2024, RLUSD.

RLUSD's market cap has grown to more than $2.5 billion. Tether's USDT and Circle's USDC still dominate the total stablecoin market, which now exceeds $300 billion.

RLUSD will also be listed on Robinhood's trading platform, and is currently available to traders on Bitstamp as well as Robinhood Chain, the company's home-grown blockchain for tokenized stocks and other assets.

Robinhood, which reports third-quarter results for the three months ended in September after the market closes, rose about 2.5% in early trading Tuesday, snapping a three-day decline.

Correction: A previous headline misspelled RLUSD.

#2898. Apple stock sinks as global memory crisis hurts profits, giving Ternus his first big test

John Ternus' first earnings report as Apple CEO was going fine until it was time to issue a forecast for the company's gross margin, one of the key metrics followed by investors.

Apple reported better-than-expected earnings and revenue on Monday, including in the company's core iPhone unit, and even projected sales in the current period that topped estimates. But just before Ternus opened the earnings call up to analysts' questions, CFO Kevan Parekh came forward with the first real piece of bad news.

Parekh said that for the December quarter, which goes through the holiday season, Apple's gross margin will be between 45% and 46%, trailing the 46.3% average analyst estimate, according to LSEG.

"This includes primarily the impact of higher memory costs, as well as some costs related to the ramp of Siri AI," Parekh said.

It's part of an ongoing trend for Apple, which is absorbing some of the soaring costs from the global memory shortage. A margin drop in the fiscal first quarter would be a third straight decline for Apple, following a gross margin of 50.1% in the June period and 47.9% in the September quarter, boosted by 1 percentage point of tariff refunds.

At the midpoint of the forecast range, it would be Apple's lowest gross margin since the fiscal fourth quarter of 2023.

"What we're seeing with memory pricing right now is unprecedented," Ternus said on the call, adding that the company is paying more than it did earlier this year. "Looking beyond the December quarter, we see the pricing for memory purchases continuing to increase."

Ternus, who took the reins from Tim Cook on Sept. 1, is grappling with a problem that his predecessor spent his final months addressing. Cook told The Wall Street Journal in June that the memory situation was a "hundred-year flood." Apple followed by raising prices across the board on Macs, iPads and accessories.

In September, Apple increased the prices of its iPhones by $100, which Ternus said the company did "reluctantly."

Computer and smartphone makers everywhere are suffering. On Friday, Intel had its worst day on the market since the 1970s due to a weak forecast tied to the softening PC market. It's all because of insatiable artificial intelligence demand.

AI server giants like Nvidia and hyperscalers are buying up all the RAM they can find, and they're willing to pay a premium for it, leaving little supply for companies that make smartphones and tablets.

Despite its size and global clout, Apple said it expects supply constraints on iPhones and Macs in the holiday quarter, primarily driven by memory.

On the call, Ternus had some top lieutenants field component-related questions. Sabih Khan, chief operating officer, became the first Apple COO to appear on an earnings call since Cook in 2010, when Steve Jobs was CEO.

"As we have said before, we're seeing less flexibility in the supply chain than normal," Khan told analysts on the call.

In response, Apple has drastically increased its purchase obligations to secure supply, Khan said. Those obligations rose by $33 billion from the June quarter to the September period, Khan said, though he declined to answer questions about whether Apple had signed "long-term agreements" with memory makers.

"We're always working hard to improve supply," Khan said.

Even with the recent margin slide, Apple's gross margins are still well above where they sat earlier in the Cook era, when the number would predictably come in between 38% and 39%. As Apple's profitable services business grew, its gross margin expanded, hitting 40% in 2021 and growing from there.

Analysts see a potential silver lining for Apple in the current market turmoil. Because Apple is more capable of eating a portion of the increased costs, some experts predict the company will take market share from makers of Windows PC and Android phones that don't have such a cushion.

Citing data from a third-party researcher, Ternus said gains are already happening.

"According to IDC, we gained share globally for the quarter and for the full fiscal year," Ternus said. "And we have never sold more Macs in a fiscal year than we did this time, which is a truly remarkable testament to these incredible products."

#2897. Crude oil prices fall for second day as market hopes for diplomatic resolution to Iran war

Crude oil prices fell Tuesday for a second consecutive session, as the market hopes the U.S. and Iran will reach a diplomatic settlement to the conflict that has disrupted global energy supplies for eight months.

U.S. West Texas Intermediate futures lost 37 cents to close at $96.09 a barrel after dipping below $95 earlier in the session. Brent crude, the international benchmark, shed $1.17 to settle at $103.60.

The move lower came after rumors circulated the U.S. and Iran were making progress toward a deal.

Oil sold off more than 7% on Monday after Washington and Tehran signaled they were both open to discussions to reach a settlement. President Donald Trump told reporters Tehran wants "to make a deal and that could happen."

Qatar is continuing to mediate talks between the U.S. and Iran, the Gulf state's foreign ministry said Tuesday. A delegation from Qatar held talks in Tehran in an effort to reach an agreement that prevents further escalation in the region and ends the U.S.-Iran conflict, the foreign ministry said.

However, Iran's Foreign Ministry spokesman Emsaeil Baqaei said Tuesday that Tehran will not accept diplomacy based on coercion, according to the state-owned media outlet Islamic Republic News Agency. It was unclear where talks actual stand.

The market had been pricing in the risk of escalation after the U.S. midterm elections, as reports circulated that Washington was drafting plans for strikes on Iran. Crude prices gained more than 10% in October in response.

The situation in the Middle East remains volatile and there's still substantial risk that fighting could break out. Washington attacked Iran's Qeshm Island on Monday, U.S. officials told The Wall Street Journal, adding that the U.S. has been carrying out "limited strikes" against the country in recent weeks.

The military campaign against Iran has been kept deliberately low-profile to avoid signaling plans for a major escalation in fighting, the report said, quoting an unnamed official.

And commercial ships continue to come under attack in the Persian Gulf, Strait of Hormuz and Gulf of Oman. At least four ship attacks have been reported since the start of the month, according to the United Kingdom Maritime Trade Operations Centre.

Despite these attacks, the Trump administration has also been touting a possible diplomatic solution to the conflict, with Vice President JD Vance telling CNN, "The president and I both feel really good about the prospect of potentially coming to a negotiated settlement."

On Sunday, U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio said Trump would like to resolve the Iranian war "diplomatically," and prefers not to use military force.

High oil prices have contributed substantially to inflation and high gasoline prices in the U.S., which have become a cause of concern for Trump ahead of the midterm elections.

Last week, two Federal Reserve presidents said interest rates will need to move higher to rein in inflation. Higher rates generally crimp growth and raise costs for consumers, but help to bring down inflation.

— CNBC's Priyanka Salve contributed to this report.

#2896. FC Barcelona’s $721M fan spending estimate puts it ahead of its rivals

FC Barcelona's $721M fan spending estimate puts it ahead of its rivals appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

FC Barcelona had more in common with the NFL and Major League Baseball than with its closest soccer rivals in Zero1 Gaming's October 2026 fan spending report. The study put Barcelona sixth among 45 fandoms, with an estimated $721.28 million in spending. The NFL and MLB were the only sports entries ahead of it. Manchester United and Real Madrid finished seventh and eighth, respectively, leaving Barcelona as the highest-ranked individual team in the study.

Sixth place sounds less impressive until you look at the company. Pokémon finished first at an estimated $1.71 billion, and the NFL came second at $1.36 billion. Disney and Super Mario followed before MLB rounded out the top five. Barcelona was the first individual club on a list that, to that point, had been led by entire leagues and entertainment franchises. Its position is striking because the study puts one club's shopping demand on the same scale as all of those different kinds of fandom.

Zero1 Gaming put Manchester United at $562.31 million and Real Madrid at $558.69 million. That leaves Barcelona about $159 million clear of United and $163 million ahead of Madrid in the model. The gaps are especially interesting because the study used the same price for each club's signature item, a $100 replica jersey. Barcelona had the advantage before the model put a dollar value on any of those searches.

The club drew 12.32 million purchase-related searches in the study's September 2026 data. Real Madrid drew 9.68 million, and Manchester United drew 9.63 million. Barcelona's lead of more than 2.6 million searches over either club matters because the model applies the same purchase assumptions to each fandom. With the jersey price held constant, a bigger pool of searches produces a bigger estimate for that part of the calculation.

The report gives signature products more weight than any other purchase category. It assumes a 35% conversion rate for those products, compared with 17.5% for apparel, 5% for premium collectibles, and 1.25% for tickets. Across all 45 fandoms, signature items represented almost half of estimated spending. A jersey serves that role for a soccer club, just as a game can for a gaming franchise. That shared framework helps explain how Barcelona and Super Mario can occupy places on the same list.

There's also a reason the jersey makes sense as the centerpiece of a sports fandom. As a Zero1 Gaming expert put it, "A jersey, a trading card, or a plush toy works like a membership badge, and fans are happy to pay for it when it ties them to a community." The study can attach a price to the shirt. The quote gets at why the same basic item can mean so much more to the person wearing it.

MLB offers a useful test of how far search volume alone can take a fandom. Baseball drew 10.42 million purchase-related searches, about 1.9 million fewer than Barcelona. Yet its estimated spending reached $822.23 million, roughly $101 million more. The study priced MLB's replica jersey at $135, compared with $100 for Barcelona, and used $385 for baseball's premium collectible, compared with about $196 for the club. Those differences help explain how MLB could sit one place higher despite attracting fewer searches.

The reverse comparison with Real Madrid makes the point from another direction. Madrid's average ticket price in the study was $159.70, comfortably above Barcelona's $95.30. But tickets carried the lowest assumed conversion rate and accounted for just 4.6% of estimated spending across the study. A higher ticket price has relatively little leverage when the model assumes roughly one purchase per 80 searches. Barcelona's advantage in search volume had more room to show up in the final ranking.

That distinction matters when the number at the center of the story is as precise as $721.28 million. Zero1 Gaming combined a month of Google search data with price research and assumptions about how often shoppers would buy. Each conversion rate is an input to the estimate. Even across all 45 fandoms, the study's spending total ranged from $6.02 billion to $16.88 billion when it tested lower and higher conversion rates. Under those tests, every fandom stayed within two spots of its original rank. The dollar figures had room to swing much further than the order did.

That's why Barcelona's place on the list deserves more attention than the decimal points. Against Manchester United and Real Madrid, the club had a substantial search advantage with all three replica jerseys priced alike. Against MLB, the comparison shows how much prices can change the outcome even when Barcelona generates more searches. Taken together, those comparisons give the ranking some meaning beyond bragging rights. In the club comparison, the shared jersey price makes it easier to see what Barcelona brought to the calculation through its higher search volume.

The most telling detail may be the company Barcelona keeps on either side of sixth place. Below it sit two rival clubs that offer the same signature product at the same modeled price. Immediately above it sits an entire league whose higher-priced items helped it do more with fewer searches. Barcelona landed between them on the strength of shopping interest attached to a single club. That's a better reason to pay attention to sixth place than the $721 million figure alone.

#2895. Bill Ackman warns of 'casino' stock market as speculative trading grows

Bill Ackman told CNBC on Tuesday he worries that the stock market is broken, saying the rise of speculative trading is leading to wild price swings thanks to the amount of leverage in the system.

"Every number of days, I feel like markets are broken," Ackman said on CNBC's "Squawk Box." "Stocks trade at prices that make no sense to me. Yes, it's good if you're a long-term investor and you can buy at a stupid price. I don't think it's good for the country generally."

"We're back to the casino-type markets when there was 95% leverage," he added. "I don't think that's inherently good for the economy, for society, and it's leading to these massive moves in stocks."

The founder of Pershing Square Capital Management said those same dislocations in the market have given the hedge fund the opportunity to snag big bargains.

Pershing bought Microsoft, for example, when it was pummeled during the "SaaSpocalypse" earlier this year, and Amazon when it fell following President Donald Trump's tariffs, Ackman said. It also bought Alphabet when the stock moved "massively" following ChatGPT's launch, he said.

But the billionaire investor worries that the rise of one-day leveraged vehicles, perpetual futures and sports betting is dangerous for investors who are treating the stock market as a get-rich-quick scheme — an attitude that could have disastrous consequences.

"It's good for us long term — 'us' being Pershing Square," he said. "I don't think it's good for the country."

Among the stocks Ackman is especially bullish on are Visa and Mastercard, which he called among the world's "greatest" businesses, as well as streaming platform Netflix, in which he disclosed a position in August.

#2893. 5 Reasons For The Jazz To Consider A Lauri Markkanen/Domantas Sabonis Swap

The Jazz have been disappointing to start the year, and you know what that means; it is time to completely alter the foundation of the team, purely for the sake of it. Will Trade A or Trade B actually make the team better? No one can say, but nevertheless, they must be completed – SOMETHING must be done, anything!

There are the obvious, small-potatoes that Jazz fans are proposing. Kris Dunn would be nice; he would immediately be our only positive defender who can also dribble and pass. Tre Jones, maybe? He can run a clean offense off of the bench. Maybe we give Nikola Topic a shot! I wonder if there's a reason our minds are all immediately going to back-up point guard options.

However, one report (potentially not based in reality) out of Sacramento really got people thinking:

(EDITORS NOTE: Since writing, this report has gained a bit more credibility, via Tim McMahon)

Before I elucidate the 5 reasons I think a trade for Domantas Sabonis makes basketball-sense, let me give the lay of the land:

The trade that I am defending is a one-for-one Lauri Markkanen and Domantas Sabonis swap, which works financially.

I do not know who has more value. Markkanen's contract is longer, but seemingly more desired across the league. Thus, due to my confusion, I am assuming no picks are exchanged.

Maybe this is a three-team trade, with Markkanen sent elsewhere. That doesn't impact my thinking in this article. I do not care where Markkanen lands, and am operating under the assumption that this deal nets us Sabonis and costs us Markkanen.

With that in mind, please indulge me as I arrogantly, ignorantly, and prematurely attempt to fix the Utah Jazz.

Will Hardy loves his top-of-the-key, dribble-handoff offenses. He gave Kelly Olynyk his last strong full season showing, he got lots of run out of Kyle Anderson, and he built an entire offense around Jusuf Nurkic after premature claims that Father Time had conquered the Bosnian Beast. A big man at the top of the key and 4 swarming, cutting teammates is Hardy's idea of a good time. I mean, look at how every set from Nurkic's 3rd triple double of last year was organized:

Jusuf has still yet to play a game in the 2026-2027 season as he deals with injury, and his absence is obvious. Hardy has attempted to run some of the sets with Jaxson Hayes, but Hayes simply doesn't have the feathery touch or sense of timing that makes Nurkic so deadly from these spots. Because of this, the offense has stalled. The Jazz are last in the league in rim attempts per game, and in the bottom 10 in three point attempts. These are the shots that get prioritized in offenses built off of ball movement and motion, but with Nurkic out, many possessions end with Keyonte George or Isaiah Collier dribbling for 18 seconds before taking a contested mid-range jump shot.

Domantas Sabonis was born for the Will Hardy offense. In 2023, the Kings had the best offense in the association, and it was built almost entirely on the back of Domantas Sabonis and his top-of-the-key decision making. He is, indisputably, the 2nd best big-man passer in the league, and around the top when it comes to screen-setting as well. Hardy has turned players that the league had given up on into the centerpieces of offenses, and Sabonis, being a player still in the thick of his prime, would actualize Hardy's offensive dreams to an extent not yet before seen. Sabonis would potentially average a triple double on this team, and the offense would have a structure in a way that has been heretofore, absent.

When Sabonis is being derided, it is fairly easy to anticipate the reasoning of the deriders. He isn't a vertical, shot-blocking presence around the rim. He isn't the quickest when switched out onto the perimeter. And he isn't necessarily the most intimidating shooting threat. Domantas Sabonis, for all his skills, is a known commodity – what he can't do now, he likely won't be able to do later.

Similarly, people seem to generally understand what Jaren Jackson Jr. struggles with. He struggles as a rebounder and with physicality in general, and thus isn't really cut out to play consistent center minutes. He often falls victim to tunnel vision, and seems to have made the decision to pass or shoot before he begins his offensive process. On paper, Jaren seems like the versatile big man option that every contender craves, but once you peel back some of the layers, you can see that he quite dependent on a context that caters to his flaws.

I struggle to think of a more synergistic front court pairing than these two.

JJJ can struggle on the boards as much as he wants, and the Jazz would still be carried to a respectable mark by 3 time rebounding champion Domantas Sabonis. There's a limit to the level of defense Jaren can anchor on his own due to his poor rebounding, but Sabonis, who is not a good individual defender, would boost Jaren by, at the very least, consistently being able to end plays with rebounds. Domantas can continue to struggle blocking shots (his highest per-game mark is 0.6), but as long as he can be large down low, he allows Jaren Jackson Jr. to play to his strengths as a help-side roamer. Jaren provides spacing for Domantas to both hit cutters up top and bang down low, and Sabonis can set the bruising screens that Jaren never really could. Together, these two former all-stars can amplify strengths and mitigate weaknesses. We would not just be trading for Domantas Sabonis; in this deal would be included a superior version of JJJ.

I hope to make one thing clear; I am not a Lauri Markkanen "hater". In fact, I have strongly advocated for keeping him throughout the rebuild, and my opinion of him has not really changed after his slow start to the year (although I do have a theory that his transformation into a body builder has hurt his flexibility and, in turn, his impact). Today, Markkanen is still an all-world level off-ball shooter and scorer who, in most contexts, can add to a team's attack without taking anything away. However, I am beginning to wonder that Utah is not one of those contexts. This team is facing a severe drought of dribblers and passers – everyone is attempting to do their work off-ball. Without players with gravity to work off of, Lauri Markkanen is taken out of his ideal role.

Normally, I wouldn't mind this too much. Even playing out-of-role, Markkanen is still clearly a positive offensive player. However, with the rapid development of Ace Bailey, one must now consider the opportunity cost of every Lauri Markkanen minute played and action ran. This season, Ace has made huge strides defensively (making him an even better fit next to a guy like Sabonis), and has continued to improve his efficiency in all the same sets that Hardy runs for Markkanen. Both are big forwards who move and shoot like smaller, quicker players, but who aren't necessarily the most capable creating their own shot. Considering that (a) Markkanen is significantly more expensive and (b) he is significantly older, it has been clear to me that, eventually, he would be the odd-man out and the first of the core to be traded. With Ace taking a leap and Jaren filling the other forward possession, now is as good a time as any to start looking into Markkanen trades (although I would feel a twinge of guilt if he did spend the heart of his prime in Utah, waiting for a playoff run, and then we trade him to Sacramento). If that trade can net a former all-star who is a perfect fit in Hardy's offense, that should cause some real consideration on the part of the Jazz brass.

As discussed in Reason #1, the Jazz are currently outlierishly bad at interior scoring, making 5 less shots in the paint per game compared to the #29 team in that stat. Sabonis will help in one aforementioned regard; his top-of-the-key passing will open up rim attempts for cutters. However, that isn't the only mechanism Sabonis will utilize to put consistent pressure on the paint; he himself would be the most intimidating rim presence on the roster. Sabonis consistently shoots near half of his shots within 3 feet of the basket, and shoots those shots at a career 72% clip. Many of these shots are assisted, but not all of them – Sabonis has shown the ability to post-up on his own and collapse the defense. Even the assisted shots from Domantas have more value than any of the same plays from the current interior players the Jazz employ. Sabonis has a combination of fluidity, strength, and touch that no one of the team can match, which makes him a more intimidating presence rolling to the rim than any of Hayes, Markkanen, Bamba, or whoever. Currently, the Jazz attack is centered entirely from the outside – rim attempts feel more lucky than strategic. An inside-out attack would strain opposing defenses in a way the current roster simply cannot.

I hope my sarcasm was evident in that subheading. In reality, I have 4 reasons and an anti-reason. I think each of the prior 4 are strong enough on their own, but they also operate under a base assumption that might not be true: that something must be done. In doing so, they disregard the most likely scenario. To me, that scenario still is that the Jazz are not a bad team. Maybe they're not the level of a 6 seed, as I saw some hoping, but likely they are closer to the average projection of quality than how they are currently performing. Would it be nice for the Jazz to have performed better than they have? Sure, but in that same vein, it would be nice if Jaren Jackson Jr. did not immediately get injured. I would be nice if we could see Jusuf Nurkic hitting cutters like he did last season. It would be nice if we weren't missing a disproportionate amount of our three pointers. Yes, it would be nice if we were winning, but this losing is not something that we can yet draw meaningful data from.

So, a proposition; let us continue to watch the games with caution, and a critical eye, but with a mind not yet predisposed to make changes at the first sign of trouble. Make careful notes of where the team is struggling, and pay close attention to how those areas develop throughout the remainder of the season, and even keep potential trade targets in your mind, but to this point? There is limited need for any of these changes to be made reality. This team has disappointed me too, Lauri Markkanen included, but I think it is important to maintain a sense of confidence in the player evaluations we have crafted for years, instead of tossing them out the window and calling the Kings at the first sign of trouble. Sabonis would be a good fit on this roster, but he'd be a good fit at the February trade deadline too; at this point, there should be no rush for transactions.

What are your thoughts on Sabonis's fit on the team? Markkanen's? When is the right time to start discussing trades? Sound off below!

#2892. The Bears’ Big Trade May Have Unlocked Something Else

The Chicago Bears paid a substantial price for Jeffery Simmons because they needed someone who could change games from the middle of their defensive line. His first appearance offered an encouraging possibility: the trade could also help them get more from another expensive investment.

Montez Sweat returned from his two-game suspension Monday night and immediately gave Seattle problems. He finished Chicago's 34-17 victory with a sack, three quarterback hits and a tackle for loss. Two additional sacks were erased by defensive holding penalties.

Afterward, Simmons revealed that he had arrived with a challenge for his former Mississippi State teammate.

"I challenged him this week," Simmons said during his postgame press conference. "Get back to the old Sweat."

Nov 2, 2026; Seattle, Washington, USA; Chicago Bears defensive line Montez Sweat (98) reacts after a sack against the Seattle Seahawks during the third quarter at Lumen Field. Mandatory Credit: Joe Nicholson-Imagn Images

That request carried some history. Simmons knew Sweat long before either player joined the Bears. Their reunion gave him a chance to demand the version of his teammate he remembered from college, and their first game together provided reasons to believe the partnership could work.

Sweat entered Monday needing a productive return. He had two sacks through five appearances before serving a suspension for violating the NFL's performance-enhancing substances policy. Chicago won both games he missed, beating Atlanta and New England.

The Bears had committed heavily to Sweat after acquiring him in 2023, signing him to a four-year, $98 million extension. His ability to affect quarterbacks remained important to their ambitions, even as attention shifted toward the All-Pro arriving from Tennessee. Simmons' presence gave Sweat a different set of opportunities.

"I definitely got my opportunities," Sweat told The Athletic's Kevin Fishbain. "I definitely had some one-on-ones. I was looking forward to taking advantage of it, so I'm looking forward to doing that for the rest of the year."

Those individual matchups are a significant part of the appeal. An offense that devotes extra help to Simmons has fewer resources available elsewhere. Pressure through the middle can also reduce the space a quarterback has to step away from a defender coming around the edge.

Sweat still has to defeat the blocker in front of him. In Seattle, he repeatedly did enough to disrupt Sam Darnold, including on the two sacks that penalties wiped away. Those plays cannot be added to his official total, but they belong in an assessment of how he performed. Chicago saw more successful rushes than the final sack column alone would suggest.

The benefits also reached defensive coordinator Dennis Allen. According to Fishbain's reporting, the Bears posted their lowest blitz rate of the season at 20.5 percent while generating 14 pressures.

Nov 2, 2026; Seattle, Washington, USA; Chicago Bears defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons (99) reacts after a defensive play against the Seattle Seahawks during the fourth quarter at Lumen Field. Mandatory Credit: Joe Nicholson-Imagn Images

Sweat said Simmons kept stressing the importance of reaching the quarterback with four rushers. Doing that allows more defenders to remain in coverage, giving Allen another way to make passing downs difficult.

Simmons described those opportunities as a responsibility for everyone across the front. If opponents assigned two blockers to him, he expected teammates to win against one. When he received an individual matchup, he expected the same standard to apply to him.

That makes the reunion about more than familiarity. Simmons has already established that he intends to push Sweat, while inviting his teammates to hold him accountable in return.

There is still a difference between a promising debut and a partnership that delivers consistently. Future opponents will adjust their protections. Chicago will need answers when offenses find ways to limit the opportunities Seattle allowed.

The Bears also had been improving defensively before Simmons arrived. His addition gives that progress another potential source of momentum, with Sweat well positioned to benefit. For now, Monday offered the kind of response Chicago needed. Sweat returned, took advantage of his chances and helped the Bears beat the defending champions on the road.

Simmons had plenty to manage during his first few days in Chicago. Finding time to challenge an old teammate may turn out to be another valuable part of his arrival.

#2891. The 'poison pill' ballot measure that could kill the California billionaire tax

The Inside Wealth newsletter by Robert Frank is your weekly guide to high-net-worth investors and the industries that serve them.

The California billionaire tax could face a stiff challenge from a competing ballot measure that would ban state taxes on retirement accounts, according to tax experts.

California voters will decide the fate today of Proposition 40, known as the Billionaire Tax Act, which would impose a one-time tax of 5% on the total net worth of Californians worth over $1 billion. The so-called "billionaire tax" is one of the most widely watched ballot measures this election season, since it would be the first state tax on net worth and taps popular anger over the rising power and wealth of tech billionaires.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm elections

The tax requires a simple majority to pass. Since it would only tax the state's roughly 250 billionaires, many political experts initially predicted easy passage. Yet a recent poll by the Public Policy Institute of California shows it falling short, with 46% supporting and 52% against.

Supporters of the tax blame a $300 million ad blitz funded by tech billionaires and business groups opposing the measure. Many of the ads claim the ballot measure would allow the Legislature, with a two-thirds vote, to expand the tax to everyday Californians.

Yet tax experts cite another potential roadblock to the tax: Proposition 42. One of two ballot measures that would act as poison pills against the billionaire tax if they get more votes, Prop 42 calls for a permanent ban on any statewide tax on property of all Californians.

The ballot measure "Prohibits any new state tax (1) imposed on the ownership of personal property (all things people own other than real estate), or (2) that applies retroactively based on taxpayer's past activities."

The official California Voter Information Guide explains the "pro" argument by saying that Prop 42 protects Californians from double taxation by "prohibiting new and retroactive taxes on personal property, including retirement and savings."

The "con" argument, according to the voter guide, is that Prop 42 is "just another billionaire-funded trick." Propositions 41 and 42 were created and submitted by Building a Better California, a political action committee funded by tech executives and several California billionaires opposed to the billionaire tax.

Political and tax experts say voters' fears about protecting retirement accounts will be a powerful motivation to vote "yes" on Prop 42, thus nullifying the billionaire tax. As much as they may want to tax billionaires, their retirement accounts may be more important.

"Both Proposition 41, which prohibits new taxes from disregarding voter-approved spending limits, and Proposition 42, which prohibits new taxes on retirement accounts and personal property, could win majority support," said Jared Walczak of Walczak Policy Consulting.

#2890. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.

#2888. The Dallas Mavericks need to grow up

The NBA regular season is filled with numerous peaks and valley's. 4 game winning streaks can become 6-game losing skids, dominant wins can be followed by embarrassing losses.

You will find no better example of this phenomenon than the Dallas Mavericks in their two-game stretch against the Houston Rockets and Sacramento Kings. The Rockets game was an announcement to the rest of the NBA.

Cooper Flagg and Kyrie Iriving have brought the Mavericks back, and they will no longer be a punchline in NBA culture.

Losing to the Kings by 15 points is a reminder that this team won 26 games in the 2026-2027 season, and that their superstar is still only 19-years-old.

The feeling among the fanbase was already negative hours before tip-off when the Mavericks ruled Irving out with left-knee injury management. This left many (myself included) very confused, seeing as the Mavericks had not played since Friday.

But Irving's absence is not the reason for their performance, nor should it excuse the frankly embarrassing effort the Mavericks showcased Monday night. Flagg had, in my estimation, the worst game of his NBA career, shooting 3-19 from the field with 6 turnovers. But the stats don't illuminate just how poor Flagg was. He was lazy in transition, didn't show any interest in boxing out, and struggled to generate any advantages for his teammates.

Flagg was not alone in his lack of intensity, as the rest of the Mavericks roster (save for Walter Clayton Jr. and Morez Johnson Jr.) played like they had forgotten what a three-pointer was. The Mavericks shot 7-33 from three, and were 3-28 outside of Clayton Jr.'s shooting. They struggled to execute any sort of offensive set, nor could they effectively space the floor at any point. The Kings, of all teams, exposed many of the flaws this team possesses, all of which were amplified by the lack of Irving.

But the players weren't the only ones at fault.

For the first time this season, Dusty May showed that he is a first-time NBA coach. The starting lineup was a baffling decision. Starting Flagg alongside no other ball-handlers or shooters is a proven terrible strategy, and yet, May fell into the same trap that Jason Kidd fell into last season. Flagg is simply much better when he can play off of another ball handler, and not when he is next to Caleb Martin and P.J. Washington.

Losing games like this is reminiscent of last year's team, which triggers dark memories for all Mavericks fans.

Now, this game could simply be a mirage. An off-night where your best player simply played terrible. But the Mavericks have to break their bad habits of years previous. These losses are unacceptable for a team that should have playoff aspirations. And this isn't simply a one-year focus point. Good habits are the things that lead to championships in the future, and those good habits need to be built as early as possible.

Hopefully the Mavericks use this game as a learning experience, because if they don't, the season could turn ugly before the blink of an eye.

#2886. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney
