#2884. Sullivan: What the 2026 midterms could mean for energy investors

Don't expect huge near-term moves in energy stocks off the results of the midterms. But there are some investing implications over the longer-term. Let me break it down.

Yes, it's entirely possible that one or both houses of Congress will shift to Democratic control. That's not my opinion. It's what many of the research reports, polls, and prediction markets are suggesting as of this writing.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm elections

For argument's sake, let's say that this time the polls are correct. The election ends with a "blue trickle," and Democrats win out with a slight edge in the House, Senate, or both. It won't be enough to meaningfully change, well, anything. If the election ends with just a slight lean to the Democratic side, it won't be enough for a veto-proof majority. Candidates can specify policies — like halting the growth of data centers — all they want, but there's a chasm between being elected and passing new laws.

Research I've read and conversations we've had on CNBC suggest that the next two years in Washington, D.C., will be flush with finger-pointing, investigations into the president and his family, and posturing ahead of the far-more-important 2028 presidential election.

S&P Global doesn't exactly agree with me, saying that Democratic control of either chamber would bring "tougher oversight of agency decisions" and could impact things such as tax incentives and "other policies underpinning long-term energy investments."

A Democratic Congress could be good for offshore wind, an energy source the president seems to hate with the white-hot passion of the sun. Speaking of the sun, a shift blue could also be positive for the continued growth of solar, perhaps via new tax incentives. Though both are growing rapidly.

If the Republicans maintain control over one or both houses, we keep doing more of the same, albeit with more urgency. S&P Global adds that "a continued Republican majority would give the administration more room to pursue expanded domestic production, faster permitting and environmental deregulation, though narrow margins could limit major legislation."

Any change in control of Congress is also unlikely to make a big difference around the Iran conflict. Once any new Congress begins in January, things will have either ramped up, stayed the same, or cooled down. A new Congress could try to use its authority under the War Powers Act. The team at Piper Sandler agrees, writing that "elections matter, but will likely not change the direction of things" as Trump focuses more on his own legacy. That's a polite way of saying that the President is going to do largely what he wants, Congress be darned.

On the state level, the midterms may matter a little bit more, particularly as they relate to energy-hungry data centers. In Texas, a change in the statehouse could bring a change in sentiment around data centers. Candidate Gina Hinojosa has a policy to "end data center madness" and supports a moratorium on new builds. Amy Acton also supports a "conditional moratorium" on new data center builds in Ohio. Vivek Ramaswamy is also calling for conditions on new data center construction.

The issue is a big deal in many states. Yet even if those candidates, or candidates with similar views on big new energy projects, win, they'd still have to enact meaningful change. Which could happen, of course. So let's say it does happen. New governors enter office and use their power to throttle or even stop new projects. Even if some of the big new projects are stalled or stopped, power demand, at a minimum, would stay at current levels. Which means demand for power production inputs such as natural gas, nuclear, and yes, even coal, would remain at current levels.

As far as power-hungry utility stocks go, Bank of America agrees with my election take above - mostly. Its team writes that it thinks the "2026 cycle matters for utility equities in far fewer states than the headline count suggests," but that committee appointments in each state will matter. The BofA team says that, regarding the election and utility stocks, five states matter most: Georgia, Ohio, Oregon, Pennsylvania, and Texas.

Texas is the "most consequential," as state environmental regulators have halted data center permits. That state's Commission on Environmental Quality (TCEQ) has actually stopped electronic permitting to make sure any permit is unrelated to data centers. To my point, that's already known information. It matters, but markets are already aware of it and have priced it in. Regardless, if you are investing in anything related to data centers, it wouldn't hurt to pay attention to Texas' election most of all.

There's a lot here. So take a breath, step back, and consider where we are right now.

The Strategic Petroleum Reserve is at a 40-year low.

We have two wars in some of the most important energy regions in the world.

The backlog for data centers - and thus the power needed to run them - is years long. Even if a number of them end up canceled, others will be built.

These days, it's hard to be certain of anything. But I am certain that overall energy demand isn't going down in America anytime soon. The only way to go is up. It's just a question of how much farther up.

That's my opinion. It could be wrong. And since I love hearing from you, I put out a poll on both X and LinkedIn. The results suggest you disagree with me.

Note → I am fully aware that the results of any poll on X or LinkedIn will be skewed by my type and makeup of followers on each platform - who lean heavily into both business, money and energy.

So if the elections won't matter in the near term for energy, what does?

Glad you asked.  Here's my ranking of what will matter for energy in the next year:

(Disagree?  Hit me up on X or LinkedIn and let me know why)

An end to either the Iran conflict or Russia's war on Ukraine would be massively positive for the world and send oil, natural gas, and refined products lower relatively quickly. Let's all hope for peace.

Lower interest rates would help many of the beaten-up stocks that rely on debt and consumer lending to prosper. Think residential solar panels.

The macro economy is going to do what it will, regardless of the U.S. elections, and American tax policy is largely unchangeable for the next couple of years.

Permitting reform is, of course, one factor that could be impacted by the election outcome, though it's unlikely to happen in the near term.

No, my friends, whatever the outcome of America's elections, it's safe to say the real political attention will be on Trump, more Trump, Trump's family, even more Trump, and then looking ahead to 2028's presidential vote.

If we could harness political infighting into some sort of power source, it may be better than nuclear!

I'm leaving for Europe this week to continue our five-year coverage of Europe's slow-rolling energy crisis. And while the word "crisis" is way overused in the media, I don't think it is here. Natural gas import prices are soaring. Storage levels are below recent averages. Interest rates are rising, and the public is already rage-filled about higher energy prices. It's a toxic combo. And I'll be in Europe later this week to talk about it.

First, from a macro markets perspective, I remain wary of tech and the stock market right now. As I posted on October 14, the markets "feel fragile."

Since writing that, the Nasdaq is down over 5%. A.I. isn't in a bubble, but not all companies are going to prosper. Some may go away. Watch the debt markets, particularly some A.I.-related high-yield (aka "junk") bonds. The State Street High Yield (JNK) ETF is prominently on my screens these days. Get to know the term "leveraged ETF," because those are in focus these days. The selling in many of these products can lead to selling in other products and stocks. If we get a slowdown in selling in those, some of the big tech stocks that have been selling off might stabilize.

Turning more specifically to why you are here: energy.

Earnings are (almost) behind us. ExxonMobil (XOM) and Chevron (CVX) posted big numbers last week. Marathon Petroleum (MPC) just reported, and those numbers were even bigger. The company earned $26.59 per share on $11.7 billion in EBITDA. That compares to EPS of $3.01 on earnings of $3.2 billion EBITDA one year ago.

ConocoPhillips (COP) caps things off later this week.

What now is that the world waits, watches, and focuses on Iran, the Strait of Hormuz, and Russia. What happens with those will determine the path of prices for oil and much more. Peace, and prices go down. New attacks, prices go higher. Higher prices mean many big-cap oil and gas stocks keep going higher. If Russia continues its brutal war against Ukraine, diesel fuel and jet fuel prices are going to remain volatile.

My take → Keep an eye on any headlines related to Russia selling diesel fuel to the United States. There is one ship on the way (see the RBI below), and let's see if more follow.

The continued risk around Iran and Russia pushed Goldman Sachs to raise its oil price forecast for the rest of this year. The firm is taking Brent crude to $105 and WTI crude to $94, and raising its average targets for next year to $88 for Brent (from $80) and $78 for WTI (from $73). Let's see if others follow.

While earnings have U.S. analysts swamped, Goldman's European team isn't too busy to update its "European Conviction List" of stocks. Some of the names it likes most are energy-related, including German power producer RWE (RWE-DE) and the better-known Siemens Energy (ENR-DE).

Concerns about AI, power, and rising interest rates have recently hit both stocks. RWE is down 4% this quarter, with Siemens Energy falling over 10%. Goldman remains bullish. The firm has a €212 price target on Siemens Energy and a €75 target on power producer RWE. That implies about a 30% return on RWE and a huge 70% upside on Siemens Energy.

Europe may have a world of energy problems, but that doesn't mean some of its stocks can't make you money. For more, tune in to my live CNBC coverage from Italy on Thursday, November 5.

We recently spoke with Toby Rice, CEO of natural gas giant EQT (EQT). His company just made a massive deal to buy Range Resources (RRC). We spoke with Toby about the deal, energy markets, and the importance of U.S. gas to Europe.

Another important interview on energy and AI this week was with Brian Nowak of Morgan Stanley. Brian focuses on tech firms such as Amazon, but his comments about some of the AI power players - the names we mention above - are worth a listen.

This week's Inside Line is with Christoph Halser, Gas & LNG analyst at Rystad Energy.

Could the one ship carrying Russian diesel fuel bound for America… actually skip America? It might.

The vessel we've had a close eye on for a week is moving slowly in the Atlantic Ocean. So slowly that some are now wondering if the ship will actually make it to New York. Could the destination change mid-voyage? It happens with ships all the time. A new buyer steps in to buy the cargo, and the ship changes course.

It does make you wonder… what American company would buy a load of Russian diesel right now? Putting money into Putin's pocket is not the PR that any company I know would want, but stranger things have happened.

The ship is supposed to dock in New York on November 7. Following the course of this ship right now is definitely random, but interesting.

OpenAI deepens Abu Dhabi foothold as Sam Altman inks deals spanning energy, government and investment

World's energy supply buffers have been eroded, ADNOC CEO says (Reuters)

#2883. Charles Bassey calls out ‘fake toughness' after Warriors' fiery win over Suns

Charles Bassey calls out 'fake toughness' after Warriors' fiery win over Suns originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

Warriors center Charles Bassey would rather let his play do the talking.

After Golden State's 116-113 win over the Phoenix Suns on Monday night at Chase Center, during which Draymond Green was ejected following a fiery third-quarter sequence, Bassey had a chance to log more minutes and fill up the stat sheet in his veteran teammate's absence.

But as things became heated on the court, Bassey stayed within his game while Dillon Brooks and Co. unraveled, allowing the Warriors' to overcome a 17-point deficit in the comeback victory.

"To be honest, man, I play hard," Bassey told Bonta Hill, Festus Ezeli and Juan Toscano-Anderson on "Warriors Postgame Live" after the win. "You know, I'm always going to be there for my teammate. And of course, when stuff is going like that, you just still got to stay composed, man. Stay in the game, you know. Don't let all this stuff get you out of the game sometimes. It's fake toughness, and I just got to make sure I'm in the game. …

"It's all fake toughness, man. You're not doing that outside."

Green and Brooks got into it with the Warriors trailing by 10 in the third quarter, after the Warriors' longtime nemesis took exception to Green tossing his young teammate Khaman Maluach to the ground beneath the rim.

Brooks ran over to Green and the two grabbed each other by the jerseys, resulting in double technical fouls — Green's second of the game after receiving one for the shove moments before, resulting in his ejection.

Green getting tossed lit a fire under the Warriors, and Bassey stepped up in his teammate's absence. The backup center scored 10 points and grabbed six rebounds after Green left the game, and finished with a career-high 19 points and eight rebounds over 23 minutes.

"It's the next-man-up mentality," Bassey told Hill, Ezeli and Toscano-Anderson. "As soon as Dray got the technical foul, got ejected, it's next-man-up mentality. I just had to step up on my team."

It remains to be seen if Green will face any further discipline following his ejection, but the Warriors know they'll see Brooks and plenty of "fake toughness" again as the 2026-27 NBA season progresses.

And if one thing's for sure, it's that Bassey will be ready.

#2880. AI-generated 'slopaganda' in political ads puts internet platforms on alert

The midterm election cycle has been dominated by "slopaganda," or campaign ads featuring AI-generated clips and images, creating another challenge for Instagram, YouTube and X as they attempt to monitor their platforms.

The term slopaganda dates back to early last year, when a group of experts on artificial intelligence used it in a paper to describe a new phenomenon combining generative AI slop with propaganda and misinformation.

There's no comprehensive federal law governing the use or disclosure of AI in political ads. And while there are some 30 state laws with different requirements about AI use and disclosure, candidates don't always abide by them. On the leading internet sites run by Meta, Google and Elon Musk's SpaceX, users run into AI-generated ads and AI-generated posts shared by politicians, alongside authentic videos and clips.

More than $187 million has been spent on nearly 300 ads generated or enhanced by AI, according to an analysis released last week by the Wesleyan Media Project. That's almost certainly an undercount, the organization says, because so many ads don't contain disclaimers and because determining AI use is difficult in some instances. Over 90% of the spending came from Republican candidates, a Republican party committee or a Republican-leaning group, the report found, and most don't include disclosures.

CNBC found a number of AI-generated ads running online without disclosures, including a fake video of New York Democratic Gov. Kathy Hochul that was posted by her opponent, Republican challenger Bruce Blakeman.

In Texas, Republican senatorial candidate Ken Paxton created an AI-generated video showing Democratic opponent James Talarico making controversial statements, with a three-second disclosure flashing in the middle. It's being shared as organic content on YouTube and X by Paxton's campaign. "Organic content" refers to free, unpaid posts shared on a profile or channel.

The Blakeman and Paxton campaigns didn't immediately respond to requests for comment.

YouTube told CNBC that the company applies "prominent labels to realistic AI content."

"For organic content on YouTube, political candidates can request removal where their face appears to be altered or generated by AI," the company said. "For advertisers, we require disclosure when an election ad contains synthetic or digitally altered people or events to provide viewers with transparency."

YouTube parent Google also requires political ads to complete its election ads verification process and include "paid for by" disclosures.

X says advertisers must comply with applicable laws, putting the responsibility on the advertiser, not the platform. The company didn't respond to CNBC's request for comment.

Meta referred to its guidelines on political ads, including restrictions on their use in the week prior to the election. The company also mandates "paid for by" labels and maintains an ad library of political ads. In June, the company ramped up its labeling, adding a new "about this ad" tool for ads that Meta detects have been created with third-party AI tools.

One risk the tech platforms face with generative AI is being asked to create misleading images and videos. The Brennan Center for Justice tested ChatGPT, Gemini, Grok, Claude, Perplexity and DeepSeek for their willingness to facilitate deceptive content. The study from August found that many models may avoid generating content around familiar conspiracies, but can generally be manipulated into creating misleading material.

A retest of those platforms in October found that ChatGPT had made progress. It had started redirecting prompts for harmful election images into illustrations so they look like comics, or refusing to take action on them altogether.

A number of national politicians are trying to address the issue.

The Protect Elections from Deceptive AI Act, backed by senators from both parties, would prohibit knowingly distributing materially deceptive AI-generated video or audio about federal candidates. Other bills are being led by Democrats in Congress, including the AI Ads Act, which is looking to expand current bans on campaign misrepresentation to include generative AI content.

None of the proposed bills have advanced out of committee.

#2879. Coinbase net income surges 66% in third quarter, benefiting from bitcoin investment gains

Coinbase Global earned third-quarter adjusted net income of $719 million, up 66% from $433 million a year ago, largely due to a recovery in the cryptocurrency market.

Coinbase said it earned $2.51 a share in the latest quarter, although it wasn't immediately clear if that compared with the 6-cent-per-share profit that Wall Street analysts had estimated. Revenue of $1.20 billion trailed the $1.233 billion expected by analysts polled by LSEG.

The third quarter value of Coinbase's crypto investment portfolio climbed due to a resurgence in the digital asset market. Coinbase also expanded its prediction market offerings, making a wider range of event contracts available to users.

In the third quarter, prediction markets revenue grew to more than $200 million on an annual basis, while the number of traders on those markets increased 60% compared with the second quarter.

#2878. Meta seeks to raise up to $25 billion in debt sale as AI spending jumps

Meta is seeking to raise between $20 billion and $25 billion in a bond sale on Tuesday as the company ramps up its spending on artificial intelligence infrastructure along with AI models and services.

The debt sale is expected to be two-thirds in U.S. dollars and one-third in euros, according to people familiar with the matter who asked not to be named because the details are private. Final pricing for the bond sale should come Tuesday afternoon, the sources said.

Meta declined to comment. Bloomberg News first reported on Meta's plans.

Meta's bond sale comes days after S&P Global Ratings revised the company's outlook from AA- stable to AA- negative, citing "uncertainty" about its hefty capex spending and negative free cash flow.

In its third-quarter earnings report last month, the Facebook parent revealed that its full-year capex will come in between $135 billion to $145 billion, representing a $5 billion increase to the low end of its previous guidance. Meta posted negative free cash flow of $2.12 billion for its latest quarter, joining Alphabet and Amazon in reporting a negative figure.

Meta previously raised $25 billion in an April bond sale, and said last week that it plans to raise $95 billion in an effort to fund its AI initiatives. Of that, Meta intends to first raise $35 billion in underwritten public equity offerings, and an additional $60 billion through an at-the-market program.

Meta's stock rose 2.7% on Tuesday after dropping in 12 of the prior 14 trading sessions and closing out October with its worst month in four years.

#2877. Ohio State football moving forward with Luke Fahey as starting quarterback

Ohio State coach Ryan Day wasn't coy about the Buckeyes' plans at quarterback this week.

Day confirmed freshman Luke Fahey will make a second straight start behind center when the Buckeyes host Oregon on Nov. 7.

While a loss at USC on Oct. 31 marked Fahey's starting debut in place of Julian Sayin, who remains sidelined indefinitely with an injury, Day expressed confidence in Fahey moving forward.

"I thought you saw him make some plays," Day said. "He kept playing all the way to the end. I thought his attitude was good on the sideline, even though we couldn't quite get in some of the red zone opportunities. There's things to build on for sure."

Fahey had been the Buckeyes' backup quarterback since the start of the season, but held off sophomore Tavien St. Clair as a rare midseason competition unfolded following Sayin's injury. Day kept their plans to start Fahey against the Trojans under wraps for most of last week.

As Day reflected further on Fahey's first start, he continued to praise the intangibles that were on display at the Los Angeles Coliseum.

"He had command of the huddle," Day said. "He showed some grit out there and toughness."

"I go to the players," Day said, "the offensive line, the wide receivers, and you say, 'What's it like in there?' And they were very positive about his approach out there."

Day also pointed to Fahey's ability to extend plays. He twice scrambled to avoid the rush and gained 17 yards between the runs.

Fahey finished 21 of 38 for 260 yards and one touchdown without an interception.

"It was not all perfect," Day said. "There were certainly things that happened operationally that, for a young quarterback, it was a lot on him, and he was working through it. There were some misthrows. But we expected all that to happen."

A majority of the missed throws were on deep balls. Fahey completed only two of 11 passes that traveled at least 20 yards through the air, according to Pro Football Focus.

The most noticeable miss was an overthrown pass to wide receiver Devin McCuin in the second quarter. Instead of connecting with McCuin in the end zone for a 43-yard touchdown as he blew past the coverage, the ball sailed out of bounds.

Fahey went deep to star receiver Jeremiah Smith three times, but they connected only once for 27 yards. Each of Smith's previous games with double-digit catches this season against Illinois and Iowa saw him finish with more than 200 receiving yards, but he had just 83 yards with his 10 catches last week.

The missed shots left Fahey with an average of 6.8 yards per attempt against the Trojans. It was a drop-off from the 10.5 yards per attempt that Sayin had averaged during the first seven games, though not uncharacteristic for a first-time starting quarterback.

When Sayin made his debut as a redshirt freshman last season against Texas, he averaged 6.3 yards per pass attempt and posted lower marks later in 2025 against Illinois (6.1) and UCLA (5.9).

But Day made it clear that the Buckeyes should have provided more support for Fahey.

Fahey faced pressure on 18 of 43 dropbacks (41.9%) at USC, the most since Ohio State's loss to Miami in the College Football Playoff quarterfinals last season when Sayin was under pressure on 42.5% of his dropbacks, per PFF.

The Buckeyes also dropped three of Fahey's passes after registering a total of just six drops over the first seven games.

It was the first time since Sayin made his first career start last year that they registered multiple drops in a game.

"We got to do a better job around him," Day said, "helping him out as a young player."

Joey Kaufman covers Ohio State football for The Columbus Dispatch. Email him at jkaufman@dispatch.com and follow him on @joeyrkaufman on X.

#2874. $6.3 billion Binance hack is largest crypto theft on record

Binance said Friday that a security incident impacted about $6.3 billion in client assets, with around $3 billion frozen on-chain, marking the largest crypto hack on record.

The company later added it began recovering assets impacted by the breach after pledging to cover losses using its Secure Asset Fund for Users, which the company created in 2018 as a protection for clients in the event of a hack.

It wasn't clear how the attack took place. The world's largest crypto exchange initially said the hack may involve "a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop, which allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions." However, Co-CEO Richard Teng later said the attack involved a more complex process rather than the misuse of a single engineer's permissions.

The FBI said Friday it was aware of the incident and was in touch with "relevant public and private partners to understand the scope of the activity, evaluate ​potential impacts, ​and ⁠coordinate appropriate investigative steps."

It wasn't clear either who was responsible for the breach. But TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence and AI investigations company, pointed to North Korean hackers as the potential culprits. "Several features of this attack match earlier North Korea-linked heists, including the Bybit hack and the KelpDAO hack in April 2026: small Tornado Cash withdrawals funding an operator wallet, staging that began well before the theft, and signatures obtained in advance then executed in one burst," the company said in a post.

Blockchain data platform Chainalysis also said this appears "be the work of North Korean cybercrime group TraderTraitor. This group has repeatedly targeted the cryptocurrency industry, exchanges, DeFi protocols, and investors, to raise funds for the regime. We base our intelligence assessment on on-chain activity observed in the hours following the hack."

Binance temporarily suspended withdrawals while it overhauls its withdrawal security systems and has revoked the affected address authorizations, Teng said. The exchange is also working with law enforcement and blockchain intelligence firms to trace and recover the stolen funds, while deposits remain unaffected.

Binance co-founder Yi He said deposits remained unaffected and continued to operate normally, while the exchange was working on enhancements to its withdrawal service and related address controls. It expects to begin resuming withdrawal services within the next 48 hours.

"We have found no evidence of any wallet private key leak. We will share a detailed technical post-mortem once the investigation is complete and the facts are fully verified," Binance said.

The announcement followed moves by several crypto firms, including Circle Internet Group, Tether and Ripple, to freeze funds tied to the hack.

Exploits are not uncommon in the crypto space. In 2025, Bybit suffered roughly $1.5 billion in losses due to a hack. More recently, Bitget lost more than $350 million worth of digital assets in a cyber theft incident. Over the past several years, bad actors have also drained billions of dollars in crypto from decentralized exchanges.

This week's breach "proves our exact point in proposing regulations for crypto markets earlier this month," Michael Selig, chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, said Friday in a CNBC "Squawk on the Street" interview. The U.S. derivatives regulator on Oct. 5 proposed a framework that would effectively put crypto exchanges under federal government oversight — after Congress failed to approve legislation to regulate the asset.

"The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don't have the same sorts of safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States. We want to offer a federal option to these exchanges to come and register with the CFTC, and they would be subject to the same sorts of cyber security safeguards that we have in our markets today," he said.

Bitcoin fell more than 4% to $77,187.60 on Friday as the hack weighed on crypto markets.

Circle CEO Jeremy Allaire said Thursday evening in a social media post that the company had frozen more than 800 million in USD Coin associated with the incident. USDT stablecoin issuer Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino also said in on X that his firm had frozen more than 1.5 billion of its stablecoin that may have been related to a potential breach.

Jack McDonald, Ripple's senior vice president of stablecoins, said it was actively freezing affected funds held in RLUSD, its U.S. dollar-backed stablecoin.

#2873. The next wave of luxury real estate: marina developments with yacht parking

Marinas are becoming the next big draw for luxury real estate, as the global wealthy search for homes to park their ever-larger yachts, according to developers.

With the number of yachts surging since Covid-19, real estate developers and investors are rushing to fill the shortage of boat slips and marina communities. There are now over 6,000 superyachts over 30 meters (98 feet) around the world, and another 200 delivered every year, according to SuperYacht Times.

The growth in the size and number of yachts has created a shortage of yacht slips. And investors are rushing to fill demand. Last year, Blackstone paid $5.6 billion for Safe Harbor Marinas, which then paid $1.5 billion in August for 65 marinas and storage sites purchased from MarineMax. European private equity firms are also on a marina buying spree to fill the shortage in the Mediterranean.

Real estate developers now see marinas as a powerful magnet for ultra-wealthy buyers, especially in South Florida. Billionaire Ken Griffin is building a megayacht marina in Miami with eight megayacht slips, including one for his 308-foot yacht.

The largest and most ambitious is Related Group's $2 billion new marina development in Fort Lauderdale. Slated to break ground next year and open in 2030, the project will include a St. Regis Hotel, condos, a beach club and slips for yachts up to 350 feet long.

"Here in South Florida is there's been a scarcity of yachting destinations and marinas and slips," said Related Group's Nick Pérez. "So when you're able to combine irreplaceable real estate and a marina, you just have a winning formula."

The 40-acre development will include a St. Regis hotel with 122 rooms, a 78-residence condo tower, 106 resort residences, as well as a deepwater marina that can hold 250 boats. It will also include a sports and wellness club with a padel and tennis court, yoga lawn, fitness center and pools. And it will have its own beach club.

Fort Lauderdale has long been the yachting capital of the U.S., with a vast infrastructure of yacht repair and renovation companies, along with crew, brokers, charter experts and shipbuilder offices. An estimated 2,000 superyachts pass through Fort Lauderdale each year, according to tourism officials. Many yacht owners are looking for real estate not only for themselves but also their crew.

Pérez said the new St. Regis development will not only appeal to the yachting community but also to a growing number of international buyers who are looking for a lower-cost alternative to Palm Beach and Miami. Several buyers from Mexico and Brazil have already purchased in the building.

He said up to 30% of the buyers at the project could be international.

"Fort Lauderdale is no longer T-shirt shops and flip-flops, it's now a luxury destination, with national retailers like Rolex, Patek, Cartier," Pérez said. "When you look at Miami and Palm Beach and the growth, now it's Fort Lauderdale's time to shine."

While boat slips are the main attraction, many buyers in Fort Lauderdale are pouring in from Southern California and the Northeast to take advantage of Florida's lower taxes.

"With the taxation situation in California, we're seeing a lot of interest from Newport and the Laguna area and San Diego," Pérez said.

The Inside Wealth newsletter by Robert Frank is your weekly guide to high-net-worth investors and the industries that serve them.

#2869. Which offseason narratives were wrong (and right) about the Sixers

It hasn't always been pretty, but nearly two weeks into the season, the Sixers have the best record in the Eastern Conference at 7-1. They've already toppled three other East title hopefuls in the New York Knicks (twice!), Cleveland Cavaliers and Indiana Pacers, although the Pacers were without both Tyrese Haliburton (hamstring) and Ivica Zubac (ankle), which takes some of the shine off that win.

There's still plenty of meat left on the bone for the Sixers, who are nowhere close to a finished product yet. But they easily could have been below .500 right now, which would have inevitably led to questions about Nick Nurse's job security and whether they would be the next would-be superteam that went up in smoke.

Given the scope of the Sixers' roster changes this past offseason, plenty of people had plenty of questions about them coming into the year. Some of them have proven valid. Others looked hilariously off base in real time and have proven to be exactly that. (The local media has a far better batting percentage than the national media in that regard.)

Let's go through which offseason Sixers takes are aging like spoiled milk and which are proving to be hauntingly prescient.

What do you get when you combine two former MVPs, four former All-NBA selections and a high-upside, second-year high-lottery pick in your starting lineup? Would they form a cohesive unit, or would they devolve into the "your-turn, my-turn" style of play that's common when stars link up?

The offense is still very much a work in progress, especially in the half court, but sharing the ball has not been a problem thus far. The Sixers are just outside of the top 10 leaguewide in total passes made per game, and all four of LeBron James, Joel Embiid, Tyrese Maxey and Jaylen Brown are averaging at least four assists per game.

As it turns out, putting together a team of extremely talented, high-IQ basketball players is actually a good idea. Who knew?

Given their collection of high-end talent, the Sixers figured to be elite offensively. One of the main questions about them coming into the season was what they'd look like on the other end of the floor.

Hilariously, they've been far better on defense than on offense thus far.

With the usual small-sample-size caveat at this time of year, the Sixers currently have the NBA's third-best defensive rating. When they aren't turning the ball over and allowing easy runouts, they've shown far more cohesion in half-court settings than expected this early in the year.

After the season-opening win over the Knicks, Embiid told reporters that he's hoping to earn an All-Defense nod this season, because playing defense at that level "changes everything" for our team. Brown said "defense is what we're going to have to continue to hang our hat on," and it's "non-negotiable."

The Sixers figured to have a high defensive ceiling in the playoffs once they were exerting maximum effort and had individually tailored, opponent-specific game plans. The regular season was the bigger question. So far, they're acing that test.

Joe Vardon of The Athletic asked that question to Brown on media day—and promptly got shut down—but he was hardly the only one who was curious about that. After all, the Sixers were importing two strong personalities into their core, both of whom have been the best player on their respective teams before.

After the first two weeks, there's little question who the Sixers' alpha is, though. It's Tyrese Maxey.

Because the Sixers' starting five is so stacked, the have the luxury of mismatch-hunting whenever one of their starters has a clear matchup advantage. But against the Pacers last Wednesday, the Sixers' entire late-game offense effectively boiled down to "Tyrese, make something happen." The same goes for their shorthanded win over the Charlotte Hornets on Halloween, when Maxey erupted for a season-high 38 points.

Back in training camp, Brown talked about how Maxey took it upon himself to welcome the newcomers and help them acclimate. He's clearly taken the leadership mantle upon himself both on and off the floor, and the rest of the Sixers appear content to let him roll with it.

With that said, should we start a nightly Alpha Tracker just in case?

Shout-out to Draymond Green for this galaxy-brained take.

"If I'm VJ Edgecombe's agent, I'm not sure I'm happy with the summer that the 76ers have had," Green said on his podcast this offseason. "If I'm just VJ Edgecombe's agent, because this could affect a lot for VJ Edgecombe. Like you go from being sometimes the second option when Embiid was out to sometimes being the fifth option. It's a big, big, big difference. I don't know if y'all understand how big of a difference that is."

Edgecombe's production has dipped relative to his rookie year—he's averaging only 12.7 points, 7.1 rebounds and 3.0 assists per game thus far—but that hardly means his development is being stunted by his new superstar teammates. Look no further than his 17-point, 13-rebound game against the Milwaukee Bucks or his 11-point, seven-rebound, six-assist outing against the Cavaliers.

Edgecombe might not put up monster stats this year, but his willingness to play whatever role the Sixers ask of him and play it well should only further endear him to his teammates and coaching staff. That's the type of player who you want—and should pay to have—in your locker room.

Even before Ariel Hukporti went down with a torn Achilles in the Sixers' preseason opener, backup center looked like a potential weak spot. Now, it's arguably their biggest issue, particularly given Embiid's injury history and ongoing load management.

Adem Bona has largely been invisible to start the year other than the one game that Embiid missed against Detroit. Tacko Fall (hip) and Dominick Barlow (back) have yet to play, while Jabari Walker (knee) missed two games. That's left them relying on LeBron and Dean Wade as small-ball 5s at times, which is not a sustainable solution.

As Maxey astutely noted after the Pistons loss, "It would be great to get some of our tall guys to come back." External reinforcements do not appear to be on the way, at least for another month, which means they'll have to look inside for ways to solve this problem for now.

The Sixers have largely been a dismal rebounding team under Nick Nurse. That didn't figure to change this year given their personnel, and through five games, those concerns have played out as expected.

While the small-sample-size caveat very much applies at this time of year, the Sixers are currently 24th in rebounding percentage. They were 22nd in 2023-24 and 21st last season, so their early-season output is right in line with how they've fared in recent seasons (at least ones when their entire roster doesn't get injured).

With Andre Drummond now in New York and Hukporti out for the year, the backup center situation plays into these struggles to some extent. However, this is a teamwide issue that they need to improve upon.

"We've got to be better at rebounding the basketball, and we have to do it by committee," head coach Nick Nurse recently told reporters. "I mean, I think if you're missing some of your size and all that kind of stuff, obviously it becomes a problem. And, all five guys have to rebound. I just look at the numbers there, and the defensive rebounding from just across the board, no matter the position, just hasn't been good enough.

"So, everybody's got to chip in with a few more. That has to be the answer."

Maxey is the only plus three-point shooter in the Sixers' starting lineup. They have Anfernee Simons and Kentavious Caldwell-Pope as snipers off their bench, but long-range shooting always figured to be an issue for this group. Lo and behold, the Sixers are 24th in three-point makes, 26th in three-point attempts and 18th in three-point percentage.

They're due for some positive regression to the mean in the coming weeks. Maxey is shooting only 33.9 percent from deep, which would be the third-worst mark of his career. He figures to bounce back up into the high 30s (if not the 40s) in due time. After all, Simons started 5-of-21 from distance over his first four games and then went on a three-games heater, going 16-of-27 from deep. He's now over 40% on the season on high volume.

With that said, Brown is shooting 41.9 percent from deep, which would be his career high if he keeps shooting that well across this entire season. In other words, he's due for negative regression moving forward.

The bigger-picture bottom line: Unless Embiid and/or Edgecombe become more reliable from three, this issue might plague their starting five all year.

Given their compilation of talent, the Sixers figure to have one of the league's most lethal offenses this year. However, the players have spent the past month warning that it'll be a work in progress early in the season given how many new pieces they're incorporating.

As it turns out, they weren't kidding. The Sixers are 19th in overall offensive rating and 20th in half-court offense.

That's bound to normalize as the starters develop more chemistry with one another. It's hard to imagine this group finishing in the bottom 10 of the NBA in half-court offensive efficiency. Until then, they at least need to cut down on their turnovers to avoid generating run-out opportunities for opponents.

Unless otherwise noted, all stats via NBA.com, PBPStats, Cleaning the Glass or Basketball Reference. All salary information via Spotrac and salary-cap information via RealGM.

#2866. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."

#2865. CrowdStrike launches AI kill switch that it says can stop internal agents that go rogue

CrowdStrike is betting that it has a solution to rogue artificial intelligence.

The cybersecurity vendor on Tuesday launched an AI kill switch designed to let security teams stop internal AI agents that go rogue, block them from relaunching and cut off their enterprise network access. The tool is built into CrowdStrike's Falcon Guardian platform, launched in September to secure AI agents. CrowdStrike also announced an integration with Anthropic's Claude Cowork.

"Having the ability to control and turn off, and make a decision and decide, I think that's really important for this next chapter of AI adoption," Chief Business Officer Daniel Bernard told CNBC.

A string of recent high-profile cyber incidents has added urgency to calls for some kind of emergency shutdown mechanism.

The Hugging Face breach orchestrated by rogue OpenAI agents in July was particularly alarming for businesses. Last week, Binance disclosed the largest crypto cyberattack on record and hackers have recently targeted hundreds of refrigerators and freezers at supermarkets in France.

Over the summer, U.S. representatives introduced bipartisan legislation that would require AI companies to build shutdown capabilities into their models.

Critics argue, however, that an emergency stop button is far too complicated to implement across multiple systems, data centers and complex AI models.

Bernard said the kill switch operates like a circuit breaker in systems where customers use its Falcon platform. The process can detect internal agent activity but requires a human to decide to cut the agent off and reinstate access.

"If you kill a process at the root, and we have that level of access at the root … it's very binary," Bernard said. "You either kill the AI and the agent, or you don't, and then you report on that."

The kill switch doesn't delete the agent's code but uses connected AI gateways to cut off access to the AI models that influence decisions, CrowdStrike said.

In the age of AI, businesses are moving quickly to adopt autonomous tools, often without the necessary safeguards and protections.

CrowdStrike believes its new tool will let companies stop AI agent 'misbehavior in its tracks, while leaving room for innovation.

The company also announced plans to acquire AI observability startup Fiddler AI and AI security testing firm Dreadnode.

CrowdStrike and its rivals are rapidly scooping up cybersecurity startups to keep up with increasing security demands. The need for AI security tools has also powered a broader wave of cybersecurity deal-making.

SAN FRANCISCO — The first time Draymond Green and longtime nemesis Dillon Brooks shared the floor this season, only one of them was still left by the end.

In that battle, consider Brooks the winner.

In the one that actually matters in terms of wins and losses, point to Green and the Warriors.

Green's ejection 2 minutes into the second half Monday night awakened the sleep-walking Warriors, who erased a 17-point deficit in his absence to pull out a 116-113 win.

"He lit a fire in all of us," said Stephen Curry, "and we had to respond. So I'm glad we did."

Golden State Warriors' Draymond Green (23) and Phoenix Suns' Jalen Bridges (15) are separated by security in San Francisco on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. JOSE CARLOS FAJARDO/Bay Area News Group via AP

While the double technical ended Green's night and put him two closer to the 16-tech threshold that automatically merits a one-game suspension, the Warriors had no problem with it.

Golden State Warriors' Draymond Green throws down Phoenix Suns' Khaman Maluach during an NBA game at Chase Center in San Francisco, Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. JOSE CARLOS FAJARDO/Bay Area News Group via AP

Upon review, the incident resulted in technical fouls for Green and Brooks, and a second on Green for his "throwdown" of Maluach. That earned Green an early trip to the showers.

The Suns responded to Green's absence by going small, subbing out Oso Ighodaro with 6-foot-7 forward Ryan Dunn at center. Coach Steve Kerr didn't care to weigh in on Green's testy back-and-forth with Brooks but said the Suns' adjustment "opened up the game."

"I think they played maybe 12 minutes of super small with [6-foot-7 forward Ryan] Dunn at the five," Kerr said. "It kind of opened up the game a little bit. Our crowd got into it and our guys just did a great job of finding energy on a back-to-back."

Stephen Curry (30) and Draymond Green (23) of the Golden State Warriors smile on the bench against the Denver Nuggets on Monday, Oct. 26, 2026, in Denver. NBAE via Getty Images

While the Warriors were on the back end of their second back-to-back of the season, the Suns were well-rested having played Friday. When they squared off Monday night, it was the Warriors' eighth game of the season to only the Suns' fifth.

So even though Green and Curry sat out against the Clippers the night before, it was understandable for the Warriors to come out sluggish.

The fact that they were able to rally and finish stronger, well, maybe some credit belongs to the guy who spent most of the second half in the locker room.

"We've got to have our brother's back when he defends us," Curry said. "But it's also one of those games, most of the guys on the back to back, Phoenix had been off for a couple days, they started 1-3 and [we] knew they were gonna come out playing desperate. …

"We just tried to weather the storm, execute, give ourselves a chance, and that third quarter was the run that we needed to get back into it."

#2863. AMD has been crushed into earnings. Here’s one way to trade it

Advanced Micro Devices has been under significant pressure leading up to earnings after the close. The stock has been caught in the swings of the AI debate all year, but this most recent leg lower was triggered by a wave of disappointing tech earnings.

A record drop in Alphabet last Thursday was followed by weak results from Amazon, as well as AMD's long-standing CPU rival, Intel. Intel guided fourth-quarter revenue well below consensus and expects the PC slowdown to last through at least the first half of next year. That sent Intel's stock down 29% on Friday, its worst day since the 1970s. Caught in the fallout of Intel and the broader tech sector, AMD fell 10.9% that day and slid another 7.5% Monday.

For the past week, AMD has traded on other companies' news. Now its own numbers get a vote.  AMD's stock reactions have been mixed in recent quarters, sensitive to guidance and high investor expectations. It fell 17% after Q4 2025 results, when a beat and a guide above estimates still underwhelmed some analysts. It rose 18.6% after Q1 2026 results, when it guided about 6.5% above expectations. It fell 7% after Q2 2026 results, when a guide above consensus still fell short of the $14 billion some analysts wanted. That may make direction hard to call, but options let investors build a trade that doesn't have to pick a side.

Based on Monday's close, I'm selling the November 4 425/415 put spread and the 510/520 call spread for a credit of roughly $3.65.

AMD's 30-day implied volatility is elevated going into earnings, closing Monday higher than on about 81% of days over the past year. This means sellers are collecting richer premium than usual. To capture that premium while limiting time beyond the earnings announcement, the trade uses the November 4 weeklies. These contracts imply a move of about 9% through Wednesday, in line with AMD's 8.4% average close-to-close move over its last 10 reports. The short strikes sit at the edges of the expected move, with long strikes $10 away to limit capital at risk going into earnings. This isn't a bet that options are mispricing the move or a directional call. It's a neutral play that aims to profit from the elevated premium that tends to come out after the print.

The trade reaches its maximum profit of $365 if AMD closes between $425 and $510 on Wednesday. Breakevens are $421.35 and $513.65, about 9.7% below and 10.0% above Monday's close, just outside the expected move. The maximum loss of $635 comes if AMD closes below $415 or above $520, roughly 11% in either direction. That has happened in 11 of its last 40 reports, and AMD has had moves as large as 19% in its last four, emphasizing the importance of capping the risk with the long wings.

If AMD finishes between $415 and $425, the short put can be assigned, creating an obligation to buy 100 shares per spread at $425. After the credit, the cost basis is $421.35, or roughly 35% below the Oct. 6 closing high. For investors who like AMD's long-term story, that's a way to end up owning the stock at a discount. A finish between $510 and $520 can assign the short call and leave a short stock position. For investors looking to avoid either risk, consider managing the position before Wednesday's close.

#2861. Binance co-CEO Richard Teng says hackers used fake job interviews, AI tool to steal $6.3 billion in crypto

Binance said hackers used fake job interviews, malicious software and compromised internal AI tools to infiltrate its wallet infrastructure, leading to the record-breaking Oct. 29 cyberattack that resulted in more than $6 billion in assets being stolen from the world's largest crypto exchange.

The attackers "spent months building their way in," Binance co-CEO Richard Teng told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Tuesday, rejecting suggestions that there was a single point of failure through one employee's laptop.

"In June, [the attackers] set up a fake version of a developer tool and lured one of our engineers to install that," he added. "In September they posed as recruiters and ran a series of job interviews with another engineer, and that ended up in a coding exercise that contained hidden malware designed to evade security ... from there they moved violently through our internal AI tools and obtain[ed] access that breach[ed] the system that authorized our wallet transaction."

He also said Binance has replenished 98% of $6.34 billion in affected assets, and that it is still in the process of purchasing outstanding, more illiquid assets.

Teng himself did not attribute the attack to North Korea but acknowledged that the FBI has linked it to North Korean hackers tied to the TraderTraitor group.

Preparations for the attack began in June, when a Binance Wallet DevOps engineer downloaded a tampered Homebrew installation "to install a popular AI coding tool after searching for it on a search engine," according to a security report published by Binance Tuesday. It said it detected the suspicious activity, replaced the engineer's device and rotated credentials as a precaution, but that by then the employee's "role and his related colleagues on our wallet infrastructure team was exposed to the hacker."

As Binance works to recover from the incident, it has temporarily restricted certain withdrawals, pledged to cover all customer losses and tapped its own reserves to replenish affected assets. Binance and industry partners including Tether, Circle and Ripple have reportedly frozen about $3 billion in assets, though some stolen funds continue to move through laundered channels. Coinbase blocked transfers to compromised wallets and offered Binance direct assistance.

Although the incident represents a significant security breach, Binance maintains that it remains financially sound and focused on identifying the vulnerability, recovering remaining funds and preventing another attack.

The hack sparked a sharp but short-lived hit to confidence across crypto markets, especially compared with previous failures at major exchanges. Investors are now watching whether Binance can fully restore confidence in centralized exchanges.

As of Tuesday Binance has rebuilt the affected wallet system and internal AI tool, replaced compromised devices, decommissioned credentials and tokens associated with the affected engineers and expanded security scans across employee devices, Teng told "Squawk on the Street."

"We're also getting an independent expert to review everything that we have done, and we'll keep strengthening our defenses as the investigation continues," Teng said. "We're working around the clock with law enforcement agencies and with industry partners to trace and freeze the stolen funds for that front."

#2859. What the 2026 midterm elections could mean for Trump's final two years

Tuesday's midterm elections will at their heart determine control of Congress for the remainder of Donald Trump's presidency. U.S. voters will decide whether Trump will maintain his Republican iron grip on Washington or recede into lame-duck status after a Democratic triumph.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm elections

If Republicans win, Trump could spend another two years facing little congressional resistance and pushing through major policy packages. If Democrats win one or both chambers, the president's remaining legislative priorities would be severely kneecapped, and even the basic functions of government could become gridlocked.

Oddsmakers and public polls placed Democrats in a favorable position ahead of Election Day, especially for flipping control of the Republican-held House. Still, the balance of power will not be decided until ballots are counted — a process that could take days or, in some cases, weeks to complete. The Senate is expected to be particularly close, and if the balance of power comes down to Alaska, the nation may have to wait while the state counts.

"The election results could trigger an immediate market reaction, particularly as the Senate outcome becomes clearer," the Washington Policy Strategy Group of Stifel wrote Sunday in an analyst report, where it predicted Democrats will win the House while the Senate remains too close to call. "However, the market's knee-jerk trade could reverse as investors recognize that gridlock remains the most likely policy outcome in either case."

Republicans also control the Senate and with a larger majority than in the House.

If Democrats capture one chamber of Congress, Washington policymaking would become significantly more gridlocked. Single-party budget reconciliation bills, the procedural vehicle for most major spending and policy changes in the current status quo of tight congressional margins, would become impossible. Bipartisanship would be required to advance legislation, making must-pass bills such as government funding packages, defense spending and the looming debt ceiling hike more important — and more contentious.

In a divided Congress, the party that doesn't control the White House typically uses moments including government funding and debt-ceiling hikes to extract concessions from the majority party, a practice that can lead to brinksmanship.

"If Republicans defy the conventional wisdom and maintain their Senate majority, then we would expect a relief rally in the sectors that might react negatively to a Democratic victory," Stifel wrote. "Democrats would still govern the House and gridlock would prevail. In this scenario, Senate Republicans would act as a check on both the Democrats and many of Trump's populist impulses."

Democrats are also widely expected to use any majority in Congress to launch investigations into the White House and businesses that have worked with the Trump administration. The majority party gains subpoena power in congressional committees.

Polling indicates a Democratic sweep of the House and Senate is possible. In that case, Democrats may more aggressively pursue party-line reconciliation measures that wouldn't become law but would give the party a megaphone ahead of the 2028 presidential election. Trump would still have the ability to veto legislation that Congress passed, severely limiting Democratic bills' prospects of becoming law.

Must-pass legislation would be slightly easier to get through Congress if Democrats controlled both chambers. But deals would still need to be brokered with the White House, and the Senate's 60-vote filibuster threshold could still prove a hurdle that requires bipartisanship. Whichever party gains Senate control is highly unlikely to have at least 60 seats.

Democratic lawmakers in both the House and Senate would jump at the chance to lead investigations of the White House and business if given the opportunity.

In either a divided Congress or unified Democratic Congress, analysts expect Trump's use of executive orders will increase due to congressional gridlock.

There is a possibility Republicans maintain control of both chambers of Congress. In that case, Trump's remaining legislative agenda could continue to be enacted, and the status quo would be maintained.

Democrats would still hold some leverage, largely due to the Senate's 60-vote filibuster threshold, but would be limited in investigating the White House and business.

#2858. Giannis Antetokounmpo argues Luka Doncic may be the best scorer ‘to ever play this game’

Giannis Antetokounmpo had very high praise for Luka Doncic following the Lakers' 138-117 win over the Heat on Monday.

Doncic finished with 42 points (14-for-24 from the field, 6-for-15 from 3), seven rebounds, 13 assists and two steals, notching his fourth 40-plus point performance in the last five games as the Lakers improved to 6-2.

"Obviously he's one of the best scorers to ever play this game, if not the best," said Antetokounmpo, who had 30 points, 12 rebounds, seven assists, one steal and two blocked shots.

Luka Doncic (77) of the Los Angeles Lakers plays defense on Giannis Antetokounmpo (7) of the Miami Heat on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, at Crypto.com Arena in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

"Not right now," Antetokounmpo said. "Of all-time. I feel like some people are scared to say that. But it's got to be out there. He's also an elite passer."

Doncic is averaging a league-leading 38.3 points on 50% shooting from the field (43% from beyond the arc), eight rebounds and 8.1 assists this season. His 13 assists were a season high. In Monday's win, Dončić became the first Laker player to score at least 20 points in the first half in five straight games since Kobe Bryant in 2007.

Doncic is earning a lot of early MVP chatter. He has never finished in the top-two for the award despite leading the league in scoring twice (2023-24 and 2025-26) and carrying the Mavericks to the 2024 NBA Finals.

Luka Doncic of the Los Angeles Lakers prepares to shoot a free throw against the Miami Heat on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, at Crypto.com Arena in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

Los Angeles Lakers guard Luka Doncic (77) is defended by Miami Heat guards Pelle Larsson, left, and Ryan Conwell on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, in Los Angeles. AP Photo/Jayne Kamin-Oncea

When Antetokounmpo, a two-time MVP and one-time champion in 2021, was asked if Doncic should get more consideration for the award, he didn't hesitate.

"Is he an MVP-caliber player?" Antetokounmpo asked. "I think, no doubt. He's been up there. He's definitely up there this year. Like, what he's doing is incredible. And not only that, but his team is winning, and their offense is good, everybody is being involved, they're in a good place right now. So, yeah, a guy who's averaging 38 and his team is winning, he's definitely got to be in the MVP discussion."

#2857. Broncos’ Bo Nix Admits He Was ‘Scared to Death’ When Patrick Mahomes Got the Ball Back in Late Drive

The Denver Broncos secured a win over the Kansas City Chiefs, but Patrick Mahomes had one final chance to change the result. That late opportunity became the focus of a postgame exchange between Mahomes and Bo Nix.

Speaking to Mahomes after the game, Nix reflected on the Chiefs late fourth-quarter possession.

The Kansas City Chiefs entered the season as Super Bowl contenders, but back-to-back defeats have raised questions about their recent form. Following the Broncos' win over the Chiefs on November 1.

Nov 1, 2026; Denver, Colorado, USA; Denver Broncos quarterback Bo Nix (10) leaves the field after defeating the Kansas City Chiefs at Empower Field at Mile High. Mandatory Credit: Ron Chenoy-Imagn Images

During the postgame exchange, captured on microphone, Nix told Mahomes, "I've always admired you. I was scared to death when you got the ball back." He said Mahomes' late drive made him uneasy because of the quarterback's history of delivering in crucial moments.

The Broncos had taken a 28-24 lead with 1:56 remaining in the fourth quarter, leaving the Kansas City Chiefs with a final opportunity. Patrick Mahomes moved the offense into scoring position. Travis Kelce made a 32-yard reception during the drive, while Kenneth Walker III contributed with important runs as the Chiefs approached the Broncos goal line.

The Chiefs eventually reached Denver's 1-yard line. Mahomes appeared to complete a touchdown pass to Rashee Rice, but the play was called back because of a false start penalty on right tackle Jaylon Moore. Kansas City failed to score on its subsequent attempts. On fourth down, Malcolm Roach brought Mahomes down for a six-yard sack, ending the Chiefs' final possession.

Mahomes finished with 30 completions on 41 attempts for 321 passing yards and three touchdowns without an interception. He connected with Emmett Johnson for a 73-yard touchdown, while Travis Kelce finished with five receptions for 67 yards. Rice recorded seven catches for 53 yards and a touchdown. Walker added 69 rushing yards on 21 attempts and 15 receiving yards.

Nix completed 20 of 32 passes for 222 yards and two touchdowns without an interception. Jonah Coleman contributed 41 rushing yards and 63 receiving yards, including a 51-yard reception that set up Denver deep in Chiefs territory late in the fourth quarter. Coleman finished the drive with a five-yard rushing touchdown. Jaylen Waddle recorded three receptions for 39 yards and a touchdown, while Courtland Sutton added 53 receiving yards.

The Broncos had trailed 24-13 before scoring 15 unanswered points in the fourth quarter. Kansas City had taken a 17-13 halftime lead before Noah Gray's four-yard touchdown reception extended its advantage in the third period. Harrison Butker also made a 45-yard field goal in the second quarter but missed a 54-yard attempt late in the fourth.

Penalties proved costly for Kansas City, which was flagged 11 times for 105 yards. Denver committed six penalties for 45 yards.The false start that wiped out Rice's apparent touchdown proved costly during Kansas City's last drive. Both teams finished without an interception, while Kansas City recorded two fumbles and Denver had one, with neither side losing possession.

The Chiefs finished with 399 total net yards, including 309 passing yards and 90 rushing yards. Denver recorded 294 total net yards, with 217 passing yards and 77 rushing yards. Kansas City converted six of 14 third-down attempts, while Denver converted two of 11.

The win moved Denver to 6-2 and extended its winning streak to four games, putting the Broncos atop the AFC West. The Chiefs fell to 5-2 after its second consecutive loss. Nix's postgame remarks focused on Mahomes and Kansas City's final possession, which ended with Roach's fourth-down sack.

#2856. Strand bookstore to pay nearly $1.5 million over Covid loan tied to Sen. Wyden's wife

The Strand, the iconic New York City bookstore, agreed to pay nearly $1.5 million in a civil settlement for accepting a Covid government loan that was off-limits because the store is owned by the wife of Oregon Democratic Sen. Ron Wyden, according to the U.S. Attorney's Office in Washington, D.C.

The Strand applied for the $932,350 loan in February 2021 from the Paycheck Protection Program administered by the Small Business Administration, U.S. Attorney Jeanine Pirro's office said Tuesday.

Store owner Nancy Bass Wyden certified on the loan application that "the spouse of a Member of Congress does not directly or indirectly hold a controlling interest in the applicant," Pirro's office said.

"Ms. Wyden's certification was knowingly and materially false and Strand was not eligible for the second draw loan it received," the office said.

The Strand will pay $1,482,560 to settle the case. There was no determination of liability by the store, Pirro's office said.

The Strand, in a statement, said the store made an "unintentional" mistake in applying for the loan, and said it was unaware at the time the funds were barred to businesses owned by the spouse of a member of Congress.

Bass Wyden's husband, who has served in the Senate for more than 30 years, is a staunch critic of President Donald Trump, who appointed Pirro to the post of top prosecutor in the District of Columbia.

Bass Wyden is the granddaughter of Ben Bass, founder of the Strand, whose flagship store is in Greenwich Village.

Congress authorized the forgivable PPP loans when it passed the Coronavirus Aid, Relief, and Economic Security Act in March 2020.

In December 2020, Congress provided additional funding for the PPP loan program with separate legislation, which "included a prohibition against businesses obtaining second draw PPP funds if a member of Congress or their spouse directly or indirectly held a controlling interest in the PPP loan applicant," Pirro's office said.

In a statement Tuesday, Pirro said, "The Paycheck Protection Program was created to save American jobs, not to enrich those who aren't eligible for taxpayer-funded relief, especially the wife of a U.S. Senator."

Her office noted that the False Claims Act, which the Strand was alleged to have violated, "allows the government to establish liability based on reckless disregard and permits the government to recover more than its actual losses," which is what occurred in this case.

The civil settlement resolved claims brought under a so-called qui tam lawsuit, which allows private whistleblowers to sue on behalf of the U.S. government and receive a portion of any recovery.

The qui tam action in this case was filed by a corporate entity, Blockquote Inc., in U.S. District Court in Washington. Blockquote, whose owners were not disclosed by Pirro's office, will receive a total share of about $148,256 in connection with the settlement.

The docket for Blockquote's lawsuit is sealed from public view.

The Strand, in a statement after CNBC requested comment from the store and Bass Wyden, said the restriction that the store violated was passed by Congress between the first and second rounds of the PPP.

"Unfortunately, the bookstore's accountant and the bank administering the loan were not aware of this change when the second-draw application was processed," the Strand said.

"We stand by the facts: the circumstances arose during an unprecedented and rapidly changing public health emergency, the mistake was unintentional," the store said. "We hope this matter will be viewed objectively and in its proper context, with the understanding that the application of justice should always be fair, impartial, and free from political considerations," the store said.

Sen. Wyden's office did not immediately respond to requests for comment.
