#2855. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2854. The Point Guard Problem

"What If" Wolves Volume 6: "Khan, you bloodsucker!" – Captain James T. Kirk or JetAllen

When the Minnesota Timberwolves acquired LaMelo Ball this offseason, I found myself asking a simple question: did the Wolves finally solve their point guard problem? Early returns are optimistic at this point. Some good games, some not so. But based on history, what else should we expect from this franchise?

The Timberwolves have been through point guard after point guard. None of them seem to last long, if at all. This team has been going through PGs like Spinal Tap goes through drummers. RIP Peter James Bond.

The Timberwolves started off by taking what they thought was a franchise cornerstone in Pooh Richardson, their first-ever draft pick. We have previously discussed why that didn't work out in the first "What If" Wolves article. Since picking Pooh, the Wolves have struggled to find a franchise player at the one. There have been some solid players, even All-Stars, but they were always short stints. The point guard problem should have been solved by now, and hopefully it finally is with Ball, but repeated mistakes have left this position in a constant state of flux.

I had originally planned to write an article focusing only on the reign of terror brought to this franchise by former General Manager David Kahn. But my liquor cabinet was only half-stocked at the time, and I would need copious amounts of booze to relive that whole ordeal. Instead, we will focus on one issue. One teeny, tiny issue that I consider the biggest mistake in franchise history.

David Kahn was on the job for exactly 33 days (trust me, I counted) when he made quite possibly the greatest trade in Timberwolves history.

Kahn traded former Rookie of the Year Mike Miller and guard Randy Foye to the Washington Wizards for Etan Thomas, Darius Songaila, Oleksiy Pecherov, and the fifth pick in the upcoming 2009 NBA Draft. Only Pecherov would ever play for the Wolves (44 games), but the prize of the trade was the pick. With the addition of the fifth overall pick, MN would now draft back-to-back with the fifth and sixth picks.

With the fifth overall pick in the 2009 NBA Draft, the Timberwolves selected my (and Leo's) favorite player of all time, Ricky Rubio.

NEW YORK – JUNE 25: Ricky Rubio, selected number five overall by the Minnesota Timberwolves poses for a portrait during the 2009 NBA Draft on June 25, 2009 at the WaMu Theatre in New York, New York. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this photograph, User is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. Mandatory Copyright Notice: Copyright 2009 NBAE (Photo by Jennifer Pottheiser/NBAE via Getty Images) | NBAE via Getty Images

Rubio was a dynamic PG from Spain. His list of accomplishments overseas was incredible: he turned pro at 14 years old, won the Rising Star Award, FIBA Young Player of the Year Award, was named Spanish ACB League's best point guard, Defensive Player of the Year, and won a EuroLeague Championship. Quite the trophy room.

But I believe what put Ricky at the top of the draft class was his final performance of the 2006 Europe Under-16 Championship, where he scored 51 points, grabbed 24 rebounds, had 12 assists, and stole the ball 7 times. One of the greatest games ever played. Rubio also held his own in the 2008 Olympic Finals against the USA, where Ricky went up against the likes of Jason Kidd, Dwyane Wade, Chris Paul, and Kobe Bryant.

To me, Rubio was a lock with the 5th pick in the draft. But man, did Kahn ever mess up the next pick. This is where the "What If" Wolves will take over, fire Kahn immediately, and take the player that I was begging the Timberwolves to take.

I remember the draft like it was yesterday. I was sitting on the couch at my in-laws' house. My son was born a few months prior, so we were at the grandparents' house to get some baby relief. And I was yelling at the television.

David Kahn was on the job for exactly 34 days (trust me, I counted again) when he made the biggest mistake in Timberwolves history.

With Ricky Rubio in the fold, my thoughts were to get him a lights out shooter that can pair with him in the backcourt. Rubio had already proven that he could lock down defensively (yes, bigger guards would have been a problem, but only a few teams had elite big guards that operated out of the post), so the other guard could take on the smaller matchup. So who was the best shooter in the draft? Not Jonny Flynn, I can tell you that.

With the 6th overall pick in the 2009 NBA Draft, the "What If" Wolves selected Wardell Stephen Curry II from Davidson College.

NEW YORK – JUNE 25: Stephen Curry, selected seventh overall by the Golden State Warriors speaks to Mark Jones of ESPN during the 2009 NBA Draft on June 25, 2009 at The WaMu Theatre at Madison Square Garden in New York City, New York. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this photograph, User is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. Mandatory Copyright Notice: Copyright 2009 NBAE (Photo by David Dow/NBAE via Getty Images) | NBAE via Getty Images

Steph Curry was the best story coming out of the 2008 NCAA Tournament. Curry owned March Madness that year by beating larger schools Gonzaga (40 points), Georgetown (30 points), and Wisconsin (32 points) before eventually losing in the Elite Eight to Kansas (25 points). Curry would leave tiny Davidson College the following year after being named to the All-American First Team, leading the nation in scoring (28.6 ppg), and shooting 41% on three-pointers for his college career. In my eyes, Curry was the perfect complement to Ricky Rubio (Watch how effortless Curry's game is in the video below).

David Kahn compared Flynn and Rubio to Hall of Famers Earl Monroe and Walt Frazier of the New York Knicks. I can sort of see the familiarities with the flash of Rubio and Monroe along with the defense of Flynn and Frazier. But both Pearl and Clyde could score. Flynn couldn't shoot a lick, finishing his career shooting 33% from three and 40% overall from the field. Don't get me wrong, Rubio shot just as bad for his career, but he found a way to average double digits in scoring for his career, plus he was an elite set-up man.

According to NBC Sports' Kurt Helin, even the Wolves players and coaches were baffled by the draft. This is why Curry was the right pick from the get-go.

There were also some issues with good ol' dad when it came to drafting Steph Curry. Dell Curry was a great shooter with the Charlotte Hornets in the 90s (40% career three-point shooter) and taught his boys how to let it fly. He also was trying to force his eldest son to get drafted by the Knicks, who owned the 8th overall pick.

That's right. Dell didn't want his kid to go to the Golden State Warriors either. So why did they not listen to the father's threats? Well, they didn't have David Kahn as a GM, and since the "What If" Wolves fired Kahn, neither do we. We ignore Dell's wishes and take Curry right after Rubio.

David Kahn looking over his shoulder as he is about to be fired. | NBAE via Getty Images

Now, if this fantasy scenario plays out according to my plan, the Timberwolves don't have to wait two years for Rubio's arrival like in reality. Ricky comes over as an 18-year-old PG prodigy with impervious knees. Steph plays at the shooting guard position offensively. Being off-ball saves his ankles a bit, and his career trajectory starts on a more positive note. Rubio and Curry switch defensively, and both play the passing lanes for easy steals. Rubio now has a more open lane to finish at the rim due to the actual threat of a Curry three-pointer (Wolves shot 34% as a team in 2009-10).

The pace of play is quickened after great outlet passes from Kevin Love. And the "What If" Wolves become a playoff contender for the first time since Kevin Garnett was traded to Boston. Hell, KG might even come back earlier if he gets to play with Rubio and Curry. Rubio may still eventually be traded, or he retires to go back home to Spain, but Curry would then slide over to the point, and all would be right in the world. Curry would go on to possibly beat out KG as the best Timberwolf of all time. As a consolation, he would at least go down as the best point guard. Unless Melo gets us a chip.

Just for fun, here is a top ten list of the most games started at the point guard position for the Timberwolves.

Ricky Rubio – 384 games started (two separate stints)

Bonus: Jonny Flynn – 89 games started for the Timberwolves (only a three year NBA career)

Bonus Bonus: Stephen Curry – 1065 regular season games started for the Golden State Warriors, 149 playoff games started.

Bonus Bonus Bonus: Steph would've been too miserable to start any games with his prized golf courses covered in snow.

#2853. Chiefs Help Carry CBS to NFL Ratings Boost

The Chiefs remain very good for the business of the NFL.

CBS Sports said its late Sunday afternoon broadcast of Week 8's Chiefs-Broncos game averaged 28.18 million viewers, falling just shy of the most-watched broadcast of the 2026 NFL season as it reaches its midpoint.

The Kansas City–Denver broadcast fell narrowly below a Week 4 game between the Chiefs and Raiders that averaged 28.5 million viewers and remains the season's viewership leader. It did, however, become the most-watched Week 8 game on record for CBS.

Several factors helped boost the audience figure in the Chiefs-Broncos game. A resurgent Kansas City, winners of three Super Bowls and five conference titles since 2019, has long been one of the NFL's top viewership draws. The matchup for the top position in the AFC West went to the very end before Denver prevailed 28–24.

It also had a strong audience lead-in from 1 p.m. ET games, including a Ravens-Bills game. It was the first late Sunday afternoon national slot since the change to standard time—creating earlier sunsets until mid-March.

CBS Sports, the NFL's regular-season audience leader in 2025, is now averaging 19.56 million viewers so far this season, up 5% from this point a year ago. The network's 4:25 p.m. ET Sunday national window is averaging 25.3 million viewers and is the most-watched program of any type in U.S. television. CBS Sports also has the two most-watched games of the season, and three of the top five.

Through Week 7, the NFL is averaging 18.4 million viewers per game across all domestic rights holders, marking the league's highest average at that point since 2015. Updated figures incorporating Week 8 are expected on Thursday, and the NFL is aiming to improve upon a banner 2025 season that included its best viewership in 36 years.

#2852. Midterm elections could jolt stocks if Democrats sweep Congress

The stock market is not yet pricing in a Democratic sweep of Congress — despite some polls and most prediction markets pointing that way — so that scenario could weigh on share prices Wednesday, according to investors and analysts.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm elections

Republicans managing to keep control could cause a short-term relief rally, they said.

Short-term moves aside, the typical post-midterm pattern in the months ahead is bullish. But some wonder if the market could buck the positive historical trend this time because the S&P 500 is already up so much this year going into the election.

As far as certain stocks to watch, a big Democratic win on Tuesday night — especially in the Texas governor race — could add fuel to an artificial intelligence stock sell-off on the notion that more data center restrictions would gain momentum nationally. Defense stocks could come under pressure on fears of limits on spending.

Polls are pointing to a Democratic victory in the House and a toss-up in the Senate. Prediction markets have been more favorable to Democrats, calling for a blue sweep.

While investors are signaling "begrudging acceptance" to a base-case scenario that there will indeed be a Democratic sweep, that's not how they're positioning, said Tobin Marcus, head of U.S. policy and politics at Wolfe Research. The firm's recent survey data among more than 100 institutional investors suggests they are not strongly positioned for any particular result.

"Investors seem to have a significant dose of skepticism about whether the final outcome is going to be quite as favorable to Democrats as the polling suggests," Marcus told CNBC, noting that the firm shares the view that the "polling is likely overstating Democratic strength."

JPMorgan sees a blue wave knocking the S&P 500 by about a half percent on Wednesday.

On the other hand, if Republicans end up retaining both chambers of Congress, that could spur a rally, said Sameer Samana, head of global equities and real assets at the Wells Fargo Investment Institute.

"From a certainty standpoint, the market knows what to do with Republicans holding both chambers," he said.

Citigroup strategists said Monday that Republicans losing the House but managing to keep the Senate could be "a tailwind for equities into year-end." JPMorgan traders predict up to a 1% pop on Wednesday if Republicans hold on to that chamber.

Looking past any short-term move, stocks tend to rally after the midterms have concluded. In fact, the S&P 500 was up one year out after all midterm elections going back to the early 1980s.

But the S&P 500 has already recorded double-digit gains so far in 2026, so some on Wall Street worry that investors are being overly complacent heading into this year's elections.

During midterm election years, the S&P 500 has typically averaged a 14% drawdown at some point in the second half of the year found Jonathan Krinsky, chief market technician at BTIG.

"The reason stocks often perform well in November during midterms is because [the S&P 500] typically is weak INTO the elections. We have the opposite setup this year," Krinsky wrote last week. "Therefore, while consensus is that we see a post midterm rally, we think the opposite is actually more likely."

It's not helping that the broader market has recently come under significant pressure due to turbulence in the artificial intelligence trade, which led the Nasdaq Composite to post its worst day since June on Friday. AI shares led the market lower again on Monday.

A strong showing from Democrats in Tuesday's midterm elections could pressure the trade even more.

At the state level, increased Democratic control is probably a headwind for stocks associated with data centers and technological capital equipment, analysts said.

"It [would solidify] the early kind of cautionary signs in different states around the advancement of AI infrastructure," Rob Rains at Washington Analysis told CNBC.

Goldman Sachs strategists said this week that a Democratic Congress "might limit data center tax incentives, but a national moratorium looks unlikely in any election outcome."

One example would be Texas — a key hub of the data center buildout. A surprise loss there by Republican Gov. Greg Abbott has the "potential to spook" the market in the short term, Wolfe's Marcus said.

A blue wave might pressure defense shares, while boosting healthcare stocks, analysts and investors said.

"A substantial Democratic sweep could limit real spending growth unless a broader agreement increases both defense and health-care funding," said Alex Sagal, global equity analyst at Wells Fargo Investment Institute. However, he noted that the earnings outlook for the defense industry looks "stable to modestly positive."

Wolfe Research's Marcus said that healthcare is going to be "the biggest focus," as Democrats will push for restoring expired Affordable Care Act enhanced premium subsidies or unwinding some of the Medicaid reforms in the One Big Beautiful Bill Act.

"Healthcare stocks have been doing really well lately," Jed Ellerbroek, portfolio manager at Argent Capital Management, said to CNBC. "I think part of that is the anti-AI trade, but I also think part of it is just optimism that Democrats are taking over."

Ellerbroek is keeping his eye on hospital-related stocks such as HCA Healthcare and Tenet Healthcare as well as health insurers like UnitedHealth and Centene.

Financials was the worst S&P 500 sector in early 2026 on the heels of President Donald Trump calling on Congress to pass a law that would cap credit card interest rates at 10%. While the sector has been able to notch gains since, with its year-to-date rise now at around 3%, things could get more unstable for the group under Democratic control.

"Democrats will come in wanting to show tangible progress on the affordability issue, and that means targeting sectors that are front and center in consumer budgets," said Evercore ISI's Matthew Aks. "We know Trump has populist instincts, populist leanings, and could he pick a couple of issues and decide to team up with the Democrats on those issues and target those industries in a populist way? Absolutely."

Given that Democratic control is often associated with increased regulation, while Republican control is thought to promote deregulation, Democrats could be interested in passing credit card rate caps if they come out victorious.

"I think if Democrats have control of one or both chambers, they would try to pass it and see if they could get Trump to either sign it or force him to veto it, and that would let Democrats kind of score a political point," Aks said.

Trump could restart the war against Iran after holding off on any major strikes leading up to the midterms, a move that could upend the overall market and cause energy stocks to outperform. Crude oil prices jumped more than 10% in October, though they gave a lot of that gain back on Monday.

Gasoline prices stood at $4.17 a gallon on average nationwide Sunday, a nearly 40% increase compared with the same period last year, according to AAA.

"If there are escalations, that could obviously put upward pressure on prices just ahead of Thanksgiving," said Patrick De Haan, head of petroleum analysis at Gas Buddy, "which is also when Americans have really staunch traditions in terms of driving to friends and family."

If Trump decides to take forceful action, it could snuff out hope for a typical post-midterm year-end rally.

Evercore ISI said the S&P 500 could retreat by double digits to end the year at 6,800 as oil spikes if the president "moves to kinetic escalation in Iran ahead of seating the new Congress in January" and Democrats take Congress.

— With reporting by Sarah Min, Tobias Burns and Spencer Kimball.

#2851. Bills release safety C.J. Gardner-Johnson

Another short-lived stint on a new team for C.J Gardner-Johnson, as the Buffalo Bills have released the mercurial safety following his one-game suspension for spitting toward Raiders tight end Brock Bowers.

Gardner-Johnson was signed by the team in March to a one-year, $3.5 million deal.

And now he's no longer a member of Bills.

Jordan Schultz was first on the report of Gardner-Johnson's release:

After his first three NFL seasons on the Saints, the team that drafted him, since the beginning of the 2022 campaign, Gardner-Johnson has been on the Eagles, Lions, Eagles, Texans, Bears, and Bills, and has yet to stay with any of those teams for consecutive seasons.

Beyond his on-field antics, Gardner-Johnson, who'll turn 29 in December, had seriously problems tackling while in Buffalo. In fact, entering Week 9, his 25.7% missed tackle rate was the highest in the NFL among qualifying safeties.

In six games for the Bills, the former University of Florida star had 26 tackles, two pass breakups, and one interception.

This news isn't overly surprising, and not just because of the suspension.

Buffalo has gotten quality production from Damar Hamlin next to Cole Bishop this season — 23 tackles, one pass breakup, and a 4.2% missed tackle rate. Beyond him, they just saw veteran Geno Stone make a big impact in his short audition against the Ravens in Week 8, as he led the team with six tackles on just 19 snaps, and there are two large, explosive youngsters waiting in the wings, Jalon Kilgore and Jordan Hancock, the latter of whom presumably will be activated from IR relatively soon.

Also, versatile defensive back Te'Cory Couch played a fine game against the Ravens, can man the slot, or be deployed at safety if need be.

Also, the trade deadline is a week from today on November 10, and there's been plenty of speculation about the Bills adding veteran safety to pair with Bishop.

Much like for his past teams, Buffalo's Gardner-Johnson experiment did not work out.

#2849. Steve Nash calls Kyrie Irving most skilled player ever. What does LeBron James think?

Steve Nash calls Kyrie Irving most skilled player ever. What does LeBron James think? appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

Dallas Mavericks guard Kyrie Irving has made his long anticipated return to the court after missing the entirety of the 2025-26 season due to an ACL injury. And so far, he's been phenomenal for the Mavericks, forming a solid one-two punch with budding star Cooper Flagg. Irving is without a doubt a future Hall of Famer, but for Steve Nash and LeBron James, the praise is greater than that.

During a recent episode of the 'Mind The Game' Podcast, LeBron James and Steve Nash discussed Kyrie Irving's talent, while admitting their belief that the Mavericks guard is the most-skilled player in NBA history.

"I feel like Kyrie's maybe the most, probably the most skilled player to ever play our game," Nash said. "Just the breadth of skills, handling, shooting from all angles, finishing … he can play a long time just on that. But I think what makes him special is he's one of the best movers … his quickness, his change of direction, deceleration, his ability to control his body air whether that's shooting or finishing. Special, special athlete."

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

James agreed with Nash's sentiment regarding his former teammate. James and Irving won a championship together with the Cleveland Cavaliers in 2016 after pulling off a 3-1 comeback against the Golden State Warriors.

This season, Irving has appeared in five games, at a little over 28 minutes per game. He's averaging 21.4 points, 3.4 rebounds, 4.8 assists and 1.2 steals with splits of 52.7 percent shooting from the field, 39 percent shooting from the 3-point line and 100 percent shooting from the free-throw line.

#2848. Jeffery Simmons’ biggest contribution to Bears might be reactivating Montez Sweat

The play seemed innocuous enough at the time—just another errant throw by Seattle Seahawks quarterback Sam Darnold against a rapacious Chicago Bears front to force a punt late in the game.

But I pointed something out to my brother on the text thread that I thought was the cause:

He'd seen, as he had for much of the night, newly acquired defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons and defensive end Montez Sweat lined up on the same side of the formation, and he was thinking more about them than he was about delivering an accurate football. They didn't get home, but they pushed the pocket enough around him and into his throwing window that he lost his composure and threw a bad ball. There was a lot of that for Darnold and the Seahawks offense, which was the first to go against this new-look Bears defensive unit with Simmons at the center.

To be fair to Darnold, that fear wasn't entirely unfounded.

A Bears defense that has struggled for much of the last two years to get pressure with just four rushers has apparently solved that issue in one masterstroke by trading for Simmons, one of the NFL's best interior pass-rushers. Defensive coordinator Dennis Allen, who had been making ends meet without an elite front four by spamming blitzes, let the big dogs eat on Monday Night Football, and they left no crumbs.

But as hellacious as Simmons (two pressures, a sack, and an 86.9 pass rush grade) was in the Bears' 34-17 beatdown of the Seahawks—arguably the best defensive debut we've seen since Khalil Mack's in 2018—the effect he had on Sweat, his old college teammate at Mississippi State, might've been the biggest takeaway from last night.

Sweat, who was just coming off of a two-game suspension for violating the NFL's PED policy, had his best game of the season with four pressures and a sack. (He would've had even more if it hadn't been for a few untimely penalties in the secondary.)

Maybe it was the layoff. Maybe it was the addition of his old friend Simmons, with whom he already seems to have insane chemistry. Maybe it was primetime football. Maybe it was all of the above. Whatever the reason, Sweat looked as good as he ever has in a Bears uniform.

The Bears traded for and signed Sweat to a four-year, $98 million contract extension back in 2023 for games like this. But too often, he hasn't had the running mates, especially on the interior, to maximize his skills, which are less those of a truly elite pass-rusher and more of a strong running mate to an alpha dog. Now, Chicago went and got him one, and the difference, even in a small sample size, is enormous.

This was one game, of course, and the Bears will have some tough tests up ahead—the Detroit Lions, who swept the Bears last year, the AFC South-leading Jacksonville Jaguars, and AFC-leading Buffalo Bills, to name a few. (Plus, they need to get revenge on the Packers for that drubbing a couple weeks back without Caleb Williams.)

But if the Bears are going to get this version of Sweat alongside Simmons, not to mention what other ways general manager Ryan Poles might improve the defense, Chicago might just be the scariest team in the NFL.

The best team in the NFL resides in Chicago.

#2847. Trump DOJ sends target letters to Democrats in Alaska Senate criminal probe: Reports

The Trump administration's months-long campaign to challenge how states conduct elections and pushback against those efforts came to a head on Election Day on Tuesday.

Those efforts have included lawsuits, an attempt to implement a new U.S. Postal Service rule for mail-in ballots this year, threatening letters to state election officials and a plan to increase the number of poll monitors.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm elections

On Tuesday afternoon, The New York Times reported that the Department of Justice had sent letters to about 20 Democrats, among them prominent election lawyer Marc Elias, informing them that they might be targets in a criminal investigation of how a candidate with the same name as incumbent Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan ended up on the ballot of the Alaska Senate race.

NBC News reported that the tally of letters includes both target letters "and preservation requests to third parties to retain records."

Elias has represented Mary Peltola, the Democratic nominee in the Alaska election. In addition to him, at least one official at the Democratic Senatorial Campaign Committee was sent a target letter, The Times reported.

"The department is investigating possible donor fraud and conspiracy charges," according to The Times, citing a person familiar with the investigation.

Receiving a target letter does not mean a person has been charged with a crime, and does not necessarily mean that they will be charged.

In a statement, Elias said, "No one should be surprised that President Trump's Department of Justice is targeting me and my law firm on Election Day."

President Donald Trump, during an interview earlier Tuesday on Fox News' "Fox and Friends," blasted Elias.

"You had the Democrat Party headed up by Elias, their lawyer, who was a bad guy, very bad guy — should go to jail, in my opinion, " Trump said. "But you had the Democrat Party look all over Alaska for somebody named Sullivan."

A federal judge on Sunday dismissed the DOJ lawsuit that sought to delay certification of Alaska's Senate election if incumbent Sen. Sullivan loses by a margin that could be attributable to voter confusion from having another Dan Sullivan on the ballot. The DOJ is appealing that ruling.

That case, and the reported letters to Elias and other Democrats, underscore the likelihood that the Trump administration's election-targeting efforts will continue beyond Election Day.

Democratic lawmakers, attorneys general and advocacy groups are poised for battle if the administration tries to interfere with voting sites or challenge ballot counts, particularly in the states that could determine whether Trump's fellow Republicans lose their narrow Senate majority.

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., on Sunday night convened the entire Senate Democratic Caucus for a briefing by him and Elias about what a Democratic aide said was the "current landscape of election threats from Trump and his allies." The aide spoke on condition of anonymity to discuss the private briefing.

"We've been, you know, blocking and tackling and protecting and defending our elections for months and through the last few weeks of the voting window, and it'll all culminate tomorrow," California Attorney General Rob Bonta told CNBC on Monday evening. "And each of Trump's attempts has failed."

"I'm not sure what he has up his sleeves," Bonta said of Trump.

Bonta said he and other California officials, along with the 23 other Democratic state attorneys general for months have been preparing for Election Day and potential actions by the Trump administration "to disenfranchise and suppress votes and voters."

"We game-planned, and ... war-gamed and tabletop- and scenario planned all the possibilities," Bonta said.

Bonta said that if extreme events happened, California officials would quickly seek relief from the court system.

He noted, "We have laws in place right now where it is a criminal offense" to remove ballots from the custody and control of an election official.

On Monday, multiple federal courts dealt with urgent motions in election-related lawsuits and appeals by and against the Trump administration.

The DOJ on Monday asked the Supreme Court to block California from counting about 20,000 mail-in ballots from voters whom the DOJ suggests might not be U.S. citizens. Hours later, the Supreme Court rejected that request without any noted dissents.

In U.S. District Court in Washington, a DOJ lawyer told Judge Reggie Walton on Monday that the department expected to deploy 900 people to monitor polling places at sites in all 50 states.

"In 2024, there were about 100 monitors," the lawyer, Kathryn Barragan, said.

The DOJ on Tuesday said it was deploying approximately 1,000 election monitors and observers across the country. The areas it is focusing on are primarily in urban areas that vote Democratic.

Barragan's statement came at a hearing where Walton was considering an emergency request by the NAACP to bar the DOJ from using election monitors who are federal law enforcement personnel.

John Cusick, assistant counsel at the Legal Defense Fund, a civil rights group, said the administration has used the DOJ, Department of Homeland Security and other federal agencies to target election procedures this election cycle for three main reasons.

"First and foremost, it's an attempt to intimidate voters and election officials," Cusick said in an interview.

"The second component is confusion" about election rules, he said. "And the third, the unfortunate part, is trying to lay the groundwork for challenging the post-election counting and certification of ballots."

He said there has been a "constant barrage" by the administration to target election workers and election officials with unlawful actions.

"That has created such a chilling effect," Cusick said. "There's no doubt that the threats are threats to our democracy."

The DOJ, which did not respond to a request for comment by CNBC for this article, has previously defended its election-related efforts.

"Voting by noncitizens and other ineligible voters is a federal crime, and the department will enforce the law whether a case involves one illegal ballot or hundreds," the DOJ's rapid response account on X said on Oct. 12.

Attorney General Todd Blanche, when asked on Oct. 22 if there was any scenario in which the DOJ would try to block states from certifying their election results, conceded that was possible.

"I mean, is there any scenario?" Blanche told reporters in Virginia. "Yes, of course. There's scenarios that we could all come up with, hypothetically, you know, some doomsday scenario, for example."

"It is not DOJ's intention to, and it's never our intention to improperly interfere with elections and the state confirmation process or certification process," the attorney general said.

"But, you know, to say that there's no circumstances that we would ever do that? Of course I'm not going to say that."

Cusick and other experts note the president and executive branch of the federal government have no official role in administering elections, which are handled by the states as designated under the Constitution.

In her ruling Sunday dismissing the DOJ's Alaska Senate lawsuit, District Court Judge Sharon Gleason said the Trump administration was making requests that involve "the time, place, and manner of Alaska's election, which are the responsibilities of the State, not the executive branch of the federal government."

On Monday, a bipartisan group of former election officials in a filing told the 9th Circuit U.S. Court of Appeals that "increased federal pressure and threats on election officials ... are part of a pattern by the administration of attempting to assert authority over elections and questioning the integrity of state and local officials."

The filing came in a case where the DOJ is appealing an order issued Friday by a federal judge in California barring the Trump administration "from carrying out a series of efforts to actually or attempting to intimidate, threaten, or coerce election workers who aid others in exercising their right to vote."

The judge cited threats by the DOJ to prosecute state and local election officials if they process and count ballots using procedures that are legally required.

Bonta, California's attorney general, said he expected Trump and the DOJ To continue targeting states over their election procedures through the next cycle in 2028, when voters will choose a new president.

"We can't be naive about it. We have to expect it to get worse, prepared for it to get worse. I don't want to be alarmist," Bonta said. "I am a realist though, and I see the way he thinks and acts and what he's done, and maybe we'll be prepared for the worst, and it'll never happen."

"But we can't not be prepared for it. And I expect him to escalate as he gets more desperate."

#2845. Apple warns of risks related to AI agents behaving in 'unintended or unpredictable' ways

Apple said in its annual financial filing on Tuesday that AI agents that can act in "unintended or unpredictable ways" could represent a threat to its business by opening the company up to lawsuits or hurting its reputation.

The new risk factors in Apple's filing show the disruptive potential of AI agents, which have rapidly become top of mind for Silicon Valley's most important companies.

"In particular, artificial intelligence agents and other technologies can take actions autonomously, including interacting with internal or external systems that in some cases they may not be authorized to access, executing or altering code, sending communications, and completing transactions," Apple wrote in the filing.

The company added that agents "may behave in unintended or unpredictable ways, including by taking actions that were not directed, instructed or authorized."

Earlier this year, OpenAI released Dots, and Meta introduced Muse. Both AI agents use advanced AI models to work autonomously for tasks. They can even email or remind users when new messages arrive.

Apple's disclosure comes after several episodes that involved rogue AI agents breaking out of their sandboxes to hack third-party websites, like the Hugging Face incident, which has drawn renewed attention from policymakers in Washington, D.C., and around the world.

Separately, the ability of agents to use a computer in human ways is forcing big companies like Amazon and JPMorgan Chase to come up with policies around agentic access to services for strategic or security reasons.

Last month, Apple announced changes to full disk access on Macs to reduce "risks related to this level of access" for AI agents installed on PCs.

"The Company's ability to oversee and control third-party artificial intelligence agents and other technologies may be limited," Apple warned in its filing.

Apple doesn't currently have an agentic AI. But when new CEO John Ternus was asked on Monday's earnings call about AI agents, he said that the company had "real room" to expand Siri's capabilities.

This fall, Apple released Siri AI, a new assistant that uses the iPhone's local data storage to access data like calendars and emails, but Apple's Siri can't work autonomously for long stretches like Dots or Muse.

Ternus said Apple can't access private data on its devices, calling it a "core differentiator."

"If you think about it, so much of your personal data is on your iPhone, whether it's your contacts, your communications, health data, financial data, all of that," Ternus said. "Siri can tap into that and help you access it in a way that's very secure and private and that nobody else has access to."

Commercial AI agents like those from OpenAI and Meta ask for high-level access to the user's accounts like email in order to be more useful.

Apple indicated that the current regulations around AI and "super intelligence" are changing, unpredictable and that it could cost a lot of money to adapt to new laws governing AI.

"In addition, the Company is subject to the risks posed by ethical concerns and negative consumer perceptions related to automation, artificial intelligence and super intelligence, including perceived threats to safety or other societal harms or complications," according to the filing.

#2844. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

Nearly eight months after joining the Giants on a one-year, $8 million contract, the 26-year-old cornerback has agreed to a three-year contract extension with the team worth $46 million, including $32 million in guaranteed money, his agency told ESPN's Adam Schefter.

Newsome has played in six of the seven Giants games this season, recording one interception, six pass deflections, and 25 total tackles; his PFF pass coverage grade of 66.1 ranks 37th among 102 qualified cornerbacks.

#2842. PSG fly out of blocks to hit sorry Villarreal for six in Champions League

Reigning champions Paris Saint-Germain scored four times in the first 17 minutes on their way to a 6-0 demolition of Villarreal in Spain in the Champions League on Tuesday, as the French side shrugged off injuries to key players to maintain their perfect record in the competition this season.

Joao Neves, Desire Doue, Senny Mayulu and Fabian Ruiz all struck at the Estadio de la Ceramica as PSG set a record for the fastest team to score four times in a match in Europe'e slite club competition.

Ruiz added another early in the second half and teenager David Boly completed a comprehensive demolition of the side that finished third in La Liga last season.

"I obviously wasn't expecting that but you get matches where you start in that way," PSG's Spanish coach Luis Enrique told broadcaster Canal Plus.

"We were impressive throughout and we are really pleased even if it is very difficult for Villarreal -- but it is hard when you play against a team like us that presses constantly."

PSG have probably lost star winger Khvicha Kvaratskhelia for the rest of the campaign after he suffered a torn ACL in his right knee in last month's win over Barcelona, while Ousmane Dembele, captain Marquinhos and Nuno Mendes are also missing just now.

Despite that, the Ligue 1 leaders made it 10 straight wins in all competitions, including four in the Champions League as they aim to lift the European Cup for a third season running.

They are the only team to have maximum points after four matches in this year's Champions League -- Liverpool, Real Madrid and Real Betis all have maximum points from three games before playing on Wednesday -- and Luis Enrique's prolific side have now scored 20 goals on that run.

Like all matches in the Champions League on Tuesday, a minute's silence was held prior to kick-off in tribute to Jesus de Miguel, the Tenerife player who died following a collision in a Spanish second-division game on Monday.

The poignant silence here was also held to remember the victims of last week's deadly train crash in Spain, in which 69 people were killed.

Neves opened the scoring from close range in the ninth minute, and PSG netted three more goals in under eight minutes following that.

Doue swept in as the hosts were punished for calamitous defending, and Villarreal collapsed under the intensity of PSG's pressing as Mayulu made it 3-0.

Spain World Cup star Ruiz rounded off the stunning opening salvo, and the same player finished in style for 5-0 early in the second half after latching onto a ball over the top.

The 17-year-old full-back Boly came on for Achraf Hakimi and almost immediately got his team's sixth goal -- his first senior strike coming on his Champions League debut.

Villarreal have now lost three of their four games in Europe this season, after collecting just a single point in eight matches in last season's league phase.

More than that, this was the worst home defeat ever for a Spanish outfit in the Champions League, but PSG march on before taking on Roma and then Aston Villa in their remaining European fixtures before the end of the calendar year.

They have won the Champions League in each of the last two seasons despite failing to finish in the top eight in the league phase.

That has forced them to go through an extra play-off round to reach the last 16, but the chances of them finishing below the top eight this time look paper-thin.

#2841. AMD stock jumps 8% on earnings as data center revenue nearly doubles

Advanced Micro Devices reported third-quarter earnings on Tuesday that beat Wall Street's expectations, with revenue jumping 45% and data center sales nearly doubling from a year earlier. The stock was up 8% in extended trading.

Here's how the chipmaker did versus LSEG consensus estimates for the quarter:

Revenue increased from $9.25 billion a year earlier, AMD said in a statement. Neoclouds like CoreWeave and Nebius rallied earlier Tuesday and remained higher after AMD reported results.

CEO Lisa Su said on the earnings call that she expects server revenue to double year over year in 2027 and data center AI growth to accelerate even faster than the company had projected 90 days ago. The company expects data center to drive the majority of its revenue and earnings growth.

"We have worked across our supply chain to secure the wafers, substrates, advanced packaging, memory, and other key components needed to support much higher growth next year," Su said on the earnings call.

AMD's report comes days after Intel disclosed similar data center growth, with revenue in that segment up 78% in the third quarter. But Intel's stock plummeted 29% on Friday over concerns about weak guidance and Intel's ability to meet demand.

The data center unit makes up the majority of AMD's business, accounting for 64% of overall revenue, up from 47% a year ago.

After years spent chasing Nvidia in the market for artificial intelligence graphics processing units, or GPUs, AMD's central processing units, or CPUs, are experiencing a major resurgence with the rise of agentic AI apps like Meta's Muse. AMD's server CPUs are key for agentic apps because they power virtual machines and can run for hours on their own. Demand from agentic AI boosted AMD shares to a record in October.

"Adoption is just starting, and we expect demand to grow sharply as agents are deployed more broadly and take on more complex work," Su said on the earnings call with analysts.

AMD's stock is up about 125% this year, but it saw three consecutive days of losses before rallying on Tuesday amid an industrywide sell-off tied to fears about overspending on AI.

AMD is also a major player in the weakening PC market, which Intel blamed for its poor outlook last week. AMD and Intel are the largest makers of traditional x86 processors that power many laptops and desktops, while other laptops rely on Arm-based processors from the likes of Qualcomm and Apple.

Global PC shipments declined 4% in the second quarter from a year earlier due to soaring costs for memory and other components, according to Counterpoint Research.

AMD reports PC chip sales in a segment combined with gaming chips. Revenue in that business fell 8% from a year earlier to $3.7 billion. Gaming revenue was down 40% from a year ago.

For the fourth quarter, AMD said it expects about $17 billion in revenue, beating the $16.2 billion average analyst estimate, according to LSEG. AMD sees its gross margin for the quarter coming in at 55%, slightly below the average estimate of 55.3%, according to StreetAccount.

AMD also sells data center GPUs and full rack-scale systems called Helios that compete directly with Nvidia's Grace Blackwell and Vera Rubin systems. Meta, Microsoft, Anthropic, OpenAI, and Oracle are all beginning to use AMD Helios in their data centers. Meta said last week that its Helios racks are up and running, and AMD agreed to invest $1 billion in Meta's equity offering.

AMD has also committed to investing up to $5 billion in Anthropic, which unveiled its highly anticipated prospectus last week as it inches closer to a potentially historic initial public offering.

During a visit to Taiwan in October, Su said she plans to "substantially increase our supply in 2027." Su and Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang have both made multiple trips to Taiwan and South Korea this year, holding meetings with key suppliers like Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company and memory leaders SK Hynix and Samsung.

In September, AMD said it would acquire World Labs for $8.2 billion in an all-stock deal. The San Francisco-based AI lab, founded by industry pioneer Fei-Fei Li, is developing a so-called world model that simulates 3D environments to aid in developing robots and other physically grounded AI applications.

#2838. Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac rally as Ackman presses for conservatorship exit

The midterm elections could be a turning point for the release of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac from government control, according to billionaire investor Bill Ackman.

Investors may agree with him, if the rally in both stocks is any sign.

The Federal National Mortgage Association, known as Fannie Mae, and Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, or Freddie Mac, have each jumped more than 60% from Oct. 14 to Monday's close. The stocks were both up roughly 3% on Tuesday.

Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, has long pushed for the two entities to be released from federal conservatorship.

"The reason why it's not being touched right now is, you know, midterms," he said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk Box."

Ackman, who owns both names through Pershing Square, believes Freddie and Fannie have the potential to increase "10-fold from current share prices," he said in his quarterly letter to shareholders, issued on Monday.

Fannie and Freddie buy mortgages from banks and other lenders, and then package them into mortgage-backed securities, known as MBS. Their operations are critical to the housing market, providing liquidity to banks and creating a secondary market for MBS.

The entities were placed into conservatorship in September 2008, during the financial crisis. The stocks were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010 and have traded over the counter since then.

Ackman wants the conservatorship to end, arguing the entities would be worth multiples of their current valuation if they were freed from government control.

President Donald Trump has supported ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie. But it remains politically and economically contentious.

The entities operate under strict capital threshold requirements and would need to be recapitalized, which could happen through issuing common equity. However, the Treasury Department would need to figure out an exit plan for its roughly $388 billion ownership stake in the entities through preferred securities and warrants.

Congress may also need to play a role, passing legislation to restructure the government's role in mortgage financing and home-loan guarantees.

Nonetheless, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent recently said it could happen before Trump's term ends, telling 2Way's "The Morning Meeting" podcast that the administration will tackle the ownership structure "next year, the year after." He said affordability will be guiding the decision.

One point of controversy is whether ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie would increase the spreads on MBS over Treasuries, effectively making mortgages costlier. Bessent has sought to reassure the market that wouldn't happen.

"Whatever we do is going to be guided by making sure that the spread on [agency] mortgages … over the Treasury does not increase," Bessent said.

Ackman argues that privatizing the GSEs would help insulate them from political meddling. "Fannie and Freddie are these massive, enormously profitable institutions where whichever political party controls them…if they're still in conservatorship, can influence the outcome of elections," he told CNBC.

In one scenario, he explained, Democrats win the White House, conservatorship is still in place, and Democrats push Fannie and Freddie to lower credit standards in order to expand home ownership and affordability.

"If they're going to move more aggressively into borrowers with lower credit quality, for example, to achieve a political objective, that's going to have an impact on trillions of dollars outstanding mortgage securities," Ackman said. "It's going to have an impact on overall credit spreads."

MBS investors want certainty about how the entities will be run, he noted.

"That only gets resolved in a world in which they exit conservatorship, they become publicly traded companies that are owned by institutional and retail investors," Ackman said. "We set the rules. In conservatorship, the president can do anything."

#2831. Binance breach puts custody at center of crypto infrastructure trade

The $6.3 billion Binance hack could accelerate a shift in how investors value cryptocurrency companies, putting a greater premium on custody providers and the infrastructure needed to securely hold and protect digital assets.

Much of crypto's evolution this year has involved companies moving past their reliance on trading volumes and transaction fees, to focus instead on building the infrastructure connecting digital assets with traditional finance. Last Thursday's breach adds a new dimension, bringing cybersecurity, custody, compliance and balance-sheet resilience into sharper focus.

Importantly, the Binance breach was not an indictment of crypto itself, or a case of leadership-driven fraud, as with FTX in late 2022. Instead, Binance's leadership moved quickly to contain the fallout, protect customers and commit the exchange's own resources to cover losses.

On Tuesday, Binance CEO Richard Teng told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that the company has replenished 98% of $6.34 billion in affected assets, and that it is still in the process of purchasing outstanding, more illiquid assets.

Management's response, alongside efforts elsewhere in the industry to boost confidence, helped prevent a security breach from escalating into a broader crisis of confidence. For example, Tether, Circle, Ripple and others moved quickly to freeze or restrict access to about $3 billion in stolen assets; Coinbase blocked transfers to compromised wallets and offered Binance direct assistance.

The hack could even help shape the next phase of the crypto infrastructure trade. The new winners may not just be the businesses with the most users, volume or assets under custody, but the ones that can also demonstrate they're able to protect those assets under extreme duress.

"Which company has the resources to invest in cybersecurity and protect customer funds? For a smaller company, it could be much harder because once these crypto companies become infrastructure plays, providing the well for tokenization … they need to have the compliance and control in place so that a similar hacking will not happen or can be minimized," Owen Lau, senior analyst at ClearStreet, told CNBC. "Larger companies have more resources that could give them some advantage."

While institutional investors have increasingly embraced digital assets, broader adoption depends on confidence that those assets can be securely held, accessed and transferred. The Binance breach highlights the importance of that infrastructure, and could increase demand for specialized custodians and platforms with established asset-protection capabilities.

Coinbase has operated a dedicated institutional custody business since 2018. Circle entered the custody business this year, after the U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency granted it approval to operate as a trust bank. In the past few years, Ripple and Kraken have also launched custody businesses.

Still, smaller players compete in the space as well. Small cap crypto exchange stock Gemini Space Station has offered custody services since 2019. BitGo Holdings, founded in 2013, is one of the earliest players in institutional crypto custody and infrastructure, with a reputation for security and serving major institutions.

The shift could also drive consolidation as companies look to buy capabilities, deepen their infrastructure offerings and achieve the scale needed to compete, Lau said.

The more consequential structural shift may be the separation of custody from execution, according to Morgan Stanley.

"Crypto exchanges have historically combined trading, custody, and settlement within vertically integrated platforms, concentrating operational risk. Repeated security breaches could encourage investors to move assets toward independent custody while continuing to trade on CEXs," the firm's analyst Felix Stratmann said in a note Monday," referring to centralized cryptocurrency exchanges.

"This would preserve the role of centralized exchanges as liquidity and execution venues, while potentially benefiting regulated custodians, including banks, as institutional investors place greater emphasis on asset segregation, operational resilience, and investor protection," Stratmann said.

#2829. PFT's Week 9 2026 NFL power rankings

1. Bills (last week No. 1; 6-1): The defense is getting better, but the offense took a little while to get going.

2. Jaguars (No. 2; 5-2): They caught the Colts at the perfect time, and they’re catching the Ravens at the perfect time.

3. 49ers (No. 4; 6-1): Was the bye enough time for Christian McCaffrey to get healthy?

4. Broncos (No. 6; 6-2): They out-chiefed the Chiefs.

5. Bears (No. 8; 6-2): The Bears are NOT who we thought they were — they’re serious contenders to win the whole thing.

6. Vikings (No. 3; 6-1): A quarterback change may be needed.

7. Chiefs (No. 5; 5-2): Can the Chiefs beat the best teams in the league?

8. Steelers (No. 10; 6-2): The schedule is lining up favorably; a 12-win season isn’t out of the question.

9. Seahawks (No. 7; 4-4): Between last year’s NFC finalists (Seahawks and Rams), one of them may not make it back to the playoffs. And maybe both.

10. Patriots (No. 11; 5-3): They avoided the South Florida trap, again.

11. Bengals (No. 12; 5-2): Joe Burrow is emerging as a serious MVP candidate.

12. Lions (No. 13; 5-2): The injuries are piling up on defense, again.

13. Browns (No. 9; 5-3): Jared Verse should’ve learned a lesson, but probably didn’t.

14. Packers (No. 14; 5-3): The running game remains a liability.

15. Rams (No. 16; 4-4): The wins are happening, and Myles Garrett is lurking.

16. Giants (No. 17; 4-2-1): Will they go all-in for a high-end running back?

17. Raiders (No. 19; 4-4): A Kirk Cousins-Kyle Shanahan reunion game is looming.

18. Buccaneers (No. 20; 3-5): In two full games since returning from a thumb injury, Baker Mayfield has thrown 14 passes.

19. Ravens (No. 15; 4-4): Should they be buyers or sellers at the trade deadline?

20. Cardinals (No. 25; 3-5): They should move to Dallas. Or to L.A.

21. Falcons (No. 18; 3-5): It’s a good thing they play in a dome.

22. Panthers (No. 22; 3-4): Add them to the list of teams that will be chasing Mike Tomlin.

23. Texans (No. 23; 2-5): The margin for error is getting smaller all the time.

24. Commanders (No. 30; 2-4-1): Backward Hat Energy.

25. Saints (No. 24; 2-5): They’re still very much alive to win the worst division in the NFL.

26. Cowboys (No. 21; 3-5): Offseason hype, in-season tripe.

27. Eagles (No. 26; 2-6): That Super Bowl win from two years ago feels like it was two decades ago.

28. Titans (No. 27; 1-7): Unlike the Colts, the white-flag trade of Jeffery Simmons didn’t cause them to quit.

30. Dolphins (No. 29; 1-6): Malik Willis has had two solid games in a row.

31. Jets (No. 31; 2-6): Everyone except Woody Johnson knows it’s time for a coaching change.

32. Chargers (No. 32; 0-7): Jim Harbaugh’s stubborn refusal to change coordinators could cost him his job.

#2828. It’s fun to be a Sixer right now

Typically, he enjoys drinking it when he's not playing, but apparently exceptions can be made.

On Monday night, James went into the crowd going after a loose ball in the third quarter of the Sixers' 126-107 win over the Chicago Bulls. There was a brief stir from the fans. We later found out it was because James had taken a sip from a spectator's cup.

"I crashed into him, spilled some of his wine," James said. "So I wanted to see if it was worth it on my arm, man. How good it was."

Spoiled by California wines, no doubt, but James is profiling well with his new team.

When James' agent Rich Paul did his media blitz during his client's free agency, he mentioned happiness as the biggest factor for the King's decision.

In the fourth quarter, as the Sixers began to pull away, James found himself guarded by the 6-foot-1, 190-pound Tre Jones. LeBron easily bullied Jones into the post. Jones bit on a fake and Leonard Miller came over to help. James pump-faked again, this time getting Miller in the air, absorbing the contact and burying a short fadeaway.

And on the next offensive possession, it was Tyrese Maxey's turn to get into the lane and convert a crazy finish while getting fouled.

Safe to say, this group is having a good time.

"Listen, it's a long marathon," James said, "and if you don't find ways to have fun throughout it — even though you're working, even though you're locked in — it could wear on you. So for me, especially at this point in my career, too, being able to just appreciate the moments, the small moments. These fans are great to us. We try to be great to them as well by playing the game the right way, playing the game hard, and then also just being showmen as well."

James finished the night with 15 points, nine assists and seven rebounds. Maxey with 26 points and 11 assists. Jaylen Brown had 25 points, 11 rebounds and five assists. Joel Embiid, who was on a minutes restriction while managing plantar fasciitis, recorded 15 points, eight rebounds and three assists. The Sixers as a team had 36 assists on 48 makes.

Tough night for the "there's only one ball!" crowd.

And let's not forget about VJ Edgecombe — you know, the guy whose growth was going to be stunted because the Sixers have too many good players. The second-year guard scored a season-high 21 points, his first 20-plus point game on the young season, and dished out five assists.

It was a rewarding scoring night for a guy who's done all the dirty work for the Sixers' starting group. Edgecombe said his lower usage rate isn't his concern, though.

"I don't get antsy or impatient or anything," he said. "We're 7-1 and that's the most important thing. … And like tonight, I was able to get going a little bit, but once you just find that joy out there, just playing basketball … I was just kind of hard on myself early [this season] because I was missing shots I [usually] make and that's not on nobody else but me. And like I say, it's fun when you're winning. I don't want to be a cancer because I ain't getting the shots that I want to get. I'm still learning. I'm in year two."

The chemistry between the starting group is growing. A 7-1 start is a big help, but there seems to be an eagerness for all of them to learn each other. It's something that's been going on since training camp. In fact, a lesson from camp made its way onto the game floor.

James hit Edgecombe with not one but two absurdly accurate bounce passes in transition, but the pair needed to build trust to execute them when it counted.

"There's a couple times in training camp that he kind of slowed up and didn't think I'd get the pass there to him," James said. "And I told him all he has to do is just run, and I'll try to put it on time, on target as much as I can."

After the game, Edgecombe described it as a teaching moment. He was rewarded for that diligence.

Against the Bulls, Edgecombe was ready, flushing down multiple transition opportunities.

"If you're open, he's going to pass it to you," Edgecombe said. "The main thing is just being ready. Tonight, I had to run to go get them."

And this team is still in the fermentation process.

"It's a lot of talent, man," Brown said. "And we like each other and we're just kind of like building that chemistry of how we can get this thing to a well-oiled machine. So I think we're taking steps forward every single day. I think we still got a long way to go to be a really, really solid, great team. But we've been able to win some games. We started out the season 7-1 and you haven't seen our best version yet. So that's a good sign."

Another short-lived stint on a new team for C.J Gardner-Johnson, as the Buffalo Bills have released the mercurial safety following his one-game suspension for spitting toward Raiders tight end Brock Bowers. […] Beyond his on-field antics, Gardner-Johnson, who'll turn 29 in December, had seriously problems tackling while in Buffalo. #2826. Andy Reid: Chris Jones is giving his all, doing everything he can do

The Chiefs have lost their last two games.

Coincidentally or not, defensive tackle Chris Jones has been blanked on the official stat sheet in each one.

Jones, who turned 32 in July, has just 13 total tackles with one tackle for loss, three QB hits, and a half-sack through seven games in 2026.

On the whole, the Chiefs have just eight sacks this season, averaging just over one per game. Only Philadelphia has a lower sack rate than Kansas City, which has been getting one on just 3.69 percent of opponent dropbacks. By contrast, Minnesota leads the league with a 12.32 percent sack rate. Chicago is No. 2 at 9.65 percent.

What’s up with the Chiefs’ pass rush? How can the team get things going, particularly with Jones?

“[W]hen you rush four, you want to get somebody home, obviously. That’s what [defensive line coach] Joe [Cullen] strives for, and the guys strive for, and we’ve been doing a good job with getting pressure on,” head coach Andy Reid said in his Monday news conference, via transcript from the team. “You’ve just got to get to the quarterback. We had a lot of blocked balls, which was great, you’ve got to be relatively close to get those. Especially if they’re intermediate throws, the trajectory of the ball is one that you’ve got to be relatively close [to] get a hand up to be able to block.

“But going back to Chris, I mean, Chris is giving his all. He’s doing everything he can do. He gets doubled from various angles. He’s going to draw a lot of attention, and so that’s [an] advantage to the other guys to have a chance to get themselves home. So, as much emphasis is put on Chris, let’s take advantage of that and get ourselves on the quarterback. So, not just to the quarterback, but on the quarterback.”

The Chiefs will be back at home to face the Jets in Week 9. New York ranks No. 26, allowing a sack on 9.62 percent of passing attempts. We’ll see if that can get Kansas City’s pass rush going on Sunday.

#2824. The Christmas Day schedule is shaping up to be massive

When the schedule is released every May, plenty of standalone games that look good at the time morph into something not quite as appetizing as the season unfolds. Some standalone games get better with time.

This year, there’s a trio of massive games lurking on Christmas Day.

At 1:00 p.m. ET, it’s the Packers at the Bears. At 4:30 p.m. ET, it’s the Bills at the Broncos. At 8:15 p.m., it’s the Rams at the Seahawks.

With the Bears emerging as a threat to win the NFC North and the No. 1 seed in the conference, the rematch with their biggest rivals (who beat them 31-7 in Week 5) becomes must-see TV. Bills at Broncos, a rematch of a postseason overtime classic that got Buffalo coach Sean McDermott fired, should have direct implications on two division races and the chase for the lone bye in the AFC.

The nightcap shapes up to be not a battle for the NFC West title and/or the top seed but a potential elimination game, given that both Seattle and L.A. are currently 4-4.

Regardless, all three games should be significant. Impactful. Consequential.

And they’ll more than make up for the Christmas Eve game, which has the 2-5 Texans at the 2-6 Eagles.
